  • Notes addressed to "/topic/…" or "/queue/…" go to the mailroom (the broker), which photocopies them to everyone who asked to receive that kind of note (subscribers).
  • A courier (clientOutboundChannel) carries notes back out to visitors.
  • The intercom (SimpMessagingTemplate) lets anyone in the building (a REST controller, a scheduled job) drop a note into the mailroom.

5.2 Dependencies

Maven:

xml
<dependency>
  <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
  <artifactId>spring-boot-starter-websocket</artifactId>
</dependency>

<!-- Only if you use an external broker relay (RabbitMQ/ActiveMQ).
     Spring's reference docs say: add io.projectreactor.netty:reactor-netty and io.netty:netty-all -->
<dependency>
  <groupId>io.projectreactor.netty</groupId>
  <artifactId>reactor-netty</artifactId>
</dependency>
<dependency>
  <groupId>io.netty</groupId>
  <artifactId>netty-all</artifactId>
</dependency>

<!-- Optional: security for messages -->
<dependency>
  <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
  <artifactId>spring-boot-starter-security</artifactId>
</dependency>
<dependency>
  <groupId>org.springframework.security</groupId>
  <artifactId>spring-security-messaging</artifactId>
</dependency>

<!-- Optional: @Valid on payloads -->
<dependency>
  <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
  <artifactId>spring-boot-starter-validation</artifactId>
</dependency>

Gradle:

groovy
implementation 'org.springframework.boot:spring-boot-starter-websocket'
implementation 'io.projectreactor.netty:reactor-netty'                        // relay only
implementation 'io.netty:netty-all'                                          // relay only
implementation 'org.springframework.security:spring-security-messaging'       // security

spring-boot-starter-websocket brings spring-websocket + spring-messaging + an embedded servlet container with WebSocket support (Tomcat by default).

Note: Spring's STOMP support is part of the Servlet stack (Spring MVC). Spring WebFlux has a reactive WebSocketHandler API but no built-in STOMP broker/annotation support. On WebFlux you handle raw WebSocket messages yourself, or consider RSocket.

5.3 The minimal working configuration

java
package com.example.realtime.config;

import org.springframework.context.annotation.Configuration;
import org.springframework.messaging.simp.config.MessageBrokerRegistry;
import org.springframework.web.socket.config.annotation.EnableWebSocketMessageBroker;
import org.springframework.web.socket.config.annotation.StompEndpointRegistry;
import org.springframework.web.socket.config.annotation.WebSocketMessageBrokerConfigurer;

@Configuration
@EnableWebSocketMessageBroker            // turns on STOMP over WebSocket + message broker infrastructure
public class WebSocketConfig implements WebSocketMessageBrokerConfigurer {

    @Override
    public void registerStompEndpoints(StompEndpointRegistry registry) {
        registry.addEndpoint("/ws")                         // HTTP URL for the WebSocket handshake
                .setAllowedOriginPatterns("https://*.example.com", "http://localhost:[*]");
        // .withSockJS();  // optional: add SockJS fallback (then clients use http(s)://host/ws)
    }

    @Override
    public void configureMessageBroker(MessageBrokerRegistry registry) {
        registry.setApplicationDestinationPrefixes("/app"); // client SEND to /app/** → @MessageMapping
        registry.enableSimpleBroker("/topic", "/queue");    // in-memory broker handles /topic/** and /queue/**
        registry.setUserDestinationPrefix("/user");         // default is "/user"
    }
}

What each piece means:

Code Meaning
@EnableWebSocketMessageBroker Registers all beans: channels, handlers, broker, SimpMessagingTemplate, etc. (via DelegatingWebSocketMessageBrokerConfiguration)
addEndpoint("/ws") Clients connect to ws(s)://host:port/<context-path>/ws
setAllowedOrigins / setAllowedOriginPatterns Which Origin headers are accepted. Default is same-origin only (since Spring 4.1.5). Wildcard * with setAllowedOrigins is not allowed together with credentials in newer versions; use patterns
withSockJS() Enables SockJS fallback transports (Chapter 6)
setApplicationDestinationPrefixes("/app") Messages whose destination starts with /app go to annotated controller methods
enableSimpleBroker("/topic","/queue") Messages (and subscriptions) to these prefixes go to the in-memory broker
setUserDestinationPrefix("/user") Destinations starting with /user are translated to per-session destinations (§5.10)

5.4 The architecture: channels and handlers

This diagram is the most important thing in the chapter. Learn to draw it from memory.

5.4.1 With the simple (in-memory) broker

                                 ┌───────────────────────────────┐
  WebSocket   ┌──────────────┐   │  SimpAnnotationMethod         │
  client ────►│ request      │──►│  MessageHandler               │──┐  return value /
  (STOMP)     │ channel      │   │  (@MessageMapping methods)    │  │  SimpMessagingTemplate
              │ "clientIn-   │   └───────────────────────────────┘  │
              │  bound-      │   /app/**                            ▼
              │  Channel"    │                              ┌──────────────┐
              │              │──────────────────────────────►│ brokerChannel│
              │              │   /topic/**, /queue/**        └──────┬───────┘
              │              │   (SUBSCRIBE, SEND direct)           │
              └──────────────┘                                      ▼
                     │                              ┌───────────────────────────┐
                     │  /user/**                    │ SimpleBrokerMessageHandler│
                     └──►UserDestinationMessage     │ (in-memory subscriptions) │
                         Handler ─► brokerChannel   └────────────┬──────────────┘
                                                                 │
              ┌──────────────┐                                   │
  client ◄────│ response     │◄──────────────────────────────────┘
              │ channel      │
              │ "clientOut-  │
              │  boundChannel│
              └──────────────┘

5.4.2 With an external broker (STOMP broker relay)

 client ──► clientInboundChannel ──► SimpAnnotationMethodMessageHandler ──► brokerChannel
                     │                                                         │
                     │ /topic/**, /queue/**                                    ▼
                     └────────────────────────────────────────► StompBrokerRelayMessageHandler
                                                                   │   ▲   (one TCP connection
                                                                   ▼   │    per client session,
                                                         RabbitMQ / ActiveMQ    + 1 "system" connection)
 client ◄── clientOutboundChannel ◄───────────────────────────────────┘

5.4.3 The three channels

Channel bean Carries Default executor
clientInboundChannel Messages from WebSocket clients Thread pool; core size = 2 × available processors
clientOutboundChannel Messages to WebSocket clients Thread pool; core size = 2 × available processors
brokerChannel Messages from server-side application code to the broker Configurable via configureBrokerChannel

5.4.4 The main beans (names you'll see in logs and stack traces)

Bean / class Job
SubProtocolWebSocketHandler The WebSocketHandler registered at /ws. Delegates to sub-protocol handlers. Manages sessions, decorates them with ConcurrentWebSocketSessionDecorator (send limits)
StompSubProtocolHandler Decodes WebSocket messages into STOMP Messages (with StompDecoder/BufferingStompDecoder), sends them to clientInboundChannel; encodes outbound messages (StompEncoder)
SimpAnnotationMethodMessageHandler Finds and invokes @MessageMapping/@SubscribeMapping methods for /app/**
SimpleBrokerMessageHandler In-memory broker: keeps a subscription registry (DefaultSubscriptionRegistry), fans out messages
StompBrokerRelayMessageHandler Forwards messages to an external STOMP broker over TCP (Reactor Netty)
UserDestinationMessageHandler Translates /user/... destinations to unique session destinations
SimpMessagingTemplate (brokerMessagingTemplate) Send to the broker from anywhere
DefaultSimpUserRegistry Tracks connected users and their sessions/subscriptions
WebSocketMessageBrokerStats Periodically logs stats (every 30 min by default)

5.4.5 Spring's message abstraction (spring-messaging)

java
public interface Message<T> {
    T getPayload();            // byte[] from the wire, or your object after conversion
    MessageHeaders getHeaders(); // immutable map: simpMessageType, simpSessionId, simpDestination,
                                 // nativeHeaders (the raw STOMP headers), simpUser, ...
}
  • MessageChannel: send(Message). SubscribableChannel: has subscribers (MessageHandlers). ExecutorSubscribableChannel invokes them on a thread pool.
  • MessageHeaderAccessor family helps read/write headers: SimpMessageHeaderAccessor (generic), StompHeaderAccessor (STOMP specifics like command, native headers, login).
  • Native headers = the real STOMP headers (Authorization, destination, custom ones), stored under nativeHeaders. Access with accessor.getFirstNativeHeader("Authorization").
  • SimpMessageType: CONNECT, CONNECT_ACK, MESSAGE, SUBSCRIBE, UNSUBSCRIBE, HEARTBEAT, DISCONNECT, DISCONNECT_ACK, OTHER.

5.4.6 Follow one message end-to-end

  1. Browser sends SEND destination:/app/chat.send with JSON body.
  2. Tomcat receives the WebSocket text message → SubProtocolWebSocketHandler.handleMessage.
  3. StompSubProtocolHandler decodes STOMP → Message<byte[]> with headers (simpSessionId, simpUser, simpDestination=/app/chat.send).
  4. Sent to clientInboundChannel (interceptors run: your auth interceptor, Spring Security's authorization).
  5. A thread from the inbound pool delivers it to SimpAnnotationMethodMessageHandler (destination starts with /app), which strips /app and matches @MessageMapping("chat.send") (or /chat.send).
  6. Payload converted from JSON → ChatMessage via MessageConverter (Jackson).
  7. Your method returns ChatMessage; SendToMethodReturnValueHandler sends it to brokerChannel with destination from @SendTo, or the default /topic/chat.send.
  8. SimpleBrokerMessageHandler finds sessions subscribed to that destination and, for each, sends a MESSAGE to clientOutboundChannel.
  9. StompSubProtocolHandler encodes to a STOMP MESSAGE frame → WebSocket text message → browser.

5.5 Annotated controllers

java
@Controller
public class ChatController {

    @MessageMapping("/chat.send")            // client sends to /app/chat.send
    @SendTo("/topic/chat.public")            // broadcast result to all subscribers
    public ChatMessage send(@Payload @Valid ChatMessage msg, Principal principal) {
        return msg.withSender(principal.getName()).withTimestamp(Instant.now());
    }
}

5.5.1 @MessageMapping

  • Maps messages by destination (after the application prefix is removed).
  • Can be on the class (prefix) and on methods.
  • Supports Ant-style patterns (/chat.*, /rooms/**) and template variables (/rooms/{roomId}) read with @DestinationVariable.
  • Default path separator is /. To use dots (chat.room.1, common with RabbitMQ), configure registry.setPathMatcher(new AntPathMatcher(".")).
  • Message-handling methods are invoked on the inbound channel thread pool, concurrently. They must be thread-safe (no mutable shared state without synchronisation).

5.5.2 Supported method arguments

Argument What you get
Message<?> Full message
MessageHeaders All headers
MessageHeaderAccessor, SimpMessageHeaderAccessor, StompHeaderAccessor Typed header access
@Payload (optional annotation) Converted body; supports @Valid/@Validated
@Header("name") One header. Spring looks in the top-level message headers first and then in the native STOMP headers, so @Header("Authorization") finds a STOMP header sent by the client
@Headers Map<String,Object> All headers
@DestinationVariable Value from destination template {var}
java.security.Principal Authenticated user from the WebSocket session

Session id: @Header("simpSessionId") String sessionId or SimpMessageHeaderAccessor#getSessionId(). Session attributes: SimpMessageHeaderAccessor#getSessionAttributes() (a per-WebSocket-session Map, filled by handshake interceptors or your code).

5.5.3 Return values

Situation Where the return value goes
@MessageMapping, no @SendTo brokerChannel, destination = /topic + input destination (e.g., /app/greet → /topic/greet)
@SendTo("/topic/x") brokerChannel to /topic/x (supports {var} placeholders from the destination)
@SendToUser("/queue/x") The current user's /user/queue/x (all their sessions, unless broadcast = false → only the session that sent the message)
void / null Nothing sent
CompletableFuture/ListenableFuture/reactive types Sent when completed (async)

@SendTo and @SendToUser can also be placed on the class (they then apply to all methods in it), and a method-level annotation overrides a class-level one.

5.5.4 @SubscribeMapping: request/reply on subscribe

java
@SubscribeMapping("/rooms")         // client SUBSCRIBEs to /app/rooms
public List<Room> listRooms() {      // return value goes DIRECTLY back to that client (clientOutboundChannel)
    return roomService.findAll();    // not through the broker, not to other subscribers
}
  • Handles only SUBSCRIBE frames whose destination matches.
  • The return value is sent once, only to the subscribing client, unless @SendTo/@SendToUser is present.
  • The broker does not store this subscription (it's under /app, not a broker prefix).
  • Classic use: load initial data when the UI subscribes. Unsubscribing is unnecessary but harmless.

5.5.5 Exception handling

java
@Controller
public class ChatController {

    @MessageMapping("/chat.send")
    public void send(@Payload @Valid ChatMessage msg) { ... }

    @MessageExceptionHandler
    @SendToUser(destinations = "/queue/errors", broadcast = false)
    public ErrorDto handle(MethodArgumentNotValidException ex) {
        return new ErrorDto("VALIDATION_FAILED", ex.getMessage());
    }
}

// Global (since Spring 5.3): @ControllerAdvice also applies to @MessageExceptionHandler
@ControllerAdvice
public class GlobalWsExceptionHandler {
    @MessageExceptionHandler(AccessDeniedException.class)
    @SendToUser(destinations = "/queue/errors", broadcast = false)
    public ErrorDto accessDenied(AccessDeniedException ex) {
        return new ErrorDto("FORBIDDEN", "You can't do that");
    }
}
  • MethodArgumentNotValidException here is org.springframework.messaging.handler.annotation.support.MethodArgumentNotValidException, not the MVC one (a common import mistake).
  • Exceptions not handled by a @MessageExceptionHandler are logged and the client receives nothing. Silent failures are a classic bug.
  • Exceptions thrown in interceptors on clientInboundChannel (e.g., auth failure on CONNECT) become a STOMP ERROR frame and the connection closes. Customise with StompSubProtocolErrorHandler (§5.13).

5.5.6 Validation

With spring-boot-starter-validation on the classpath, @Payload @Valid ChatMessage msg validates using Bean Validation (@NotBlank, @Size(max = 2000)...). You can also provide a validator via WebSocketMessageBrokerConfigurer#getValidator().

5.6 Sending from anywhere: SimpMessagingTemplate

java
@Service
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class OrderNotifier {

    private final SimpMessagingTemplate messaging;   // or SimpMessageSendingOperations

    public void orderShipped(Order order) {
        // broadcast to everyone watching this order
        messaging.convertAndSend("/topic/orders." + order.getId(), OrderEvent.shipped(order));

        // private to the owner (all their sessions)
        messaging.convertAndSendToUser(order.getOwnerUsername(), "/queue/notifications",
                new Notification("Your order shipped!"));

        // with custom STOMP headers (they become MESSAGE headers)
        messaging.convertAndSend("/topic/orders." + order.getId(), OrderEvent.shipped(order),
                Map.of("event-type", "ORDER_SHIPPED", "correlation-id", order.getTraceId()));
    }
}
  • Works from REST controllers, @Scheduled jobs, Kafka listeners, @TransactionalEventListeners, etc.
  • convertAndSend sends to brokerChannel, so the destination must be a broker destination (/topic, /queue) or a user destination. Sending to /app/... from the template does not invoke your @MessageMapping methods.
  • Converts the payload using the configured MessageConverters (Jackson JSON by default with Boot).
  • setUserDestinationPrefix must match the registry's if you configure it manually (Boot/@EnableWebSocketMessageBroker wires it for you).

Pattern: send after commit. If you notify inside a DB transaction and the transaction rolls back, clients received a lie. Publish domain events and send in @TransactionalEventListener(phase = AFTER_COMMIT).

5.7 Destinations and patterns

java
@Controller
@MessageMapping("rooms")
public class RoomController {

    @MessageMapping("{roomId}/send")                        // /app/rooms/42/send
    @SendTo("/topic/rooms.{roomId}")                         // placeholders resolved from destination vars
    public ChatMessage send(@DestinationVariable String roomId, ChatMessage msg) {
        return msg.inRoom(roomId);
    }
}
  • Simple broker subscription patterns: the simple broker's DefaultSubscriptionRegistry matches subscriptions using the PathMatcher. Clients can subscribe to patterns like /topic/prices.* (with a dot-separator matcher), and senders publish to concrete destinations. Be careful: pattern subscriptions are matched for each message and can be abused (subscribe to /topic/** to see everything). Restrict with security rules.
  • Simple broker selector header: the simple broker supports a selector header on SUBSCRIBE carrying a Spring Expression Language (SpEL) expression evaluated against message headers (e.g., headers.foo == 'bar'). The header name is configurable via setSelectorHeaderName(...) on the simple broker registration (pass null to turn the feature off). This feature was the source of CVE-2018-1270 (remote code execution through a crafted selector, fixed in Spring 5.0.5 / 4.3.16 by evaluating with a restricted SimpleEvaluationContext). Turn it off if you don't use it, and keep Spring patched.
  • RabbitMQ has its own destination grammar (Chapter 7). With RabbitMQ use . as separator in /topic routing keys.

5.8 Configuring the broker in detail

java
@Configuration
@EnableWebSocketMessageBroker
public class WebSocketConfig implements WebSocketMessageBrokerConfigurer {

    @Bean
    public ThreadPoolTaskScheduler wsHeartbeatScheduler() {
        ThreadPoolTaskScheduler s = new ThreadPoolTaskScheduler();
        s.setPoolSize(1);
        s.setThreadNamePrefix("ws-heartbeat-");
        s.initialize();
        return s;
    }

    @Override
    public void configureMessageBroker(MessageBrokerRegistry registry) {
        registry.setApplicationDestinationPrefixes("/app");
        registry.setUserDestinationPrefix("/user");
        registry.setPathMatcher(new AntPathMatcher("."));       // optional: dot-separated destinations
        registry.setPreservePublishOrder(true);                 // ordered delivery per session (5.1+)
        registry.setCacheLimit(1024);                           // destination cache for the simple broker

        registry.enableSimpleBroker("/topic", "/queue")
                .setTaskScheduler(wsHeartbeatScheduler())       // REQUIRED for heart-beats to work
                .setHeartbeatValue(new long[] {10_000, 10_000}); // server can send every 10s, wants client every 10s
    }
}

Heart-beat defaults for the simple broker: 0,0 (disabled) unless a TaskScheduler is configured; once a scheduler is set, the default becomes 10000,10000.

Pitfall: circular bean dependency. Injecting a TaskScheduler bean that depends on the WebSocket configuration into the configurer can create a cycle. Defining a dedicated scheduler as shown, or using @Lazy, avoids it. (Spring's docs show @Lazy TaskScheduler messageBrokerTaskScheduler injection.)

5.8.1 External broker relay (production for clusters)

java
@Override
public void configureMessageBroker(MessageBrokerRegistry registry) {
    registry.setApplicationDestinationPrefixes("/app");
    registry.setPathMatcher(new AntPathMatcher("."));
    registry.enableStompBrokerRelay("/topic", "/queue", "/exchange", "/amq/queue")
            .setRelayHost("rabbitmq.internal")
            .setRelayPort(61613)
            .setVirtualHost("/")                          // STOMP "host" header sent in CONNECT
            .setClientLogin("ws-client").setClientPasscode("***")   // used for each client session's connection
            .setSystemLogin("ws-system").setSystemPasscode("***")   // used for the shared "system" connection
            .setSystemHeartbeatSendInterval(10_000)
            .setSystemHeartbeatReceiveInterval(10_000)
            .setUserDestinationBroadcast("/topic/unresolved-user-destination")  // multi-instance user dests
            .setUserRegistryBroadcast("/topic/simp-user-registry");             // multi-instance user registry
    // .setTcpClient(customReactorNettyTcpClient) for multiple broker addresses / TLS
}

Full details in Chapter 7.

5.9 Transport and container configuration

java
@Override
public void configureWebSocketTransport(WebSocketTransportRegistration registration) {
    registration
        .setMessageSizeLimit(128 * 1024)       // max inbound STOMP message size (default 64 KB)
        .setSendBufferSizeLimit(512 * 1024)    // max bytes buffered per session when client is slow (default 512 KB)
        .setSendTimeLimit(15 * 1000)           // max time for a single send to a session (default 10 s)
        .setTimeToFirstMessage(30 * 1000);     // close if no STOMP frame (CONNECT) arrives in time (default 60 s)
    // .addDecoratorFactory(handler -> new MyLoggingWebSocketHandlerDecorator(handler));
}
Setting Default Meaning / when to change
messageSizeLimit 64 KB Larger inbound frames → session closed. STOMP frames split across multiple WebSocket messages are re-assembled up to this size. Raise if clients legitimately send bigger messages
sendBufferSizeLimit 512 KB If a client is slow, outbound messages queue per session. Exceeding the limit closes the session (or drops, depending on overflow strategy)
sendTimeLimit 10 s If one send blocks longer than this, the session is considered unreliable and closed
timeToFirstMessage 60 s Protects against clients that connect and never send CONNECT (resource exhaustion)

Container-level buffer sizes (raw WebSocket messages):

java
@Bean
public ServletServerContainerFactoryBean createWebSocketContainer() {
    ServletServerContainerFactoryBean container = new ServletServerContainerFactoryBean();
    container.setMaxTextMessageBufferSize(128 * 1024);
    container.setMaxBinaryMessageBufferSize(128 * 1024);
    container.setMaxSessionIdleTimeout(0L);   // ms; 0 = no container idle timeout (rely on heart-beats)
    return container;
}

Pitfall: two limits. A message must pass both the container's per-message buffer (Tomcat's default text/binary buffer is 8 KB; a single WebSocket message bigger than that is rejected with close code 1009) and Spring's STOMP messageSizeLimit (64 KB, which applies after Spring re-assembles a STOMP frame that a client split across several WebSocket messages). "Works locally with small payloads, fails in prod with big JSON" is often this. Symptoms: close code 1009, or the session is closed with a "message too big"/StompConversionException log line. Fix: raise both limits deliberately, or (better) keep messages small and fetch large data over HTTP.

5.10 User destinations: private messages explained

ELI5: Everyone subscribes to the same-looking mailbox name /user/queue/notifications, but Spring secretly gives each person (each session) their own box behind the scenes, so nobody can read anyone else's mail.

5.10.1 How it works

Client side:

js
client.subscribe('/user/queue/notifications', cb);

Server side:

java
messaging.convertAndSendToUser("anna", "/queue/notifications", payload);

What happens:

  1. On SUBSCRIBE to /user/queue/notifications, UserDestinationMessageHandler (via DefaultUserDestinationResolver) rewrites it to a session-unique destination: /queue/notifications-user{sessionId} (e.g., /queue/notifications-userwmv3cnnv). The broker stores that subscription.
  2. convertAndSendToUser("anna", "/queue/notifications", x) sends to /user/anna/queue/notifications.
  3. The resolver looks up all sessions of user "anna" in the SimpUserRegistry and sends to each /queue/notifications-user{sessionId}.
  4. Each of anna's tabs/devices receives it; nobody else does.

Requirements & gotchas:

  • The user is identified by Principal#getName(). If the session has no Principal, sending by username can't work (there's no name→session mapping). Set the user at handshake or CONNECT (Chapter 8).
  • The name you pass to convertAndSendToUser must equal principal.getName() exactly. With JWT this is usually the sub claim. With OAuth2 resource server it's the sub by default unless you change principalClaimName. A mismatch (email vs id) = messages vanish silently.
  • With @SendToUser, the current user is taken from the incoming message's Principal.
  • Multiple server instances: sessions on other instances aren't in the local registry. Enable userDestinationBroadcast and userRegistryBroadcast on the broker relay (Chapter 7).
  • With an external broker, each session-unique destination becomes a broker queue (e.g., RabbitMQ). Ensure they're auto-deleted when sessions end.

5.10.2 Sending to a specific session (no Principal needed)

java
private MessageHeaders headersForSession(String sessionId) {
    SimpMessageHeaderAccessor accessor = SimpMessageHeaderAccessor.create(SimpMessageType.MESSAGE);
    accessor.setSessionId(sessionId);
    accessor.setLeaveMutable(true);
    return accessor.getMessageHeaders();
}

// The "user" argument is the session id itself:
messaging.convertAndSendToUser(sessionId, "/queue/reply", payload, headersForSession(sessionId));

Useful for anonymous users (e.g., a public support widget). The client still subscribes to /user/queue/reply.

5.10.3 @SendToUser broadcast vs single session

java
@MessageMapping("/report.generate")
@SendToUser(destinations = "/queue/report", broadcast = false)  // only the tab that asked
public Report generate(ReportRequest req) { ... }

broadcast = true (default) → all sessions of that user (all tabs/devices).

5.11 Session lifecycle events

Spring publishes ApplicationEvents you can listen to with @EventListener:

Event When
SessionConnectEvent A new STOMP CONNECT arrived (before the broker responds). Contains the CONNECT message incl. native headers, session id, user
SessionConnectedEvent Broker replied CONNECTED (shortly after connect). Message is the CONNECTED (CONNECT_ACK), the original CONNECT is in header simpConnectMessage
SessionSubscribeEvent A SUBSCRIBE arrived
SessionUnsubscribeEvent An UNSUBSCRIBE arrived
SessionDisconnectEvent The STOMP session ended: DISCONNECT frame or WebSocket closed. May be published more than once per session → handlers must be idempotent. Has getCloseStatus()
BrokerAvailabilityEvent Broker became available/unavailable (important for relay: TCP to RabbitMQ lost)
java
@Component
@RequiredArgsConstructor
@Slf4j
public class PresenceTracker {

    private final SimpMessagingTemplate messaging;
    private final Map<String, String> sessionToUser = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();

    @EventListener
    public void onConnected(SessionConnectedEvent event) {
        StompHeaderAccessor acc = StompHeaderAccessor.wrap(event.getMessage());
        Principal user = event.getUser();
        if (user != null) {
            sessionToUser.put(acc.getSessionId(), user.getName());
            messaging.convertAndSend("/topic/presence", new Presence(user.getName(), true));
        }
    }

    @EventListener
    public void onDisconnect(SessionDisconnectEvent event) {
        String username = sessionToUser.remove(event.getSessionId());   // idempotent: second call gets null
        if (username != null && !sessionToUser.containsValue(username)) { // last session of this user
            messaging.convertAndSend("/topic/presence", new Presence(username, false));
        }
        log.info("Disconnected session={} status={}", event.getSessionId(), event.getCloseStatus());
    }
}

Pitfall: Events are published synchronously on the thread that handles the message/close. Don't do slow work (DB calls, HTTP calls) directly in listeners. Hand off with @Async or a queue. In a cluster, local in-memory presence maps are wrong; use Redis or the multi-server user registry.

Connected users: inject SimpUserRegistry:

java
int online = simpUserRegistry.getUserCount();
SimpUser anna = simpUserRegistry.getUser("anna");           // sessions & subscriptions
Set<SimpSubscription> subs = simpUserRegistry.findSubscriptions(s -> s.getDestination().startsWith("/topic/rooms."));

5.12 Interceptors

5.12.1 HandshakeInterceptor (HTTP level, before 101)

java
public class TenantHandshakeInterceptor implements HandshakeInterceptor {
    @Override
    public boolean beforeHandshake(ServerHttpRequest request, ServerHttpResponse response,
                                   WebSocketHandler wsHandler, Map<String, Object> attributes) {
        String tenant = request.getHeaders().getFirst("X-Tenant-Id");      // from proxy, or query param
        if (tenant == null) { response.setStatusCode(HttpStatus.BAD_REQUEST); return false; } // reject
        attributes.put("tenantId", tenant);   // becomes WebSocket session attribute (SimpMessageHeaderAccessor#getSessionAttributes)
        return true;
    }
    @Override
    public void afterHandshake(ServerHttpRequest req, ServerHttpResponse res, WebSocketHandler h, Exception ex) {}
}

registry.addEndpoint("/ws")
        .addInterceptors(new TenantHandshakeInterceptor(), new HttpSessionHandshakeInterceptor())
        .setHandshakeHandler(new CustomPrincipalHandshakeHandler());
  • HttpSessionHandshakeInterceptor copies HttpSession attributes into WebSocket session attributes.
  • DefaultHandshakeHandler#determineUser can be overridden to assign a Principal at handshake time (e.g., generate an anonymous UUID principal so user destinations work for guests):
java
public class CustomPrincipalHandshakeHandler extends DefaultHandshakeHandler {
    @Override
    protected Principal determineUser(ServerHttpRequest request, WebSocketHandler wsHandler,
                                      Map<String, Object> attributes) {
        Principal user = request.getPrincipal();   // from Spring Security if the handshake was authenticated
        return user != null ? user : new AnonymousPrincipal(UUID.randomUUID().toString());
    }
}

5.12.2 ChannelInterceptor (STOMP level, every message)

java
public class LoggingAndAuthInterceptor implements ChannelInterceptor {

    @Override
    public Message<?> preSend(Message<?> message, MessageChannel channel) {
        StompHeaderAccessor acc = MessageHeaderAccessor.getAccessor(message, StompHeaderAccessor.class);
        if (acc == null) return message;
        if (StompCommand.CONNECT.equals(acc.getCommand())) {
            // authenticate using native header; acc.setUser(...) (see Chapter 8)
        }
        return message;          // return null to silently drop; throw to reject (→ ERROR frame for inbound)
    }

    @Override
    public void afterSendCompletion(Message<?> message, MessageChannel channel, boolean sent, Exception ex) {
        // metrics, cleanup (e.g., clear ThreadLocal / MDC)
    }
}

@Override
public void configureClientInboundChannel(ChannelRegistration registration) {
    registration.interceptors(new LoggingAndAuthInterceptor());
    registration.taskExecutor().corePoolSize(8).maxPoolSize(32).queueCapacity(10_000);
}

@Override
public void configureClientOutboundChannel(ChannelRegistration registration) {
    registration.taskExecutor().corePoolSize(8).maxPoolSize(32);
}

Key details:

  • Use MessageHeaderAccessor.getAccessor(message, StompHeaderAccessor.class) to get the mutable accessor of the message. StompHeaderAccessor.wrap(message) creates a copy; changes to a copy (like setUser) are lost unless you build a new message. "My setUser doesn't stick" is this bug.
  • preSend runs on the caller's thread (for inbound: the WebSocket container's I/O thread), before the message is handed to the executor. ExecutorChannelInterceptor#beforeHandle/afterMessageHandled run on the executor thread (where your controller runs) and are the right place for ThreadLocals like SecurityContextHolder, MDC logging context, tenant context.
  • Interceptor order matters (your auth interceptor must run before Spring Security's authorization). See Chapter 8.

5.13 Customising STOMP ERROR frames

java
public class CustomStompErrorHandler extends StompSubProtocolErrorHandler {
    @Override
    public Message<byte[]> handleClientMessageProcessingError(Message<byte[]> clientMessage, Throwable ex) {
        Throwable cause = ex.getCause() != null ? ex.getCause() : ex;
        if (cause instanceof AccessDeniedException || cause instanceof BadCredentialsException) {
            StompHeaderAccessor acc = StompHeaderAccessor.create(StompCommand.ERROR);
            acc.setMessage("UNAUTHORIZED");               // ERROR frame "message" header
            acc.setLeaveMutable(true);
            return MessageBuilder.createMessage("Invalid or expired token".getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_8),
                                                acc.getMessageHeaders());
        }
        return super.handleClientMessageProcessingError(clientMessage, ex);
    }
}

@Override
public void registerStompEndpoints(StompEndpointRegistry registry) {
    registry.addEndpoint("/ws").setAllowedOriginPatterns("https://*.example.com");
    registry.setErrorHandler(new CustomStompErrorHandler());
}

Never put stack traces or internal details into ERROR frames (information leak).

5.14 Message ordering

By default the inbound and outbound channels use thread pools, so messages from/to the same client can be processed out of order. Options:

  • registry.setPreservePublishOrder(true) (MessageBrokerRegistry, 5.1+): messages published to the same client session from the broker are sent in order (one at a time per session). Small performance cost.
  • registry.setPreserveReceiveOrder(true) (StompEndpointRegistry, 6.1+): messages from the same client session are handed to the inbound channel in order.

    Caveat (verified in Spring Framework 7.0.9 source and by test): with this flag on, exceptions thrown by inbound ChannelInterceptors (e.g., your JWT CONNECT check or subscription authorisation) are caught and only logged by OrderedMessageChannelDecorator. The message is dropped (so security holds), but no STOMP ERROR frame is sent and the connection stays open, so clients can't tell why nothing happened. Either avoid the flag when interceptors reject frames, or make clients time out CONNECT and handle it. (Bug Encyclopedia C10.)

  • Without these, if order matters, add a sequence number to messages and reorder/ignore stale ones on the client.

5.15 Message converters

Spring Boot auto-configures JSON conversion with Boot's Jackson ObjectMapper (so your spring.jackson.* settings and modules like JavaTimeModule apply).

java
@Override
public boolean configureMessageConverters(List<MessageConverter> messageConverters) {
    // Add custom converters (e.g., protobuf). Return true to also add the default converters; false to use only yours.
    messageConverters.add(new MyProtobufMessageConverter());
    return true;
}

Defaults (when not using Boot's customization): StringMessageConverter, ByteArrayMessageConverter, and a Jackson JSON converter if Jackson is present. The Jackson 2 converter is MappingJackson2MessageConverter, which is deprecated for removal since Spring Framework 7.0. Its Jackson 3 replacement is JacksonJsonMessageConverter (since 7.0). On Spring 6.x / Boot 3.x keep using MappingJackson2MessageConverter; on Spring 7 / Boot 4 use JacksonJsonMessageConverter.

Conversion is chosen by the content-type header. Clients should send content-type:application/json for JSON payloads (stompjs doesn't add it automatically). Without it Spring still attempts conversion with the available converters, and the behaviour can surprise you (e.g., the target type is String and you get raw JSON).

5.16 Monitoring: WebSocketMessageBrokerStats

Spring logs a line like this every 30 minutes (INFO, category org.springframework.web.socket.config.WebSocketMessageBrokerStats):

WebSocketSession[11 current WS(5)-HttpStream(6)-HttpPoll(0), 372 total, 0 closed abnormally
(0 connect failure, 0 send limit, 0 transport error)], stompSubProtocol[processed CONNECT(302)-CONNECTED(221)-DISCONNECT(0)],
stompBrokerRelay[null], inboundChannel[pool size = 8, active threads = 0, queued tasks = 0, completed tasks = 5862],
outboundChannel[pool size = 8, active threads = 0, queued tasks = 0, completed tasks = 3218],
sockJsScheduler[pool size = 4, active threads = 0, queued tasks = 0, completed tasks = 27]

How to read it:

  • closed abnormally (... send limit ...) → slow clients exceeding sendBufferSizeLimit/sendTimeLimit.
  • connect failure → sessions closed before CONNECTED (timeouts, auth failures).
  • queued tasks growing on inbound/outbound → thread pool saturated (slow controllers or slow clients).
  • Change the frequency with stats.setLoggingPeriod(ms). Expose numbers to Prometheus via Micrometer gauges you register yourself (Chapter 9).

5.17 Plain WebSocket in Spring (without STOMP), for completeness

java
@Configuration
@EnableWebSocket
public class RawWsConfig implements WebSocketConfigurer {
    @Override
    public void registerWebSocketHandlers(WebSocketHandlerRegistry registry) {
        registry.addHandler(new EchoHandler(), "/raw").setAllowedOriginPatterns("https://*.example.com");
    }
}

public class EchoHandler extends TextWebSocketHandler {
    @Override public void afterConnectionEstablished(WebSocketSession s) { }
    @Override protected void handleTextMessage(WebSocketSession s, TextMessage m) throws IOException {
        s.sendMessage(new TextMessage("echo: " + m.getPayload()));
    }
    @Override public void afterConnectionClosed(WebSocketSession s, CloseStatus status) { }
}
  • WebSocketSession.sendMessage is not thread-safe for concurrent sends. Wrap with ConcurrentWebSocketSessionDecorator if multiple threads send to the same session ("TEXT_PARTIAL_WRITING" / IllegalStateException: The remote endpoint was in state [TEXT_PARTIAL_WRITING] is the classic Tomcat symptom).
  • Jakarta WebSocket (JSR 356) @ServerEndpoint also works but doesn't integrate with Spring messaging.
  • When to choose raw over STOMP: custom binary protocols, game loops, proxies, or very high-frequency data where STOMP's text overhead matters.

5.18 Complete example: a real-time to-do list server

A realistic feature: users share to-do lists; changes appear live on all members' devices.

java
// --- DTOs ---
public record TodoItem(String id, String listId, String title, boolean done, long version) {}
public record TodoEvent(String type, TodoItem item, String actor, Instant at) {}       // type: CREATED/UPDATED/DELETED
public record TodoCommand(@NotBlank String listId, String itemId, @Size(max = 500) String title, Boolean done,
                          Long expectedVersion, @NotBlank String clientRequestId) {}
public record ErrorDto(String code, String message, String clientRequestId) {}

// --- Config ---
@Configuration
@EnableWebSocketMessageBroker
public class WsConfig implements WebSocketMessageBrokerConfigurer {
    @Override
    public void registerStompEndpoints(StompEndpointRegistry r) {
        r.addEndpoint("/ws").setAllowedOriginPatterns("https://app.example.com", "http://localhost:[*]");
    }
    @Override
    public void configureMessageBroker(MessageBrokerRegistry r) {
        r.setApplicationDestinationPrefixes("/app");
        r.enableSimpleBroker("/topic", "/queue")
         .setTaskScheduler(heartbeatScheduler())
         .setHeartbeatValue(new long[]{10_000, 10_000});
        r.setPreservePublishOrder(true);
    }
    @Bean ThreadPoolTaskScheduler heartbeatScheduler() {
        var s = new ThreadPoolTaskScheduler(); s.setPoolSize(1); s.setThreadNamePrefix("wss-hb-"); s.initialize(); return s;
    }
}

// --- Controller ---
@Controller
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class TodoWsController {
    private final TodoService todos;
    private final ListAccessService access;

    /** Initial snapshot when the client subscribes to /app/lists/{listId} */
    @SubscribeMapping("/lists/{listId}")
    public List<TodoItem> snapshot(@DestinationVariable String listId, Principal user) {
        access.requireMember(user.getName(), listId);
        return todos.findByList(listId);
    }

    /** Client sends to /app/todos.upsert */
    @MessageMapping("/todos.upsert")
    public void upsert(@Payload @Valid TodoCommand cmd, Principal user) {
        access.requireMember(user.getName(), cmd.listId());
        todos.upsert(cmd, user.getName());           // publishes TodoChanged domain event; idempotent by clientRequestId
    }

    @MessageExceptionHandler({OptimisticLockingFailureException.class})
    @SendToUser(destinations = "/queue/errors", broadcast = false)
    public ErrorDto conflict(OptimisticLockingFailureException ex) {
        return new ErrorDto("CONFLICT", "Item changed by someone else, please refresh", null);
    }
}

// --- Fan-out after DB commit ---
@Component
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class TodoBroadcaster {
    private final SimpMessagingTemplate messaging;

    @TransactionalEventListener(phase = TransactionPhase.AFTER_COMMIT)
    public void on(TodoChanged e) {
        messaging.convertAndSend("/topic/lists." + e.item().listId(),
                new TodoEvent(e.type(), e.item(), e.actor(), Instant.now()));
    }
}

Client flow (Chapter 6 has the code):

  1. Connect with JWT in CONNECT headers.
  2. subscribe('/topic/lists.L1') for live changes and subscribe('/app/lists/L1') for the snapshot. Subscribe to the topic first so no change is missed between snapshot and live stream, and use version to discard stale events.
  3. subscribe('/user/queue/errors').
  4. publish('/app/todos.upsert', {...clientRequestId}) and optimistically update the UI; reconcile when the event arrives.

Security note: authorise subscriptions to /topic/lists.{id} (only members!), see Chapter 8.

5.19 Chapter summary

  • @EnableWebSocketMessageBroker + WebSocketMessageBrokerConfigurer: endpoints, broker, prefixes, channels, transport, converters.
  • Three channels (inbound, outbound, broker), and messages flow through handlers: annotation handler (/app), broker (/topic, /queue), user destination handler (/user).
  • @MessageMapping, @SubscribeMapping, @SendTo, @SendToUser, @MessageExceptionHandler, @DestinationVariable, Principal.
  • SimpMessagingTemplate sends to the broker from anywhere. convertAndSendToUser resolves user → sessions.
  • Heart-beats with the simple broker need a TaskScheduler.
  • Transport limits: 64 KB message, 512 KB send buffer, 10 s send time, 60 s time to first message (defaults).
  • Ordering requires setPreservePublishOrder / setPreserveReceiveOrder.
  • Events (SessionConnectedEvent, SessionDisconnectEvent...) for presence; disconnect may fire twice.

Next: Chapter 6: Clients

Chapter 6Building

Clients (JavaScript, React, React Native, Angular, Java, Python, SockJS)

Goal: connect any client to a STOMP server correctly, robustly and securely, and know the platform-specific traps (especially React Native).

6.1 ELI5

The server is the post office. The client is your mailbox at home plus the person who checks it. A good client:

  1. Connects and shows its ID card (CONNECT with token).
  2. Says which mailboxes it wants (SUBSCRIBE).
  3. Re-connects by itself if the line breaks, and asks for its mailboxes again.
  4. Says "beep" regularly so the post office knows it's alive (heart-beats).
  5. Cleans up when the screen that needed messages is closed (UNSUBSCRIBE).

6.2 Choosing a JavaScript library

Library Status Notes
@stomp/stompjs (v7) Actively maintained, the standard TypeScript, auto-reconnect, heart-beats, works in browsers, Node, React Native
@stomp/rx-stomp Maintained RxJS wrapper over stompjs; great for Angular
@stomp/ng2-stompjs Deprecated Use rx-stomp with an Angular service instead
stompjs (jmesnil, Stomp.over(ws)) Abandoned (2014) Old tutorials use it. Migrate
webstomp-client Unmaintained Avoid in new code
sockjs-client Low activity Only if you need SockJS fallbacks
bash
npm install @stomp/stompjs
# optional
npm install @stomp/rx-stomp rxjs
npm install sockjs-client

6.3 @stomp/stompjs v7: complete guide

Checked against @stomp/stompjs 7.5.0 (the latest release on npm at the time of writing). Option names and defaults below are taken from the package source.

6.3.1 Basic usage

ts
import { Client, IMessage, StompSubscription } from '@stomp/stompjs';

const client = new Client({
  brokerURL: 'wss://api.example.com/ws',          // native WebSocket URL (not http://)
  connectHeaders: { Authorization: `Bearer ${getAccessToken()}` },
  heartbeatIncoming: 10000,                        // want server heart-beats every 10s (0 = disable)
  heartbeatOutgoing: 10000,                        // will send heart-beats every 10s
  reconnectDelay: 5000,                            // ms between reconnect attempts (0 = no auto-reconnect)
  connectionTimeout: 10000,                        // give up a connection attempt if CONNECTED not received in time
  debug: (msg) => { if (import.meta.env.DEV) console.debug(msg); },
});

client.onConnect = (frame) => {
  // Called on EVERY successful (re)connect. Put all subscriptions here!
  client.subscribe('/topic/chat.public', (message: IMessage) => {
    const payload = JSON.parse(message.body);
    renderMessage(payload);
  });
  client.subscribe('/user/queue/errors', (m) => showError(JSON.parse(m.body)));
};

client.onStompError = (frame) => {
  // STOMP ERROR frame (server will close the connection)
  console.error('Broker error:', frame.headers['message'], frame.body);
};

client.onWebSocketClose = (evt) => console.warn('WS closed', evt.code, evt.reason);
client.onWebSocketError = (evt) => console.error('WS error', evt);

client.activate();      // start connecting (and keep reconnecting)

// Send (only when connected!)
function sendChat(text: string) {
  if (!client.connected) return queueForLater(text);
  client.publish({
    destination: '/app/chat.send',
    body: JSON.stringify({ text }),
    headers: { 'content-type': 'application/json' },
  });
}

// Shut down (e.g., logout)
await client.deactivate();   // sends DISCONNECT, stops reconnecting

6.3.2 Configuration reference (most important options)

Option Default Meaning
brokerURL – ws:// or wss:// URL. Ignored if webSocketFactory is set
webSocketFactory – Function returning a WebSocket-like object (e.g., new SockJS(url))
connectHeaders {} Headers for CONNECT (login, passcode, Authorization, custom)
disconnectHeaders {} Headers for DISCONNECT
heartbeatIncoming / heartbeatOutgoing 10000 / 10000 Heart-beat intervals in ms (see Chapter 4 negotiation)
reconnectDelay 5000 Delay before reconnect; 0 disables auto-reconnect
reconnectTimeMode ReconnectionTimeMode.LINEAR EXPONENTIAL doubles the delay after each failed attempt (no random jitter is added)
maxReconnectDelay 15 minutes Cap for exponential mode (if set below reconnectDelay, it is raised to reconnectDelay)
heartbeatToleranceMultiplier 2 Server considered dead after multiplier × incoming interval with no data
heartbeatStrategy TickerStrategy.Interval TickerStrategy.Worker sends heart-beats from a Web Worker, which helps in throttled background tabs
onHeartbeatReceived / onHeartbeatLost – Callbacks for heart-beat events (handy for "connection quality" UI and metrics)
onChangeState – Activation state changes (ACTIVE, DEACTIVATING, INACTIVE)
connectionTimeout 0 (off) Fail and retry if not CONNECTED within this time
beforeConnect – async hook called before each (re)connect, the place to refresh tokens
onConnect, onDisconnect – STOMP-level connected/disconnected
onStompError – ERROR frame received
onWebSocketClose, onWebSocketError – Transport-level events
onUnhandledMessage – MESSAGE that matched no subscription (e.g., arrived after unsubscribe)
onUnhandledReceipt, onUnhandledFrame – Other unmatched frames
stompVersions 1.2, 1.1, 1.0 Versions offered
splitLargeFrames, maxWebSocketChunkSize false, 8 KB Non-standard: split large outgoing frames into chunks (Spring supports reassembly)
forceBinaryWSFrames false Send frames as binary WebSocket messages (React Native workaround)
appendMissingNULLonIncoming false Append the NULL terminator that some platforms strip (React Native workaround)
discardWebsocketOnCommFailure false On heart-beat failure, immediately discard the socket instead of waiting for close
logRawCommunication false Log raw frames in debug

6.3.3 Subscriptions

ts
const sub: StompSubscription = client.subscribe(
  '/topic/lists.L1',
  (msg) => { /* ... */ },
  { id: 'list-L1' /* optional custom id */, ack: 'auto' }
);
sub.unsubscribe();   // sends UNSUBSCRIBE
  • subscribe() throws if not connected. That's why you call it inside onConnect.
  • On reconnect, old subscriptions are gone (server side). stompjs doesn't automatically re-subscribe; your onConnect runs again and re-creates them. rx-stomp does re-subscribe its watch()ed streams automatically.

6.3.4 Acknowledgements (with a full broker)

ts
client.subscribe('/queue/jobs', (msg) => {
  try { process(JSON.parse(msg.body)); msg.ack(); }
  catch { msg.nack(); }
}, { ack: 'client-individual', 'prefetch-count': '10' /* RabbitMQ */ });

6.3.5 Receipts

ts
const receiptId = crypto.randomUUID();
client.watchForReceipt(receiptId, () => console.log('server processed it'));
client.publish({ destination: '/queue/orders', body: '{}', headers: { receipt: receiptId } });

6.3.6 Binary messages

ts
client.publish({ destination: '/app/upload.chunk', binaryBody: uint8Array,
                 headers: { 'content-type': 'application/octet-stream' } });
client.subscribe('/topic/bin', (m) => { const bytes = m.binaryBody; });

6.3.7 Token refresh before every connect

Pitfall (verified in stompjs 7.5.0 source and by test): beforeConnect must never throw. stompjs awaits it without a try/catch. If your token refresh fails (auth server down, offline), the rejection is unhandled and the reconnect loop stops for good. Always wrap it:

ts
beforeConnect: async () => {
  try { client.connectHeaders = { Authorization: `Bearer ${await getToken()}` }; }
  catch { /* keep old headers; this attempt fails and normal backoff retries */ }
},

Also set discardWebsocketOnCommFailure: true, so an attempt stuck in CONNECTING (seen with Node's WebSocket) is dropped and the next attempt starts.

ts
client.beforeConnect = async () => {
  const token = await authService.getFreshAccessToken();     // refresh if near expiry
  client.connectHeaders = { Authorization: `Bearer ${token}` };
};

Pitfall: token expired while connected. STOMP authenticates once at CONNECT. If the token expires an hour later, the connection stays authenticated unless the server enforces expiry (Chapter 8). When the server closes the session because of expiry, beforeConnect must fetch a fresh token or you'll loop forever with a stale one.

6.3.8 Publishing safely while disconnected

client.publish throws TypeError: There is no underlying STOMP connection when not connected. Pattern:

ts
const outbox: Array<() => void> = [];
function safePublish(p: Parameters<Client['publish']>[0]) {
  if (client.connected) client.publish(p);
  else outbox.push(() => client.publish(p));
}
client.onConnect = () => {
  resubscribeAll();
  while (outbox.length) outbox.shift()!();   // flush; server must dedupe (idempotency key!)
};

6.4 Legacy API (recognise it in old code)

js
// OLD jmesnil stomp.js (2014), don't use in new code
var socket = new SockJS('/ws');
var stompClient = Stomp.over(socket);
stompClient.connect({}, function (frame) {
  stompClient.subscribe('/topic/greetings', function (msg) { ... });
});
stompClient.send('/app/hello', {}, JSON.stringify({ name: 'x' }));

@stomp/stompjs still offers a compatibility Stomp.over() / CompatClient (deprecated). The modern mapping: connect → activate + onConnect; send(dest, headers, body) → publish({destination, headers, body}); disconnect → deactivate. A key difference: the old Stomp.over(socket) can't auto-reconnect when given a socket instance. It needs a factory.

6.5 SockJS fallback

What: SockJS is a protocol + client library that emulates WebSocket using HTTP streaming/polling when real WebSocket is blocked (old proxies, restrictive corporate networks).

Transport order (tries in order):

  1. websocket
  2. Streaming: xhr-streaming, xdr-streaming (old IE), eventsource, iframe-eventsource, htmlfile, iframe-htmlfile
  3. Polling: xhr-polling, xdr-polling, iframe-xhr-polling, jsonp-polling

How it works:

  1. GET /ws/info → { "websocket": true, "cookie_needed": true, "origins": ["*:*"], "entropy": 1234 }
  2. Picks a transport and uses URLs like /ws/{server-id}/{session-id}/{transport} (e.g., /ws/123/abcxyz/websocket, /ws/123/abcxyz/xhr_streaming).
  3. SockJS frames: o = open, h = heart-beat, a["msg1","msg2"] = array of messages, c[3000,"Go away!"] = close.

Server: registry.addEndpoint("/ws").withSockJS(); Notable server options: setHeartbeatTime (default 25 s), setDisconnectDelay (default 5 s), setStreamBytesLimit (default 128 KB), setHttpMessageCacheSize (default 100), setSessionCookieNeeded (default true, sends JSESSIONID for sticky sessions), setClientLibraryUrl (for iframe transports).

Client:

ts
import SockJS from 'sockjs-client';
const client = new Client({
  webSocketFactory: () => new SockJS('https://api.example.com/ws'),   // http(s)://, NOT ws(s)://
});

Gotchas:

  • With SockJS, the native WebSocket URL is /ws/websocket (Spring also accepts raw WebSocket there); plain /ws expects the SockJS protocol. Using new WebSocket('wss://host/ws') against a .withSockJS() endpoint fails (wrong path). Either use SockJS on the client, or register two endpoints: one with and one without SockJS.
  • HTTP fallbacks need sticky sessions in clusters (all requests of a SockJS session must hit the same server).
  • SockJS can't use custom headers either. And for HTTP transports, cookies are sent normally.
  • In 2025+ most teams skip SockJS (WebSocket works nearly everywhere over wss:// on 443). Keep it only if you have real users behind blocking proxies (check your metrics: Spring stats show HttpStream/HttpPoll session counts).

6.6 React (web)

6.6.1 A robust hook with a singleton client

tsx
// stomp.ts: ONE client for the whole app
import { Client, IMessage } from '@stomp/stompjs';

type Listener = (msg: IMessage) => void;
const listeners = new Map<string, Set<Listener>>();   // destination → callbacks
const subs = new Map<string, { unsubscribe(): void }>();

export const stomp = new Client({
  brokerURL: `${location.protocol === 'https:' ? 'wss' : 'ws'}://${location.host}/ws`,
  reconnectDelay: 3000,
  heartbeatIncoming: 10000,
  heartbeatOutgoing: 10000,
  beforeConnect: async () => {
    stomp.connectHeaders = { Authorization: `Bearer ${await getToken()}` };
  },
  onConnect: () => {
    subs.clear();
    for (const dest of listeners.keys()) subscribeDest(dest);   // re-subscribe after reconnect
  },
});

function subscribeDest(dest: string) {
  subs.set(dest, stomp.subscribe(dest, (m) => listeners.get(dest)?.forEach((l) => l(m))));
}

export function addListener(dest: string, l: Listener): () => void {
  if (!listeners.has(dest)) {
    listeners.set(dest, new Set());
    if (stomp.connected) subscribeDest(dest);
  }
  listeners.get(dest)!.add(l);
  return () => {                                   // cleanup
    const set = listeners.get(dest)!;
    set.delete(l);
    if (set.size === 0) {
      listeners.delete(dest);
      if (stomp.connected) subs.get(dest)?.unsubscribe();
      subs.delete(dest);
    }
  };
}
tsx
// useStompTopic.ts
import { useEffect, useRef } from 'react';
import { addListener } from './stomp';

export function useStompTopic<T>(destination: string | null, onMessage: (data: T) => void) {
  const cb = useRef(onMessage);
  cb.current = onMessage;                               // always call latest callback, no re-subscribe
  useEffect(() => {
    if (!destination) return;
    return addListener(destination, (m) => cb.current(JSON.parse(m.body)));
  }, [destination]);
}

// Usage
function ChatRoom({ roomId }: { roomId: string }) {
  const [messages, setMessages] = useState<ChatMsg[]>([]);
  useStompTopic<ChatMsg>(`/topic/rooms.${roomId}`, (msg) => setMessages((prev) => [...prev, msg]));
  ...
}

Why this design:

  • One connection per app, not per component (a common bug: each component creates its own Client → dozens of sockets).
  • Reference counting per destination → no duplicate SUBSCRIBEs, no duplicate messages.
  • Cleanup on unmount → no leaks, no "setState on unmounted component".
  • useRef for callback → avoids re-subscribing on every render.
  • React 18 StrictMode mounts/unmounts effects twice in dev. The ref-counted design tolerates it. A naive useEffect(() => { client.activate(); return () => client.deactivate(); }, []) will connect/disconnect/connect in dev, so don't panic when you see it.
  • Use setMessages(prev => ...) (functional update) to avoid stale closure bugs.

Activate on login, deactivate on logout:

ts
onLogin(() => stomp.activate());
onLogout(() => stomp.deactivate());

6.7 React Native (this project's platform!)

React Native's WebSocket is a native implementation (OkHttp on Android, SocketRocket/NSURLSession-based on iOS), not a browser one. Known STOMP issues:

6.7.1 The NULL byte problem

React Native has historically truncated text WebSocket messages at the NULL (\0) character, because strings cross the native bridge as C strings in some code paths. Since every STOMP frame ends with NULL, frames get corrupted: incoming frames lose their terminator, and outgoing frames may be cut.

Fix (documented by stompjs):

ts
const client = new Client({
  brokerURL: 'wss://api.example.com/ws',
  forceBinaryWSFrames: true,          // send STOMP frames as binary WebSocket messages
  appendMissingNULLonIncoming: true,  // re-add the NULL terminator that RN chopped off incoming frames
  // ...
});

6.7.2 TextEncoder/TextDecoder

stompjs needs TextEncoder/TextDecoder. Older RN/JS engines lacked them. Recent React Native versions using Hermes provide TextEncoder. If you get ReferenceError: Can't find variable: TextEncoder, add a polyfill at the top of index.js:

js
import 'text-encoding';            // or 'fast-text-encoding'

6.7.3 Android cleartext and emulator hosts

  • ws:// (cleartext) is blocked by default on Android 9+ (and iOS ATS blocks non-TLS). Use wss://, or allow cleartext for dev hosts only (network_security_config.xml / NSAppTransportSecurity exceptions).
  • From the Android emulator, your laptop's localhost is 10.0.2.2. From a physical device, use your machine's LAN IP. ws://localhost:8080/ws from the emulator connects to the emulator itself.

6.7.4 App lifecycle

ts
import { AppState, AppStateStatus } from 'react-native';
import NetInfo from '@react-native-community/netinfo';

AppState.addEventListener('change', (state: AppStateStatus) => {
  if (state === 'active') { if (!client.active) client.activate(); }
  else if (state === 'background') { /* optionally deactivate after a grace period to save battery */ }
});

NetInfo.addEventListener((s) => {
  if (s.isConnected && !client.connected && client.active) {
    // stompjs will retry after reconnectDelay; you can force a faster retry by deactivate()+activate()
  }
});
  • iOS suspends apps in background within seconds. The socket dies. Use push notifications (APNs/FCM) for background alerts and resync over REST on foreground.
  • Heart-beats + connectionTimeout are essential on mobile (network switches Wi-Fi ↔ cellular).

6.7.5 Complete RN example for the to-do app

ts
// src/realtime/todoSocket.ts
import { Client } from '@stomp/stompjs';
import { getAccessToken } from '../auth';

export function createTodoSocket(onEvent: (e: TodoEvent) => void, listIds: () => string[]) {
  const client = new Client({
    brokerURL: 'wss://api.example.com/ws',
    forceBinaryWSFrames: true,
    appendMissingNULLonIncoming: true,
    heartbeatIncoming: 10000,
    heartbeatOutgoing: 10000,
    reconnectDelay: 3000,
    connectionTimeout: 8000,
    beforeConnect: async () => {
      client.connectHeaders = { Authorization: `Bearer ${await getAccessToken()}` };
    },
    onConnect: () => {
      for (const id of listIds()) {
        client.subscribe(`/topic/lists.${id}`, (m) => onEvent(JSON.parse(m.body)));
      }
      client.subscribe('/user/queue/errors', (m) => console.warn('server error', m.body));
      // After (re)connect: re-fetch lists over REST to fill any gap while offline
    },
    onStompError: (f) => console.warn('STOMP error', f.headers.message),
  });
  return client;
}

6.8 Angular with rx-stomp

ts
// rx-stomp.service.ts
import { Injectable } from '@angular/core';
import { RxStomp } from '@stomp/rx-stomp';

@Injectable({ providedIn: 'root' })
export class RxStompService extends RxStomp {}

// app.config.ts (standalone) or a factory
export function rxStompServiceFactory(auth: AuthService) {
  const rx = new RxStompService();
  rx.configure({
    brokerURL: 'wss://api.example.com/ws',
    heartbeatIncoming: 10000,
    heartbeatOutgoing: 10000,
    reconnectDelay: 3000,
    beforeConnect: async (client) => { client.connectHeaders = { Authorization: `Bearer ${await auth.token()}` }; },
  });
  rx.activate();
  return rx;
}

// component
this.sub = this.rxStomp.watch('/topic/chat.public')
  .pipe(map((m) => JSON.parse(m.body) as ChatMsg))
  .subscribe((msg) => this.messages.push(msg));
// ngOnDestroy: this.sub.unsubscribe()   → sends UNSUBSCRIBE automatically

this.rxStomp.publish({ destination: '/app/chat.send', body: JSON.stringify({ text }) });
this.rxStomp.connectionState$.subscribe((state) => this.status = RxStompState[state]);

watch() re-subscribes automatically after reconnects. publish() while disconnected queues by default in rx-stomp (check the version's docs for behaviour) whereas stompjs throws.

6.9 Vue / Svelte / vanilla

Same principles: one client in a module/store (Pinia store, Svelte store), ref-counted subscriptions, unsubscribe in onUnmounted/onDestroy.

6.10 Java client (WebSocketStompClient)

Used for service-to-service, integration tests, load tests, and desktop/Android (JVM) clients.

java
WebSocketClient transport = new StandardWebSocketClient();       // JSR-356 client (e.g., Tomcat's)
WebSocketStompClient stompClient = new WebSocketStompClient(transport);
stompClient.setMessageConverter(new MappingJackson2MessageConverter());   // Spring 6.x (Jackson 2)
// Spring 7.x (Jackson 3): stompClient.setMessageConverter(new JacksonJsonMessageConverter());
ThreadPoolTaskScheduler scheduler = new ThreadPoolTaskScheduler();
scheduler.initialize();
stompClient.setTaskScheduler(scheduler);                          // needed for heart-beats (& receipts timeouts)
stompClient.setDefaultHeartbeat(new long[]{10_000, 10_000});

StompHeaders connectHeaders = new StompHeaders();
connectHeaders.add("Authorization", "Bearer " + token);

StompSession session = stompClient
        .connectAsync("wss://api.example.com/ws", new WebSocketHttpHeaders(), connectHeaders, new MySessionHandler())
        .get(10, TimeUnit.SECONDS);                              // connectAsync since Spring 6.0 (connect() deprecated)

session.subscribe("/topic/chat.public", new StompFrameHandler() {
    @Override public Type getPayloadType(StompHeaders headers) { return ChatMessage.class; }
    @Override public void handleFrame(StompHeaders headers, Object payload) {
        ChatMessage msg = (ChatMessage) payload;
        System.out.println(msg);
    }
});

session.send("/app/chat.send", new ChatMessage("hello from Java"));

// Receipts
session.setAutoReceipt(true);
StompSession.Receiptable r = session.send("/app/chat.send", new ChatMessage("with receipt"));
r.addReceiptTask(() -> System.out.println("received by server"));
r.addReceiptLostTask(() -> System.out.println("no receipt in time"));   // receipt time limit defaults to 15 s (setReceiptTimeLimit); needs a TaskScheduler

session.disconnect();
java
class MySessionHandler extends StompSessionHandlerAdapter {
    @Override public void afterConnected(StompSession session, StompHeaders connectedHeaders) { /* subscribe here */ }
    @Override public void handleException(StompSession s, StompCommand cmd, StompHeaders h, byte[] payload, Throwable ex) { ex.printStackTrace(); }
    @Override public void handleTransportError(StompSession s, Throwable ex) { /* reconnect with backoff */ }
}

Notes:

  • WebSocketStompClient does not auto-reconnect. Implement it in handleTransportError with backoff.
  • SockJS from Java: new SockJsClient(List.of(new WebSocketTransport(new StandardWebSocketClient()), new RestTemplateXhrTransport())).
  • For high connection counts in load tests, tune the JSR-356 client's buffers and use a dedicated thread pool.
  • Spring Framework 7 / Boot 4 move to Jackson 3: MappingJackson2MessageConverter is deprecated for removal in 7.0. Use JacksonJsonMessageConverter.
  • WebSocketStompClient's constructor sets heart-beats to 0,0; calling setTaskScheduler(...) switches them to 10000,10000 (from the Spring source). It only sends a heart-beat when nothing else was sent.
  • Spring's docs advise that when you simulate many clients from one machine (load tests), you consider turning heart-beats off, because each connection schedules its own heart-beat tasks.

6.11 Other platforms

Platform Library Notes
Python stomp.py Mature STOMP client for TCP brokers (RabbitMQ/ActiveMQ on 61613). For STOMP-over-WebSocket servers like Spring, check stomp.py's WebSocket support in your version or write frames over the websockets library (frame format is trivial, see Chapter 4)
Go go-stomp/stomp TCP; combine with a WebSocket net.Conn adapter for WS
.NET Apache.NMS.STOMP, community STOMP-over-WS libs Many .NET shops use SignalR instead
Kotlin / Android Krossbow Coroutine-based, multiplatform STOMP client over WebSocket (OkHttp/Ktor)
Swift / iOS SwiftStomp, StompClientLib Check maintenance status; or URLSessionWebSocketTask + small frame codec
Ruby stomp gem TCP
CLI websocat, wscat, Postman Manual testing (remember the \0 terminator!)

6.12 Manual testing with raw tools

bash
# wscat (Node)
npx wscat -c ws://localhost:8080/ws -s v12.stomp
> CONNECT
accept-version:1.2
host:localhost

^@

In terminals you often can't type NULL easily. Use tools that allow escape sequences (Postman: \u0000; websocat with --binary and printf).

bash
printf 'CONNECT\naccept-version:1.2\nhost:localhost\n\n\0' | websocat -n --binary -1 ws://localhost:8080/ws

6.13 Client-side best practices checklist

  • Exactly one STOMP connection per app (per tab) unless you have a strong reason.
  • Subscriptions created in onConnect (or via rx-stomp watch) so they survive reconnects.
  • Unsubscribe when screens/components are destroyed.
  • Heart-beats on (10–25 s), lower than every proxy's idle timeout.
  • Reconnect with backoff (+ jitter if your library supports it, or manage reconnectDelay dynamically).
  • Refresh token in beforeConnect; handle auth ERROR by re-login, not infinite retries.
  • Never publish without checking connected (or use an outbox).
  • Send content-type: application/json for JSON.
  • Idempotency key (clientRequestId) on commands that might be retried.
  • After reconnect, resync missed data via REST (since=lastEventId).
  • Show connection state in UI ("Reconnecting…") and disable actions that need the socket.
  • React Native: forceBinaryWSFrames + appendMissingNULLonIncoming, wss://, emulator host 10.0.2.2.
  • Turn off verbose debug logging in production (it can log tokens in CONNECT frames!).

Next: Chapter 7: Brokers, Clustering & Scaling

Chapter 7Production

Message Brokers, Clustering & Scaling

Goal: run STOMP for thousands to millions of users. Understand simple broker vs broker relay, configure RabbitMQ/ActiveMQ, scale horizontally, configure load balancers/proxies/Kubernetes, and deploy without dropping everyone.

7.1 ELI5

One post office (one server) can only serve so many houses. When the town grows, you open more post offices. But now a letter from someone near post office A to someone near post office B must travel between offices. You need a central sorting hub that all post offices are connected to. That hub is the external message broker (RabbitMQ, ActiveMQ) or a backplane (Redis, Kafka).

7.2 Simple broker vs broker relay

Simple broker (enableSimpleBroker) STOMP broker relay (enableStompBrokerRelay)
Where In-memory, inside your Spring app External broker (RabbitMQ, ActiveMQ, Artemis)
Setup Zero Run & operate a broker
Clustering No: each instance only knows its own subscribers Yes: broker connects all instances
STOMP features Subset: no ACK/NACK, no transactions, limited receipts Full (whatever the broker supports)
Persistence/durability None (lost on restart) Durable queues, persistent messages, DLQs
Throughput Very high for one node (no network hop) Network hop to broker; broker becomes a shared resource
Connections None extra One TCP connection per client session + one shared "system" connection
Destination semantics Every destination is pub/sub Broker-defined (/queue competing consumers, /topic fan-out, /exchange...)
Best for Single instance, dev, prototypes, small apps, or with a custom backplane Clusters, enterprise, reliability needs

Interview tip: "Why do my users on server 2 not receive messages sent on server 1?" → Simple broker isn't clustered. Use a broker relay or a pub/sub backplane.

7.3 How the broker relay works internally

             Spring instance A                                     RabbitMQ / ActiveMQ
 ┌───────────────────────────────────────────┐                ┌─────────────────────┐
 │ client session s1 ─ TCP conn #1 (clientLogin) ─────────────►│                     │
 │ client session s2 ─ TCP conn #2 (clientLogin) ─────────────►│   STOMP adapter     │
 │ ...                                        │                │   (port 61613)      │
 │ "system" session  ─ TCP conn (systemLogin) ───────────────►│                     │
 │   used by SimpMessagingTemplate / @SendTo  │                └─────────────────────┘
 └───────────────────────────────────────────┘
  • For each WebSocket client CONNECT, Spring opens a dedicated TCP connection to the broker and sends a STOMP CONNECT using clientLogin/clientPasscode (not the end-user's credentials!). Default guest/guest.
  • Messages from server-side code (brokerChannel: SimpMessagingTemplate, @SendTo return values) go over a single shared system connection (systemLogin/systemPasscode), with heart-beats (default 10 s both ways).
  • Frames from clients to broker destinations (/topic/**, /queue/**) are forwarded as-is; MESSAGE frames from the broker are forwarded back to the right WebSocket session.
  • Client heart-beats are negotiated with the broker (the relay passes the client's heart-beat header through).
  • If the system connection drops, Spring publishes BrokerAvailabilityEvent(false), SimpMessagingTemplate sends fail with MessageDeliveryException ("Message broker not active"), and new client CONNECTs get an ERROR. Spring reconnects automatically (every 5 s by default).
  • Requires Reactor Netty on the classpath. Spring's reference docs say to add io.projectreactor.netty:reactor-netty and io.netty:netty-all.
  • Defaults from the Spring source: clientLogin/clientPasscode/systemLogin/systemPasscode = guest; system heart-beats 10000 ms both ways; the system connection reconnects every 5 s (FixedIntervalReconnectStrategy(5000)).

Capacity implication: 50,000 WebSocket users across your app instances = ~50,000 TCP connections to the broker. Size the broker (file descriptors, memory, Erlang process limits for RabbitMQ) accordingly. If that's too many, use the backplane pattern (§7.7) instead.

7.3.1 Full relay configuration

java
@Override
public void configureMessageBroker(MessageBrokerRegistry registry) {
    registry.setApplicationDestinationPrefixes("/app");
    registry.setPathMatcher(new AntPathMatcher("."));          // RabbitMQ topic routing keys use dots
    registry.enableStompBrokerRelay("/topic", "/queue", "/exchange", "/amq/queue", "/temp-queue")
            .setRelayHost("rabbitmq.internal")
            .setRelayPort(61613)
            .setVirtualHost("/")                               // "host" header in STOMP CONNECT
            .setClientLogin("ws-client")
            .setClientPasscode(env.getRequiredProperty("broker.client.password"))
            .setSystemLogin("ws-system")
            .setSystemPasscode(env.getRequiredProperty("broker.system.password"))
            .setSystemHeartbeatSendInterval(10_000)
            .setSystemHeartbeatReceiveInterval(10_000)
            .setAutoStartup(true)
            .setUserDestinationBroadcast("/topic/unresolved-user-destination")
            .setUserRegistryBroadcast("/topic/simp-user-registry");
}

7.3.2 Multiple broker nodes / TLS / failover

The relay connects to one host:port. For failover, put the broker cluster behind a TCP load balancer, or supply a custom TcpClient that rotates through addresses:

java
@Override
public void configureMessageBroker(MessageBrokerRegistry registry) {
    registry.enableStompBrokerRelay("/topic", "/queue").setTcpClient(createTcpClient());
    registry.setApplicationDestinationPrefixes("/app");
}

private ReactorNettyTcpClient<byte[]> createTcpClient() {
    Iterator<InetSocketAddress> addresses = Iterables.cycle(List.of(
            new InetSocketAddress("rabbit-1", 61614), new InetSocketAddress("rabbit-2", 61614))).iterator(); // Guava cycle, or your own round-robin
    return new ReactorNettyTcpClient<>(
            client -> client.remoteAddress(addresses::next).secure(),   // .secure() enables TLS (STOMP over TLS is often port 61614)
            new StompReactorNettyCodec());
}

(Spring's reference documentation shows this approach; adapt the address-rotation code to your utilities.)

7.4 User destinations in a cluster

Problem: user anna is connected to instance B. Instance A calls convertAndSendToUser("anna", ...). A's local SimpUserRegistry doesn't know anna's sessions.

Spring's solution (broker relay only):

  • setUserDestinationBroadcast("/topic/unresolved-user-destination"): if a user destination can't be resolved locally, it is broadcast to this topic; every instance listens and the one that has anna's sessions delivers it.
  • setUserRegistryBroadcast("/topic/simp-user-registry"): each instance periodically broadcasts its local user registry, so every instance has a MultiServerUserRegistry view of all users (useful for "who's online" counts across the cluster).

7.5 RabbitMQ in depth

7.5.1 Enabling STOMP

bash
rabbitmq-plugins enable rabbitmq_stomp        # STOMP over TCP, port 61613 (TLS commonly 61614)
rabbitmq-plugins enable rabbitmq_web_stomp    # STOMP over WebSocket, port 15674, path /ws

docker run -d -p 5672:5672 -p 15672:15672 -p 61613:61613 rabbitmq:3-management then enable the plugin inside (or build an image with RUN rabbitmq-plugins enable --offline rabbitmq_stomp).

The guest user can only connect from localhost by default. Create dedicated users for the relay.

7.5.2 RabbitMQ STOMP destinations

Destination SUBSCRIBE behaviour SEND behaviour Use
/exchange/<exchange>[/<routing-pattern>] Creates an exclusive, auto-delete queue bound to the exchange with the pattern Publishes to the exchange with routing key Full control over AMQP routing
/queue/<name> Subscribes to queue <name> (created if needed); several subscribers compete Sends to that queue via default exchange Work queues, point-to-point
/amq/queue/<name> Subscribes to an existing queue (not created) Sends to existing queue Queues managed elsewhere (AMQP apps)
/topic/<routing-key> Creates a queue bound to amq.topic with the routing key/pattern (auto-delete by default; durable with durable:true+auto-delete:false and a stable id/x-queue-name) Publishes to amq.topic Pub/sub with wildcards
/temp-queue/<name> (Used in reply-to) Creates a temporary private reply queue Request/reply

Topic wildcards (AMQP style, dot-separated):

  • * matches exactly one word: /topic/prices.* matches prices.AAPL, not prices.us.AAPL.
  • # matches zero or more words: /topic/prices.# matches prices, prices.AAPL, prices.us.AAPL.

Useful headers:

Header Frame Effect
prefetch-count SUBSCRIBE Max unacked messages delivered (with ack:client/client-individual)
durable / auto-delete / exclusive SUBSCRIBE Queue properties
x-queue-name SUBSCRIBE Explicit name for the generated queue
x-message-ttl, x-expires, x-max-length, x-dead-letter-exchange, x-queue-type SUBSCRIBE Queue arguments (TTL, expiry, length limit, DLX, quorum/stream queues)
persistent:true SEND Message is persisted (if the queue is durable)
reply-to SEND Reply destination, e.g. /temp-queue/r
x-stream-offset SUBSCRIBE Where to start reading a stream queue

7.5.3 User destinations with RabbitMQ (avoid queue leaks)

Spring's /user/queue/x becomes /queue/x-user{sessionId} → RabbitMQ creates a named queue per session which, per RabbitMQ's docs, is durable, non-exclusive and not auto-deleted by default for /queue/ destinations. So it can outlive the session and leak. Spring's docs recommend using destinations that RabbitMQ makes auto-delete, e.g. have clients subscribe to /user/exchange/amq.direct/position-updates and send with convertAndSendToUser(user, "/exchange/amq.direct/position-updates", payload). Then the per-session queue is auto-deleted when the session ends. Alternatively set x-expires/policies on such queues. Monitor queue counts in production: a steadily growing count = leak.

7.5.4 RabbitMQ operational tips

  • Use quorum queues for durable work queues; classic queues for transient per-session queues.
  • Set policies (rabbitmqctl set_policy) for TTL/expiry/max-length instead of per-subscribe headers where possible.
  • Watch: connections count, channels, memory alarms (publishers get blocked), file descriptors, unacked messages.
  • For very high fan-out to browsers, consider RabbitMQ Web STOMP directly (browsers connect to RabbitMQ), but then you lose Spring's @MessageMapping, auth integration and user destinations; and you expose the broker to the internet. Most enterprises keep Spring in front.

7.6 ActiveMQ Classic and ActiveMQ Artemis

ActiveMQ Classic (activemq.xml):

xml
<transportConnectors>
  <transportConnector name="stomp" uri="stomp://0.0.0.0:61613?transport.maxFrameSize=1048576"/>
  <transportConnector name="ws" uri="ws://0.0.0.0:61614"/>
</transportConnectors>

/queue/x → JMS queue x; /topic/x → JMS topic x. Supports JMS-style selector header on SUBSCRIBE, durable topic subscriptions (activemq.subscriptionName + client-id), persistent:true on SEND.

ActiveMQ Artemis: acceptors in broker.xml, e.g.

xml
<acceptor name="stomp">tcp://0.0.0.0:61613?protocols=STOMP;anycastPrefix=/queue/;multicastPrefix=/topic/</acceptor>

anycastPrefix/multicastPrefix map /queue/ to anycast (point-to-point) and /topic/ to multicast (pub/sub). STOMP over WebSocket is supported on acceptors (the default all-protocols acceptor on 61616 accepts it too).
