7.7 The backplane pattern (simple broker + Redis/Kafka)

When you want horizontal scale without one broker TCP connection per user:

 clients ──ws──► instance A (simple broker) ─┐                ┌─► instance A re-publishes locally
 clients ──ws──► instance B (simple broker) ─┼── Redis pub/sub ┼─► instance B re-publishes locally
 clients ──ws──► instance C (simple broker) ─┘   (or Kafka)    └─► instance C re-publishes locally
  1. Any instance that wants to broadcast publishes to Redis (or Kafka), not directly to its local broker.
  2. Every instance subscribes to Redis and, on receive, calls SimpMessagingTemplate.convertAndSend(...) locally.
  3. Each local simple broker delivers to its own connected clients.
java
public record ClusterEnvelope(String destination, String user, String payloadJson, Map<String, String> headers) {}

@Service
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class ClusterMessaging {
    private final StringRedisTemplate redis;
    private final ObjectMapper json;

    public void broadcast(String destination, Object payload) throws JsonProcessingException {
        redis.convertAndSend("ws-broadcast", json.writeValueAsString(
            new ClusterEnvelope(destination, null, json.writeValueAsString(payload), Map.of())));
    }
    public void toUser(String user, String destination, Object payload) throws JsonProcessingException {
        redis.convertAndSend("ws-broadcast", json.writeValueAsString(
            new ClusterEnvelope(destination, user, json.writeValueAsString(payload), Map.of())));
    }
}

@Configuration
public class RedisBackplaneConfig {
    @Bean
    RedisMessageListenerContainer container(RedisConnectionFactory cf, SimpMessagingTemplate messaging, ObjectMapper json) {
        var c = new RedisMessageListenerContainer();
        c.setConnectionFactory(cf);
        c.addMessageListener((message, pattern) -> {
            try {
                ClusterEnvelope env = json.readValue(message.getBody(), ClusterEnvelope.class);
                Object payload = json.readTree(env.payloadJson());
                if (env.user() == null) messaging.convertAndSend(env.destination(), payload);
                else messaging.convertAndSendToUser(env.user(), env.destination(), payload);  // no-op if user not on this node
            } catch (IOException e) { /* log + metric */ }
        }, new ChannelTopic("ws-broadcast"));
        return c;
    }
}

Trade-offs:

  • Redis pub/sub is fire-and-forget (at-most-once): if an instance is disconnected from Redis briefly, it misses messages. Redis Streams or Kafka give replay at the cost of complexity.
  • Every instance receives every message (fine up to a point). For huge scale, shard channels (e.g., per room/tenant) and only subscribe to shards with local subscribers.
  • Simple and cheap; very common in practice.

7.8 Capacity planning

7.8.1 How many connections can one server hold?

It depends on memory per connection and message rate, not a magic number. Rough components per connection in a Spring/Tomcat stack: socket buffers (kernel), Tomcat WebSocket session and buffers (the 8 KB default text/binary buffers are allocated per session), Spring session objects, ConcurrentWebSocketSessionDecorator buffers when clients are slow, the subscription registry entries, plus your own per-session state.

Measure it: load test with N idle connections, then N connections × your real message rate, and watch heap, GC, CPU, and p99 latency. Many teams see tens of thousands of mostly idle STOMP connections per instance on a few GB of heap; heavy traffic lowers that.

7.8.2 OS and network limits

  • File descriptors: each socket = 1 fd. Raise ulimit -n (e.g., LimitNOFILE=1000000 in systemd, container ulimits).
  • Ephemeral ports between a proxy and backend: one (src IP, dst IP, dst port) tuple supports ~28k–64k concurrent connections (net.ipv4.ip_local_port_range). For huge numbers, add more backend IPs/ports or more proxy IPs.
  • Kernel tuning: net.core.somaxconn, tcp_max_syn_backlog for reconnect storms; TCP keepalive settings.
  • Memory for TLS: TLS sessions add memory. Terminate TLS at the load balancer to offload, or account for it.

7.8.3 Fan-out math (the hidden cost)

Messages/second delivered = messages/sec published × subscribers per message. Example: 100 rooms × 1 msg/s × 1,000 users per room = 100,000 deliveries/s. The outbound channel and network, not your controllers, become the bottleneck. Mitigations: batching/coalescing (send the latest state every 100–250 ms instead of every tick), deltas instead of full objects, binary formats, smaller audiences (shard topics), and dropping intermediate updates for slow clients.

7.9 Load balancers and reverse proxies

7.9.1 Requirements

  1. Forward Upgrade and Connection headers (HTTP/1.1 to backend).
  2. Long idle/read timeouts (or rely on heart-beats shorter than the timeout).
  3. Sticky sessions: not needed for pure WebSocket (one TCP connection stays on one backend), but required for SockJS HTTP fallbacks and helpful if you keep server-side state across reconnects.
  4. Balance by least connections rather than round-robin (connections are long-lived).
  5. Connection draining on deploys.

7.9.2 Nginx

nginx
map $http_upgrade $connection_upgrade {
    default upgrade;
    ''      close;
}

upstream realtime_backend {
    least_conn;
    server app1:8080 max_fails=3 fail_timeout=10s;
    server app2:8080 max_fails=3 fail_timeout=10s;
    # ip_hash;   # or sticky (nginx plus / cookie) if you use SockJS fallbacks
}

server {
    listen 443 ssl;
    server_name api.example.com;
    # ssl_certificate ...;

    location /ws {
        proxy_pass http://realtime_backend;
        proxy_http_version 1.1;                          # REQUIRED (Upgrade is HTTP/1.1)
        proxy_set_header Upgrade $http_upgrade;          # REQUIRED (hop-by-hop headers aren't forwarded by default)
        proxy_set_header Connection $connection_upgrade; # REQUIRED
        proxy_set_header Host $host;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme;
        proxy_read_timeout 3600s;                        # default 60s; keep > heart-beat interval at least
        proxy_send_timeout 3600s;
        proxy_buffering off;
    }
}

Spring behind a proxy: set server.forward-headers-strategy=framework (or native) so Spring sees the original scheme/host. This matters for origin checks and SockJS URLs.

7.9.3 HAProxy

defaults
    timeout connect 5s
    timeout client  60s
    timeout server  60s
    timeout tunnel  1h          # applies once the connection is upgraded to WebSocket
backend realtime
    balance leastconn
    server app1 10.0.0.1:8080 check
    server app2 10.0.0.2:8080 check

7.9.4 Cloud load balancers (verify current limits in provider docs)

  • AWS ALB: native WebSocket support; idle timeout default 60 s (raise it, and heart-beat below it). Stickiness is optional (needed for SockJS).
  • AWS NLB: TCP passthrough; TCP idle timeout default 350 s.
  • AWS API Gateway WebSocket APIs: managed connections with Lambda integrations. It doesn't speak STOMP natively; it has limits such as 2-hour max connection duration, 10-minute idle timeout, 128 KB messages.
  • GCP HTTP(S) LB: WebSockets supported; the backend service timeout acts as the max connection lifetime for WebSockets (default 30 s, so raise it!).
  • Azure Application Gateway / Front Door: WebSocket supported; check idle timeouts.
  • Cloudflare: proxies WebSockets; idle connections without traffic may be closed after ~100 s; also connections can be closed during Cloudflare deploys → reconnect logic.

7.9.5 Kubernetes

  • Service type ClusterIP/LoadBalancer works (TCP).
  • Ingress (NGINX-based): WebSocket works; raise timeouts:
    yaml
    metadata:
  annotations:
    nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/proxy-read-timeout: "3600"
    nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/proxy-send-timeout: "3600"
    # for SockJS fallbacks:
    nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/affinity: "cookie"
    (The community ingress-nginx controller was retired in March 2026: the Kubernetes project announced it in November 2025, and after March 2026 there are no further releases or security patches. Some vendors offer extended support. New setups should use a Gateway API implementation (e.g., Envoy Gateway, Istio, NGINX Gateway Fabric, Traefik, or your cloud's gateway). The same rules apply on any of them: WebSocket upgrade enabled, long idle/route timeouts, and affinity only for SockJS.)
  • Istio/Envoy: WebSocket upgrade supported; set route timeout: 0s (disabled) or long; idle timeouts via DestinationRule/EnvoyFilter (idle_timeout).
  • HPA: CPU-based autoscaling is a poor fit (idle connections use memory, not CPU). Scale on connection count or memory with custom metrics.
  • Imbalance after scale-out: new pods get no existing connections. Options: accept it; enforce a max connection age (server closes connections older than X hours with jitter, clients reconnect and spread); use least-connections balancing.

7.10 Deployments without pain

When a pod/instance stops, all its WebSocket clients disconnect. To make that harmless:

  1. Clients reconnect automatically with jitter (spread the herd).
  2. Readiness first: on shutdown, mark the instance not ready (remove from LB) before closing connections. In Kubernetes, use a preStop hook (e.g., sleep 10) so endpoints update.
  3. Close gracefully: Spring closes sessions when the context stops (SubProtocolWebSocketHandler closes sessions with status 1001 Going Away). Clients should treat 1001/1012 as "reconnect soon".
  4. terminationGracePeriodSeconds long enough for in-flight work.
  5. Roll slowly (maxUnavailable small) so the remaining pods can absorb reconnects; scale up before deploying if needed.
  6. Resync after reconnect (REST fetch "since last event id") so nobody misses updates during the switch.
  7. Version compatibility: during rolling deploys old and new servers run side by side. Keep message formats backward compatible (add fields, don't rename/remove; version your payloads).

Spring Boot: server.shutdown=graceful waits for active HTTP requests; WebSocket sessions are closed when the WebSocket infrastructure stops. Test your shutdown sequence explicitly.

7.11 Managed real-time services (when not to build it yourself)

Service STOMP? Notes
Amazon MQ (ActiveMQ/RabbitMQ) Yes (broker) Managed broker for the Spring relay
AWS API Gateway WebSocket No Custom JSON; Lambda-based; connection IDs
Azure Web PubSub / SignalR Service No STOMP Own protocols (and MQTT for Web PubSub)
Pusher, Ably, PubNub No SaaS pub/sub with SDKs, presence, history
CloudAMQP Yes (RabbitMQ) Managed RabbitMQ with STOMP plugin

Choose managed services when real-time isn't your core competency and the cost/lock-in is acceptable.

7.12 Chapter summary

  • Simple broker = in-memory, single-node, partial STOMP. Relay = external broker, cluster-ready, full STOMP, one TCP connection per client to the broker.
  • For multi-instance user destinations: userDestinationBroadcast + userRegistryBroadcast.
  • RabbitMQ: /exchange, /queue, /amq/queue, /topic (amq.topic, */#, dot-separated), /temp-queue; watch for per-session queue leaks.
  • Backplane alternative: simple broker on each node + Redis/Kafka fan-out.
  • Proxies must forward Upgrade/Connection and have long timeouts. Sticky sessions only for SockJS fallbacks.
  • Plan capacity on connections, memory, fan-out; deploy with draining + jittered reconnect + resync.

Next: Chapter 8: Security

Chapter 8Production

Security

Goal: make a STOMP/WebSocket system that can't be hijacked, eavesdropped, abused, or used to read other users' data, and explain every attack and defence in interviews.

8.1 ELI5

Your post office needs:

  1. Locked trucks so nobody can read letters on the road → TLS (wss://).
  2. A guard at the door who checks ID cards → authentication.
  3. Rules about mailboxes: "You can read your mailbox, not your neighbour's." → authorisation of SUBSCRIBE and SEND.
  4. A check that the visitor really came from your own website, not a trick website → Origin check (CSWSH protection).
  5. Limits so one naughty kid can't stuff a million letters in the slot → rate limits, size limits, connection limits.
  6. Not trusting what's written in letters → input validation, output encoding.

8.2 Threat model (memorise this list)

# Threat Example Defence
1 Eavesdropping / tampering Wi-Fi attacker reads chat wss:// only, HSTS, modern TLS
2 Cross-Site WebSocket Hijacking (CSWSH) evil.com opens a socket to your server; the browser attaches the victim's cookie Check Origin; don't rely on cookies alone (token in CONNECT); SameSite cookies; Spring Security CSRF on CONNECT
3 Missing authentication Anyone connects and subscribes Authenticate at handshake or CONNECT; reject unauthenticated frames
4 Broken authorisation on SUBSCRIBE User subscribes to /topic/orders.123 that belongs to someone else (IDOR) Authorise every SUBSCRIBE by destination + ownership
5 Broken authorisation on SEND User sends directly to /topic/admin.broadcast, bypassing your controller Deny client SEND to broker prefixes; only allow /app/**
6 Wildcard subscriptions Subscribe /topic/** or /topic/orders.# (RabbitMQ) to see everything Deny pattern subscriptions; exact-match rules
7 Token leakage Token in URL query → access logs, proxy logs Tokens in CONNECT headers; short-lived one-time tickets if in URL; redact logs
8 Stale auth Token expires/revoked but socket stays open for days Enforce expiry on each message; server-side session termination; max connection age
9 Denial of service Connection floods, huge frames, subscription floods, message floods, slow readers Limits at LB and app: connections/IP, messageSizeLimit, rate limits, sendBufferSizeLimit, timeToFirstMessage
10 Injection XSS via chat message rendered as HTML; SpEL injection (CVE-2018-1270) Encode output; validate input; patch Spring; disable unused selector header
11 Information leakage Stack traces in ERROR frames; user enumeration via presence topics Generic error messages; authorise presence data
12 Message spoofing Client sets from: "admin" in payload Never trust identity fields from the client; take identity from Principal
13 Broker exposure RabbitMQ Web STOMP open to internet with guest Keep broker private; dedicated creds; TLS to broker
14 Compression side channels Secrets + attacker data compressed together (CRIME/BREACH style) Avoid compressing secrets with attacker-controlled data

8.3 Transport security: wss://

  • Always use wss:// in production (TLS). Pages on HTTPS must use wss:// (mixed content).
  • TLS also improves connectivity (proxies can't inspect/break the upgrade inside a TLS tunnel).
  • Terminate TLS at the LB or at the app; if terminated at the LB, the LB→app hop should be inside a trusted network or re-encrypted (zero-trust environments).
  • Use TLS for the relay → broker connection too (.secure() on the Reactor Netty client; RabbitMQ STOMP TLS port).

8.4 Cross-Site WebSocket Hijacking (CSWSH): the #1 WebSocket-specific attack

8.4.1 How it works

  1. Victim is logged in to app.example.com (session cookie).
  2. Victim visits evil.com.
  3. evil.com runs new WebSocket('wss://app.example.com/ws').
  4. CORS does not apply to WebSockets. The browser happily performs the handshake and attaches the victim's cookies (depending on SameSite).
  5. If the server authenticates by cookie alone and doesn't check Origin, the attacker's page now has an authenticated socket: it can read the victim's private messages and send messages as the victim.

8.4.2 Defences (use several)

  1. Validate Origin at the handshake against an allow-list. In Spring: setAllowedOrigins(...)/setAllowedOriginPatterns(...) (default = same origin only). Never use setAllowedOriginPatterns("*") in production with cookie auth.
  2. Don't authenticate by cookie alone: require a token in the STOMP CONNECT header (the attacker's page doesn't know it).
  3. SameSite cookies (Lax/Strict) reduce cookie sending on cross-site requests. A WebSocket handshake from evil.com to your site is a cross-site request, so SameSite=Lax/Strict cookies are generally not sent. But be careful: "same-site" ≠ "same-origin" (a compromised sub.example.com is same-site).
  4. Spring Security CSRF on CONNECT: @EnableWebSocketSecurity requires a CSRF token in the CONNECT frame by default.

Origin can be forged by non-browser clients (curl, scripts). That's fine: CSWSH is about abusing a victim's browser, which always sends the real Origin. Non-browser clients must still authenticate with credentials they actually own.

8.5 Authentication options

Option Where When to use
A. Authenticate the HTTP handshake Spring Security filter chain on /ws (session cookie, or token in query/cookie) Classic web apps with server sessions; the Principal from the HTTP request becomes the WebSocket user automatically
B. Authenticate the STOMP CONNECT frame ChannelInterceptor reads Authorization native header SPAs/mobile with JWT/OAuth2 access tokens; most common today
C. Ticket in query string Handshake When a proxy/gateway must authenticate before upgrade; use short-lived, one-time tickets
D. mTLS TLS layer Service-to-service

Spring's docs say it plainly: for token-based auth, authenticate on the STOMP CONNECT frame because browsers can't send headers on the handshake.

8.5.1 Option A: handshake auth with Spring Security (cookie session)

java
@Bean
SecurityFilterChain http(HttpSecurity http) throws Exception {
    http.authorizeHttpRequests(a -> a
            .requestMatchers("/ws/**").authenticated()      // handshake requires a logged-in session
            .anyRequest().authenticated())
        .formLogin(Customizer.withDefaults());
    return http.build();
}

The authenticated Principal of the HTTP request is attached to the WebSocket session and to every STOMP message (simpUser). Combine with Origin checks + CSRF on CONNECT.

8.5.2 Option B: JWT in the STOMP CONNECT frame (recommended for SPAs/mobile)

java
@Configuration
@Order(Ordered.HIGHEST_PRECEDENCE + 99)                 // run BEFORE Spring Security's message interceptors
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class WebSocketAuthConfig implements WebSocketMessageBrokerConfigurer {

    private final JwtDecoder jwtDecoder;                 // from spring-security-oauth2-resource-server
    private final JwtAuthenticationConverter jwtAuthConverter = new JwtAuthenticationConverter();

    @Override
    public void configureClientInboundChannel(ChannelRegistration registration) {
        registration.interceptors(new ChannelInterceptor() {
            @Override
            public Message<?> preSend(Message<?> message, MessageChannel channel) {
                StompHeaderAccessor acc = MessageHeaderAccessor.getAccessor(message, StompHeaderAccessor.class);
                if (acc != null && StompCommand.CONNECT.equals(acc.getCommand())) {
                    String header = acc.getFirstNativeHeader("Authorization");
                    if (header == null || !header.startsWith("Bearer ")) {
                        throw new BadCredentialsException("Missing bearer token");
                    }
                    try {
                        Jwt jwt = jwtDecoder.decode(header.substring(7));          // verifies signature, exp, nbf, iss (if configured)
                        AbstractAuthenticationToken auth = jwtAuthConverter.convert(jwt);
                        acc.setUser(auth);                                         // becomes Principal for this session
                        acc.getSessionAttributes().put("tokenExp", jwt.getExpiresAt());
                    } catch (JwtException e) {
                        throw new BadCredentialsException("Invalid token", e);     // → STOMP ERROR, connection closed
                    }
                }
                return message;
            }
        });
    }
}

Important details:

  • @Order(Ordered.HIGHEST_PRECEDENCE + 99): Spring's docs recommend this so your interceptor is registered before Spring Security's authorization interceptor; otherwise the CONNECT is rejected as unauthenticated before you set the user.
  • Use MessageHeaderAccessor.getAccessor(...) (mutable accessor), not StompHeaderAccessor.wrap(...) (copy).
  • You only need to set the user on CONNECT. Spring remembers it for the session (StompSubProtocolHandler stores it) and attaches it to later messages.
  • The thrown exception becomes an ERROR frame (customise via StompSubProtocolErrorHandler, Chapter 5) and the socket closes. The client shouldn't blindly retry with the same token.
  • With the broker relay, the broker still sees clientLogin credentials, not the user's. Your Spring layer is the security boundary.

Client:

ts
new Client({ brokerURL: 'wss://api.example.com/ws',
             beforeConnect: async (c) => { c.connectHeaders = { Authorization: `Bearer ${await token()}` }; } });

8.5.3 Option C: one-time ticket

  1. Client calls POST /api/ws-ticket with its normal Authorization header → server stores ticket → userId in Redis for 30 s.
  2. Client connects wss://api.example.com/ws?ticket=abc.
  3. A HandshakeInterceptor/DefaultHandshakeHandler#determineUser consumes (deletes) the ticket and assigns the Principal. Logs only ever contain a dead, single-use ticket.

8.6 Authorisation of messages with Spring Security

8.6.1 Modern configuration (@EnableWebSocketSecurity, Spring Security 5.8+/6+)

java
@Configuration
@EnableWebSocketSecurity
public class WebSocketSecurityConfig {

    @Bean
    AuthorizationManager<Message<?>> messageAuthorizationManager(
            MessageMatcherDelegatingAuthorizationManager.Builder messages) {
        messages
            .nullDestMatcher().authenticated()                                   // CONNECT, DISCONNECT, HEARTBEAT, UNSUBSCRIBE...
            .simpSubscribeDestMatchers("/user/queue/errors").permitAll()
            .simpDestMatchers("/app/**").hasRole("USER")                          // client SENDs only to controllers
            .simpSubscribeDestMatchers("/user/**", "/topic/lists.*").hasRole("USER")
            .simpTypeMatchers(SimpMessageType.MESSAGE, SimpMessageType.SUBSCRIBE).denyAll() // everything else: no
            .anyMessage().denyAll();
        return messages.build();
    }
}

What this does:

  • Messages without a destination (CONNECT, etc.) need an authenticated user.
  • Clients may only SEND to /app/**, so they cannot SEND directly to /topic/** to impersonate the server.
  • Subscriptions only to allowed patterns.
  • Deny by default (anyMessage().denyAll()): new destinations are blocked until someone thinks about them.

Legacy configuration (AbstractSecurityWebSocketMessageBrokerConfigurer, configureInbound(MessageSecurityMetadataSourceRegistry)) was deprecated in 5.8/6.x and is removed in Spring Security 7. Migrate to @EnableWebSocketSecurity + AuthorizationManager. If you still have SpEL-based rules, Spring Security offers MessageExpressionAuthorizationManager to ease migration.

Note from the Spring Security reference: Spring Security secures only the clientInboundChannel, not the outbound channel (for performance: one inbound message can fan out to many outbound ones). Secure subscriptions, not outgoing messages.

8.6.2 CSRF and @EnableWebSocketSecurity

  • By default, @EnableWebSocketSecurity requires a CSRF token in the CONNECT frame headers (because browser cookie-authenticated sessions are vulnerable to CSWSH). The client fetches the token (e.g., from a /csrf endpoint or a meta tag) and adds it to connectHeaders using the header name from the CsrfToken (commonly X-CSRF-TOKEN / X-XSRF-TOKEN). In Spring Security 6 the expected value is the masked (XOR-encoded) token form by default. Send the value your server's CsrfToken provides.
  • If you use only header tokens (JWT in CONNECT) and no cookies, CSRF provides little value. The current Spring Security reference states: "At this point, CSRF is not configurable when using @EnableWebSocketSecurity, though this will likely be added in a future release." Spring Security's docs show the alternative: don't use the annotation, and register the components yourself:
java
@Configuration
@Order(Ordered.HIGHEST_PRECEDENCE + 100)          // after your JWT CONNECT interceptor (+99)
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class WebSocketSecurityNoCsrfConfig implements WebSocketMessageBrokerConfigurer {
    private final ApplicationContext context;
    private final AuthorizationManager<Message<?>> authorizationManager;   // the bean built above

    @Override
    public void addArgumentResolvers(List<HandlerMethodArgumentResolver> resolvers) {
        resolvers.add(new AuthenticationPrincipalArgumentResolver());       // enables @AuthenticationPrincipal
    }

    @Override
    public void configureClientInboundChannel(ChannelRegistration registration) {
        AuthorizationChannelInterceptor authz = new AuthorizationChannelInterceptor(authorizationManager);
        authz.setAuthorizationEventPublisher(new SpringAuthorizationEventPublisher(context));
        registration.interceptors(new SecurityContextChannelInterceptor(), authz);   // no CSRF interceptor
    }
}

(Verify against the Spring Security version you use; this area has evolved across 5.8 → 6.x → 7.x.)

8.6.3 Fine-grained (ownership) authorisation on SUBSCRIBE

Role checks aren't enough: "is user anna a member of list L1?" requires data. Do it in an interceptor:

java
@Component
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class SubscriptionGuard implements ChannelInterceptor {
    private static final Pattern LIST_TOPIC = Pattern.compile("^/topic/lists\\.([A-Za-z0-9-]{1,64})$");
    private final ListAccessService access;

    @Override
    public Message<?> preSend(Message<?> message, MessageChannel channel) {
        StompHeaderAccessor acc = MessageHeaderAccessor.getAccessor(message, StompHeaderAccessor.class);
        if (acc == null || !StompCommand.SUBSCRIBE.equals(acc.getCommand())) return message;

        String dest = acc.getDestination();
        Principal user = acc.getUser();
        if (user == null || dest == null) throw new AccessDeniedException("Unauthenticated");

        Matcher m = LIST_TOPIC.matcher(dest);
        if (m.matches()) {
            if (!access.isMember(user.getName(), m.group(1))) {
                throw new AccessDeniedException("Not a member of list");   // → ERROR + close
            }
            return message;
        }
        if (dest.startsWith("/user/queue/") || dest.equals("/topic/announcements")) return message;
        throw new AccessDeniedException("Unknown destination");        // deny by default
    }
}

Register it in configureClientInboundChannel (after the auth interceptor).

Alternative: soft rejection. Throwing produces an ERROR and kills the whole connection. Sometimes you want to reject only the subscription: return null from preSend to silently drop the SUBSCRIBE and send an error to /user/queue/errors via SimpMessagingTemplate. Choose consciously; silent drops can confuse clients.

Note on performance: the check runs once per SUBSCRIBE (not per message), so a DB/cache lookup is fine. But membership can change later: when a user is removed from a list, you must also terminate their existing subscription. Either close their sessions, or have the server-side logic filter (e.g., send list events only via user destinations to current members). Revocation is a commonly missed requirement.

8.6.4 Authorising SENDs

Put business authorisation in the controller/service (access.requireMember(...)), just like REST. Interceptor rules handle coarse checks; services handle data-level checks. @PreAuthorize works on @MessageMapping methods when method security is enabled (@EnableMethodSecurity), since the SecurityContext is populated for message handling by Spring Security's SecurityContextChannelInterceptor.

8.6.5 Never trust client-provided identity

java
// WRONG
@MessageMapping("/chat.send") @SendTo("/topic/chat")
public ChatMessage send(ChatMessage m) { return m; }          // client can set m.sender = "ceo"

// RIGHT
public ChatMessage send(ChatMessage m, Principal p) { return m.withSender(p.getName()); }

8.7 Token expiry and revocation while connected

A JWT is checked at CONNECT. The socket may live for days. Strategies:

  1. Check expiry on every inbound message (cheap):
java
@Override
public Message<?> preSend(Message<?> message, MessageChannel channel) {
    SimpMessageHeaderAccessor acc = MessageHeaderAccessor.getAccessor(message, SimpMessageHeaderAccessor.class);
    if (acc != null && acc.getSessionAttributes() != null) {
        Instant exp = (Instant) acc.getSessionAttributes().get("tokenExp");
        if (exp != null && Instant.now().isAfter(exp)) throw new CredentialsExpiredException("Token expired");
    }
    return message;
}

This stops expired clients from sending, but they still receive subscribed messages.

  1. Server-initiated close of expired/revoked sessions. Keep references to WebSocketSessions and close them:
java
@Component
public class SessionRegistry implements WebSocketHandlerDecoratorFactory {
    private final Map<String, WebSocketSession> sessions = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
    @Override
    public WebSocketHandler decorate(WebSocketHandler handler) {
        return new WebSocketHandlerDecorator(handler) {
            @Override public void afterConnectionEstablished(WebSocketSession s) throws Exception {
                sessions.put(s.getId(), s); super.afterConnectionEstablished(s);
            }
            @Override public void afterConnectionClosed(WebSocketSession s, CloseStatus st) throws Exception {
                sessions.remove(s.getId()); super.afterConnectionClosed(s, st);
            }
        };
    }
    public void close(String sessionId, CloseStatus status) throws IOException {
        WebSocketSession s = sessions.get(sessionId);
        if (s != null && s.isOpen()) s.close(status);
    }
}
// register: registration.addDecoratorFactory(sessionRegistry) in configureWebSocketTransport

A scheduled job closes sessions whose tokenExp passed, or listens to a "user revoked/logged out" event and closes all their sessions (new CloseStatus(4001, "token expired")). The client refreshes the token in beforeConnect and reconnects. (The WebSocket session id equals the STOMP simpSessionId.)

  1. Max connection age (e.g., 1–12 hours) forces periodic re-authentication.

  2. Refresh-in-band (advanced): client sends a new token via a /app/auth.refresh message; server validates and updates the session's expiry.

8.8 Denial-of-service protections

Attack Control
Too many connections from one IP/user LB/WAF connection limits; per-user session cap in a CONNECT interceptor (simpUserRegistry.getUser(name).getSessions().size())
Connect but never send CONNECT setTimeToFirstMessage (default 60 s; lower it, e.g., 10 s)
Huge frames setMessageSizeLimit; container buffer sizes; LB body limits
Message floods Per-session rate limiting (token bucket) in an inbound interceptor; close abusive sessions
Subscription floods Cap subscriptions per session; deny wildcard destinations
Slow readers (memory blow-up) setSendBufferSizeLimit, setSendTimeLimit (sessions get closed)
Expensive handlers Bounded inbound thread pool + queue; timeouts in handlers; offload heavy work
Reconnect storms Client jitter/backoff; server-side 429-like close code (1013 Try Again Later) with longer retry

Rate limit example (per session, simple token bucket):

java
public class RateLimitInterceptor implements ChannelInterceptor {
    private final Map<String, Bucket> buckets = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();   // Bucket4j, or roll your own
    @Override
    public Message<?> preSend(Message<?> message, MessageChannel channel) {
        StompHeaderAccessor acc = MessageHeaderAccessor.getAccessor(message, StompHeaderAccessor.class);
        if (acc == null || acc.getCommand() != StompCommand.SEND) return message;
        Bucket b = buckets.computeIfAbsent(acc.getSessionId(),
                id -> Bucket.builder().addLimit(Bandwidth.simple(20, Duration.ofSeconds(1))).build());
        if (!b.tryConsume(1)) throw new MessageDeliveryException("Rate limit exceeded");
        return message;
    }
    // remove bucket on SessionDisconnectEvent to avoid memory leaks
}

8.9 Input validation and output encoding

  • Validate payloads (@Valid), lengths, enums, IDs; reject unknown fields if strict.
  • Treat message content like any untrusted input: render as text in the UI (textContent, React's default escaping); never innerHTML/dangerouslySetInnerHTML without sanitising (DOMPurify).
  • Validate destination variables (@DestinationVariable) with regexes; they come from the client.
  • Avoid deserialising polymorphic types from client JSON (Jackson default typing → RCE gadgets).
  • Keep Spring updated. Known STOMP-related CVEs: CVE-2018-1270 and CVE-2018-1275 (SpEL injection via the simple broker selector header; 1275 covered an incomplete fix for 4.3.x). Upgrading Spring fixed them; disabling the selector header removes the surface entirely.

8.10 Logging and privacy

  • Never log CONNECT headers raw (tokens, passwords). Redact Authorization, passcode, cookies.
  • Turn off debug in stompjs in production (it can print frames).
  • Don't log full message bodies at INFO (PII). Log ids, sizes, destinations.
  • For audit trails (financial/healthcare), log who sent what to which destination when, with correlation IDs, to a secure store.
  • Multi-tenant: include tenantId in every destination and authorise it; never let tenant come from the payload.

8.11 Security checklist (copy into your PR template)

  • wss:// everywhere; HSTS on the domain.
  • Allowed origins = explicit allow-list (no *).
  • Authentication at handshake or CONNECT; unauthenticated frames rejected.
  • @Order(HIGHEST_PRECEDENCE + 99) on the CONNECT auth interceptor.
  • Client SEND allowed only to /app/**; SUBSCRIBE only to allow-listed destinations; deny by default.
  • Ownership checks for parameterised topics (/topic/orders.{id}); handles revocation.
  • No wildcard subscriptions from clients.
  • Identity from Principal, never payload.
  • Token expiry enforced (per-message check + server-side close + max connection age).
  • Size limits, rate limits, connection caps, timeToFirstMessage.
  • Generic ERROR messages; no stack traces.
  • Logs redacted; stompjs debug off.
  • Broker not internet-exposed; dedicated creds; TLS to broker.
  • Dependencies patched (Spring, Tomcat, Netty, stompjs).
  • Pen-test WebSocket endpoints (Burp/ZAP support WebSocket message tampering).

Next: Chapter 9: Reliability, Performance & Tuning

Chapter 9Production

Reliability, Performance & Tuning

Goal: make real-time messaging that doesn't lose important data, doesn't fall over under load, and degrades gracefully. This is what separates "it works on my laptop" from senior-level production work.

9.1 ELI5

  • Reliability = letters don't get lost, don't arrive twice in a harmful way, and arrive in the right order when order matters.
  • Performance = the post office handles lots of letters quickly without its sorting room overflowing.
  • Resilience = when a truck breaks down, letters still get delivered later, and everybody knows what happened.

9.2 Delivery guarantees: what STOMP over WebSocket really gives you

Guarantee Meaning Where you get it
At-most-once Delivered 0 or 1 times; may be lost Default: simple broker, ack:auto, disconnected clients miss messages
At-least-once Delivered ≥1 times; duplicates possible Full broker + durable queue + ack:client(-individual) + redelivery; plus client-side dedupe
Exactly-once (effectively-once) Effect applied once Not a transport property. At-least-once + idempotent processing (dedupe by message id)

Truth bomb for interviews: With Spring's simple broker, if a client is disconnected for 3 seconds (tunnel, deploy, Wi-Fi switch), every message published in those 3 seconds is lost for that client. TCP's reliability only covers bytes on a live connection. Design for it:

9.2.1 The "WebSocket as notification, database as truth" pattern (most important pattern in this book)

  1. All state lives in a database (source of truth).
  2. Every change gets a monotonic version/sequence number or event id.
  3. WebSocket pushes events (or just "something changed, version N").
  4. Client tracks the last version it applied.
  5. On (re)connect or gap detected (received N+2 after N), the client fetches missed changes via REST (GET /lists/L1/events?after=N) or a full snapshot.
ts
let lastSeq = 0;
function onEvent(e: { seq: number; ... }) {
  if (e.seq <= lastSeq) return;                 // duplicate or stale → ignore (idempotent)
  if (e.seq > lastSeq + 1) return resync();     // gap → fetch missing
  apply(e); lastSeq = e.seq;
}
client.onConnect = () => { subscribeAll(); resync(); };   // after reconnect always resync

9.2.2 Subscribe-then-snapshot (avoid the race)

If you fetch the snapshot first and subscribe second, changes between the two are lost. Correct order:

  1. Subscribe to the live topic; buffer incoming events.
  2. Fetch snapshot (with its version V).
  3. Apply buffered events with seq > V, drop the rest.
  4. Continue live.

9.2.3 Server-side: the transactional outbox

If your service writes to the DB and then publishes to the broker, a crash between the two loses the event (or the reverse publishes a lie). Outbox pattern: write the event into an outbox table in the same DB transaction, and a relay process (polling or CDC like Debezium) publishes it to the broker/WebSocket layer and marks it sent. Gives at-least-once publish; consumers dedupe by event id.

Simpler alternative for small apps: @TransactionalEventListener(phase = AFTER_COMMIT) (no lie on rollback, but an event can be lost if the process crashes right after commit; the REST resync covers that).

9.2.4 Client → server reliability

  • Client sends a command with a clientRequestId (UUID).
  • Server processes idempotently (unique constraint on request id, or a processed-ids cache with TTL).
  • Server replies (to /user/queue/acks or via the resulting event containing clientRequestId).
  • Client retries if no confirmation within a timeout (or after reconnect). Duplicates are harmless.
  • STOMP receipt confirms the broker/server received the frame, not that your business logic succeeded.

9.2.5 Durable delivery to offline users

WebSocket is for online users. For offline users:

  • Store notifications in DB (inbox table), show on next login.
  • Send push notifications (FCM/APNs) or email for important ones.
  • With RabbitMQ you can use durable per-user queues, but per-user durable queues for millions of users are an operational burden. The DB inbox is usually better.

9.3 Ordering

Layer Ordering behaviour
TCP / one WebSocket connection Ordered
Spring inbound channel (thread pool) Not ordered by default across messages of one session → setPreserveReceiveOrder(true) (6.1+)
Spring outbound channel Not ordered by default → setPreservePublishOrder(true) (5.1+)
Simple broker fan-out Per subscriber, follows outbound ordering setting
RabbitMQ single queue, single consumer Ordered (redeliveries can reorder)
Multiple publishers / instances No global order. Use per-entity sequence numbers
Kafka backplane Ordered per partition (partition by entity id)

Rules of thumb:

  • Most apps need per-entity ordering (per chat room, per document), not global.
  • Put a sequence number per entity in each event, generated where the state changes (DB row version).
  • Clients drop stale versions (last-writer-wins by version).

9.4 Heart-beats and timeouts: tuning table

Setting Recommended starting point Why
STOMP heart-beat (both ways) 10 s – 25 s Below every idle timeout in the path; fast dead-peer detection
Proxy/LB idle timeout ≥ 2–3 × heart-beat (e.g., 60–3600 s) Never kill healthy connections
Client connectionTimeout 5–10 s Don't hang forever on half-open connects
Client reconnect delay 1 s → backoff to 30 s, with jitter Avoid thundering herds
Spring timeToFirstMessage 10–30 s Kill sockets that never CONNECT
Spring sendTimeLimit 10–20 s Kill stuck writes to dead clients
Spring sendBufferSizeLimit 512 KB – few MB Bound memory for slow clients
Mobile 10–20 s heart-beats Networks change often; NAT timeouts are short

Trade-off: shorter heart-beats → faster detection but more traffic/battery (each beat is 1 byte of payload but a packet and radio wake-up on mobile). 100k connections × 1 beat/10 s each way = 20k tiny packets/s. Fine for servers, measurable for phones.

9.5 Slow consumers and backpressure

9.5.1 What happens with a slow client in Spring

  • Messages to a session go through ConcurrentWebSocketSessionDecorator. Only one thread sends to a session at a time; others buffer messages in a per-session queue.
  • If buffered bytes exceed sendBufferSizeLimit or a send takes longer than sendTimeLimit, the session is closed (Spring's CloseStatus.SESSION_NOT_RELIABLE, 4500), and stats count it under "send limit".
  • Without these limits, one slow client on a bad network could make the server buffer unbounded data → OutOfMemoryError.

9.5.2 Strategies

  1. Bound buffers (above), so the slow client gets disconnected and resyncs on reconnect.
  2. Conflation/coalescing: for state that changes frequently (prices, positions, cursors), send only the latest value per key every X ms. A slow client doesn't need 50 stale prices.
  3. Batching: send arrays of events every 50–250 ms instead of one frame per event (fewer frames, less overhead).
  4. Deltas: send changes, not full objects.
  5. Smaller audiences: shard topics (per room, per symbol), subscribe only to what's visible on screen.
  6. Prioritise: separate critical messages (orders) from high-volume ones (market data); maybe separate connections/endpoints.

Coalescing example (server side, per destination, latest-wins):

java
@Component
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class PriceConflator {
    private final SimpMessagingTemplate messaging;
    private final ConcurrentHashMap<String, Price> latest = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();

    public void onTick(Price p) { latest.put(p.symbol(), p); }         // called thousands of times/s

    @Scheduled(fixedRate = 200)                                        // 5 updates/s per symbol max
    public void flush() {
        for (String symbol : latest.keySet()) {
            Price p = latest.remove(symbol);
            if (p != null) messaging.convertAndSend("/topic/prices." + symbol, p);
        }
    }
}

9.6 Thread pools and blocking

  • clientInboundChannel threads run your @MessageMapping methods. Blocking (slow DB, HTTP calls) in handlers occupies these threads; when all are busy, messages queue (default queue capacity is unbounded) → latency grows, memory grows.
  • Default core size = 2 × CPUs. For I/O-bound handlers, either increase the pool (e.g., 50–200) or offload to another executor and reply asynchronously (return CompletableFuture).
  • Set a bounded queue + rejection policy for back-pressure on inbound traffic only if you have a plan for rejected messages (clients must retry).
  • clientOutboundChannel mostly writes to sockets. Thread count rarely needs to be high unless clients are slow (limits handle that).
  • Java 21 virtual threads: since Spring Framework 6.2, ChannelRegistration.executor(Executor) lets you plug any Executor into a channel (it takes precedence over taskExecutor() settings). Example:
    java
    @Override
public void configureClientInboundChannel(ChannelRegistration r) {
    r.executor(new VirtualThreadTaskExecutor("ws-in-"));   // org.springframework.core.task.VirtualThreadTaskExecutor
}
    Load test before adopting: virtual threads help handlers that block on I/O, but they don't fix slow clients or fan-out costs. Keep setPreservePublishOrder/setPreserveReceiveOrder in mind, since ordering is still not guaranteed across threads.
  • Watch WebSocketMessageBrokerStats (queued tasks) or custom metrics on executor queue sizes.
java
@Override
public void configureClientInboundChannel(ChannelRegistration r) {
    r.taskExecutor().corePoolSize(32).maxPoolSize(64).keepAliveSeconds(60).queueCapacity(50_000);
}

Note: with ThreadPoolTaskExecutor, maxPoolSize only takes effect when the queue is full. With an unbounded queue, the pool never grows beyond corePoolSize. A very common misconfiguration.

9.7 Payload size and format

  • JSON is fine for most apps. Optimise only with data:
    • Short field names for high-frequency messages; omit nulls (spring.jackson.default-property-inclusion=non_null).
    • Numbers instead of strings; epoch millis instead of ISO strings for hot paths.
  • Binary formats (Protocol Buffers, MessagePack, CBOR, FlatBuffers) shrink payloads 30–80% and parse faster; send as binary WebSocket frames with STOMP content-type: application/x-protobuf and a custom MessageConverter.
  • Compression (permessage-deflate): big wins for verbose JSON, costs CPU and memory per connection. Measure.
  • Keep messages small (< a few KB). For big content, send an id/URL and fetch over HTTP (CDN-cacheable, resumable).

9.8 Observability: what to measure

9.8.1 Metrics (Micrometer → Prometheus/Grafana/Datadog)

Metric Why
Active WebSocket sessions (per instance, per transport) Capacity, imbalance
Connects/s, disconnects/s, by close code Reconnect storms, auth failures, proxy timeouts (1006)
STOMP CONNECT failures (auth) Token problems, attacks
Inbound messages/s, outbound messages/s Throughput, fan-out
Message size histogram Detect oversized payloads
Inbound/outbound executor active threads + queue size Saturation
Sessions closed due to send limits Slow clients / network problems
Handler latency (@MessageMapping timing) Slow business logic
End-to-end latency (publish ts → client receive ts, sampled) User-visible lag
Broker: connections, queue depth, unacked, memory alarms Broker health
BrokerAvailabilityEvent state Relay connectivity
Subscriptions count per destination (top N) Hot topics

Session gauge example:

java
@Component
public class WsMetrics {
    private final AtomicInteger sessions = new AtomicInteger();
    public WsMetrics(MeterRegistry registry, SimpUserRegistry users) {
        Gauge.builder("ws.sessions.active", sessions, AtomicInteger::get).register(registry);
        Gauge.builder("ws.users.connected", users, SimpUserRegistry::getUserCount).register(registry);
    }
    @EventListener void on(SessionConnectedEvent e) { sessions.incrementAndGet(); }
    @EventListener void on(SessionDisconnectEvent e) { /* guard against double events with a Set<sessionId> */ sessions.decrementAndGet(); }
}

(Because SessionDisconnectEvent can fire twice, track session ids in a concurrent set and decrement only when removal succeeds.)

Executor metrics:

java
@Bean
public MeterBinder wsExecutorMetrics(@Qualifier("clientInboundChannelExecutor") ThreadPoolTaskExecutor in,
                                     @Qualifier("clientOutboundChannelExecutor") ThreadPoolTaskExecutor out) {
    return registry -> {
        new ExecutorServiceMetrics(in.getThreadPoolExecutor(), "ws.inbound", List.of()).bindTo(registry);
        new ExecutorServiceMetrics(out.getThreadPoolExecutor(), "ws.outbound", List.of()).bindTo(registry);
    };
}

(Bean names clientInboundChannelExecutor / clientOutboundChannelExecutor / brokerChannelExecutor are Spring's defaults, and by default they are ThreadPoolTaskExecutors. If you plugged in a custom executor, adapt the binding.)

9.8.2 Logging

  • Log connect/disconnect with session id, user id, close code, duration, client version, IP (respect privacy rules).
  • Use MDC with session id/user id in an ExecutorChannelInterceptor (beforeHandle → put, afterMessageHandled → clear) so handler logs are traceable.
  • Spring debug logging for troubleshooting: logging.level.org.springframework.web.socket=DEBUG, logging.level.org.springframework.messaging=DEBUG (very verbose; never leave on in prod).

9.8.3 Tracing

  • Propagate a trace/correlation id in STOMP headers (traceparent style) from client → server → broker → consumers. Create spans manually around handlers (Micrometer Observation API) since auto-instrumentation for STOMP is limited.

9.8.4 Alerts

  • Session count drops > 30% in 1 min (mass disconnect).
  • Connect rate spikes (reconnect storm).
  • Close code 1006 rate increase (network/proxy issue).
  • Inbound queue size > threshold for 5 min.
  • Broker unavailable.
  • Send-limit closes increasing.

9.9 Load testing

Tool STOMP support
Gatling WebSocket support; write STOMP frames as text ("CONNECT\naccept-version:1.2\n\n\u0000"), check responses
k6 k6/ws and the newer k6/websockets (browser-like API) modules; send raw STOMP frames
Artillery WebSocket engine; raw frames
JMeter WebSocket Samplers plugin; raw frames
Custom Java/Kotlin WebSocketStompClient/Krossbow to simulate realistic clients

k6 sketch:

js
import ws from 'k6/ws';
import { check } from 'k6';
export const options = { vus: 1000, duration: '5m' };
const NUL = '\u0000';
export default function () {
  const res = ws.connect('wss://staging.example.com/ws', { headers: { 'Sec-WebSocket-Protocol': 'v12.stomp' } }, (socket) => {
    socket.on('open', () => {
      socket.send(`CONNECT\naccept-version:1.2\nhost:staging\nheart-beat:10000,10000\nAuthorization:Bearer ${__ENV.TOKEN}\n\n${NUL}`);
    });
    socket.on('message', (m) => {
      if (m.startsWith('CONNECTED')) {
        socket.send(`SUBSCRIBE\nid:sub-0\ndestination:/topic/chat.public\n\n${NUL}`);
        socket.setInterval(() => socket.send(`SEND\ndestination:/app/chat.send\ncontent-type:application/json\n\n{"text":"hi"}${NUL}`), 5000);
      }
    });
    socket.setInterval(() => socket.send('\n'), 10000);   // client heart-beat
    socket.setTimeout(() => socket.close(), 290000);
  });
  check(res, { 'status 101': (r) => r && r.status === 101 });
}

Load-test lessons:

  • Load generators hit their own limits (ephemeral ports, fds, CPU). Use several machines/IPs.
  • Test realistic mixes: mostly idle connections + bursts; reconnect storms (kill a pod mid-test); slow clients (throttle network).
  • Measure end-to-end latency percentiles (p50/p95/p99), not just throughput.
  • Soak tests (hours) find memory leaks (subscriptions not cleaned, presence maps growing).

9.10 Graceful degradation

  • If WebSocket can't connect after N tries, fall back to polling for critical screens (or SockJS).
  • Show connection status in the UI ("Live", "Reconnecting…", "Offline, showing data from 10:42").
  • Queue user actions offline (outbox) and replay with idempotency keys.
  • Feature flags to turn off real-time features (e.g., typing indicators) under load while keeping core ones.
  • Server-side load shedding: reject new connections with close code 1013 (Try Again Later) when overloaded; clients back off longer.

9.11 Reliability checklist

  • Every event has an id/sequence; clients dedupe and detect gaps.
  • Clients resync via REST after reconnect.
  • Subscribe-then-snapshot ordering.
  • Commands carry idempotency keys; server dedupes.
  • Events published after commit (or via outbox).
  • Ordering requirements identified; per-entity sequence or preservePublishOrder.
  • Heart-beats < all idle timeouts; timeouts tuned.
  • Send buffer/time limits configured; conflation for high-frequency data.
  • Handlers non-blocking or pool sized; bounded queues considered.
  • Metrics + alerts on sessions, close codes, queues, broker.
  • Load, soak and chaos (pod kill, broker restart, network loss) tested.
  • Offline delivery via DB inbox/push for important notifications.

Next: Chapter 10: Testing, Debugging & the Bug Encyclopedia

Chapter 10Production

Testing, Debugging & the Bug Encyclopedia

Goal: test real-time features confidently, debug any problem methodically, and look up any symptom to find its cause and fix. Keep this chapter open during incidents.

10.1 ELI5

When the telephone doesn't work, you don't randomly shake it. You check, in order: Is it plugged in? Is there a dial tone? Did the other person pick up? Are you speaking the same language? Did they hear you? Debugging WebSocket/STOMP is the same: check layer by layer, from bottom to top.

10.2 Testing strategy (the pyramid)

Level What Tools
Unit Controller methods, services, interceptors, converters JUnit, Mockito
Slice/integration Full Spring context + real WebSocket + STOMP client @SpringBootTest(webEnvironment = RANDOM_PORT), WebSocketStompClient, Awaitility
Broker integration Relay with a real RabbitMQ Testcontainers
Front-end unit Hooks/components with a mocked STOMP client Jest/Vitest, mock client
End-to-end Two browsers see each other's updates Playwright/Cypress (two contexts)
Contract Message schemas stay compatible JSON Schema / AsyncAPI validation in CI
Load/soak/chaos Capacity, leaks, resilience k6, Gatling, pod-kill scripts

10.2.1 Unit testing a controller (no WebSocket at all)

java
class TodoWsControllerTest {
    TodoService todos = mock(TodoService.class);
    ListAccessService access = mock(ListAccessService.class);
    TodoWsController controller = new TodoWsController(todos, access);

    @Test
    void upsertChecksMembershipAndDelegates() {
        Principal anna = () -> "anna";
        TodoCommand cmd = new TodoCommand("L1", null, "Buy milk", false, null, "req-1");
        controller.upsert(cmd, anna);
        verify(access).requireMember("anna", "L1");
        verify(todos).upsert(cmd, "anna");
    }
}

10.2.2 Unit testing an interceptor (build STOMP messages by hand)

java
@Test
void rejectsSubscriptionToForeignList() {
    ListAccessService access = mock(ListAccessService.class);
    when(access.isMember("anna", "L2")).thenReturn(false);
    SubscriptionGuard guard = new SubscriptionGuard(access);

    StompHeaderAccessor acc = StompHeaderAccessor.create(StompCommand.SUBSCRIBE);
    acc.setDestination("/topic/lists.L2");
    acc.setSubscriptionId("sub-0");
    acc.setSessionId("s1");
    acc.setUser(() -> "anna");
    acc.setLeaveMutable(true);                                    // so getAccessor(...) works inside the interceptor
    Message<byte[]> msg = MessageBuilder.createMessage(new byte[0], acc.getMessageHeaders());

    assertThrows(AccessDeniedException.class, () -> guard.preSend(msg, mock(MessageChannel.class)));
}

10.2.3 Full integration test with a real STOMP client

java
@SpringBootTest(webEnvironment = SpringBootTest.WebEnvironment.RANDOM_PORT)
class ChatIntegrationTest {

    @LocalServerPort int port;
    @Autowired TestTokens tokens;            // helper that mints JWTs signed with the test key

    WebSocketStompClient stompClient;

    @BeforeEach
    void setup() {
        stompClient = new WebSocketStompClient(new StandardWebSocketClient());
        stompClient.setMessageConverter(new MappingJackson2MessageConverter()); // Spring 7: JacksonJsonMessageConverter
    }

    @AfterEach
    void tearDown() { stompClient.stop(); }

    private StompSession connect(String user) throws Exception {
        StompHeaders h = new StompHeaders();
        h.add("Authorization", "Bearer " + tokens.forUser(user));
        return stompClient.connectAsync("ws://localhost:" + port + "/ws", new WebSocketHttpHeaders(), h,
                new StompSessionHandlerAdapter() {}).get(5, TimeUnit.SECONDS);
    }

    @Test
    void messageIsBroadcastToRoom() throws Exception {
        BlockingQueue<ChatMessage> received = new LinkedBlockingQueue<>();
        StompSession bob = connect("bob");
        bob.subscribe("/topic/chat.public", new StompFrameHandler() {
            public Type getPayloadType(StompHeaders headers) { return ChatMessage.class; }
            public void handleFrame(StompHeaders headers, Object payload) { received.add((ChatMessage) payload); }
        });

        // Make sure the SUBSCRIBE has been processed before sending (avoid race): use a receipt
        // or a small await. Receipts need setTaskScheduler on the client and broker support.
        Thread.sleep(200);   // simplest; prefer receipts or polling a /app/ping round-trip

        StompSession anna = connect("anna");
        anna.send("/app/chat.send", new ChatMessage(null, "hello"));

        ChatMessage msg = received.poll(5, TimeUnit.SECONDS);
        assertThat(msg).isNotNull();
        assertThat(msg.sender()).isEqualTo("anna");      // server set identity, not client
        assertThat(msg.text()).isEqualTo("hello");
    }

    @Test
    void connectWithoutTokenFails() {
        assertThatThrownBy(() -> stompClient.connectAsync("ws://localhost:" + port + "/ws",
                new StompSessionHandlerAdapter() {}).get(5, TimeUnit.SECONDS))
            .isInstanceOf(Exception.class);
    }
}

Tips:

  • Race between SUBSCRIBE and SEND is the #1 cause of flaky WebSocket tests. Use receipts (session.setAutoReceipt(true) + subscribe(...).addReceiptTask(latch::countDown), where the server supports receipts), or a "ping" round-trip through the same session, or Awaitility polling of SimpUserRegistry subscriptions.
  • Use BlockingQueue.poll(timeout) or Awaitility, never fixed long sleeps.
  • Deterministic alternative used by the Appendix B sample: in tests, run the inbound channel with one thread (registration.taskExecutor().corePoolSize(1) behind a test property), so a session's frames are handled in arrival order. A round-trip @SubscribeMapping request then proves earlier SUBSCRIBEs are registered.
  • Test negative paths: forbidden subscriptions, invalid payloads → error queue, expired tokens.
  • Test user destinations with two sessions for the same user (both receive) and a different user (doesn't).

10.2.4 Broker relay tests with Testcontainers

java
@Testcontainers
@SpringBootTest(webEnvironment = RANDOM_PORT)
class RelayIT {
    @Container
    static RabbitMQContainer rabbit = new RabbitMQContainer("rabbitmq:3-management")
            .withPluginsEnabled("rabbitmq_stomp")      // API name may differ by Testcontainers version;
            .withExposedPorts(5672, 15672, 61613);     // alternatively execInContainer("rabbitmq-plugins","enable","rabbitmq_stomp")

    @DynamicPropertySource
    static void props(DynamicPropertyRegistry r) {
        r.add("broker.relay.host", rabbit::getHost);
        r.add("broker.relay.port", () -> rabbit.getMappedPort(61613));
    }
    // ... same client-based tests; plus: restart broker and assert BrokerAvailabilityEvent + recovery
}

10.2.5 Front-end tests

  • Wrap the STOMP client behind your own small interface (RealtimeClient { subscribe, publish, onState }) so components can be tested with a fake that you control (fake.emit('/topic/x', payload)).
  • For lower-level tests, libraries such as mock-socket can fake the browser WebSocket.
  • Playwright E2E: open two browser contexts logged in as different users; act in one and expect(page2.getByText('Buy milk')).toBeVisible() in the other. Also test offline: context.setOffline(true) → assert "Reconnecting…" → setOffline(false) → assert resync.

10.2.6 Contract tests for messages

  • Define payload schemas (JSON Schema or AsyncAPI document listing channels/destinations, messages, and payloads).
  • In CI: validate server-produced sample messages against the schema; generate TypeScript types for the client from the same schema; fail on breaking changes (removed/renamed fields).

10.3 The debugging method: layer by layer

 Layer 7c  App logic        Did my handler run? Was the right data sent? Did UI render it?
 Layer 7b  STOMP routing    Destination/prefix correct? Subscribed? Authorised? Converted?
 Layer 7a  STOMP session    CONNECT → CONNECTED? ERROR frame? Heart-beats negotiated?
 Layer 6   WebSocket        101 received? Close code? Frames flowing?
 Layer 5   HTTP/proxy       Upgrade headers forwarded? Timeouts? Status code?
 Layer 4   TLS/TCP/DNS      Resolves? Port open? Cert valid? Firewall?

Step-by-step:

  1. Browser DevTools → Network → WS. Is there a request to /ws? Status 101? If not, look at the status code (Chapter 2 §2.3.5 table).
  2. Messages tab. Do you see CONNECT going up and CONNECTED coming down? If you see ERROR, read its message header and body.
  3. Check heart-beat header values in CONNECT/CONNECTED.
  4. Check your SUBSCRIBE frames: exact destination string, id.
  5. Send and verify the SEND frame destination (/app/...) and content-type.
  6. Do MESSAGE frames arrive? With which subscription id? If MESSAGE frames arrive but the UI doesn't update → client code bug (wrong callback, parse error, state bug).
  7. Server logs with DEBUG on org.springframework.messaging and org.springframework.web.socket (temporarily): you'll see each message, destination, handler resolution, and broker deliveries.
  8. WebSocketMessageBrokerStats log line: session counts, abnormal closes, queue sizes.
  9. Close code in onWebSocketClose (1000/1001/1006/1008/1009/1011/4xxx) narrows it down fast.
  10. Reproduce with raw tools (wscat/websocat/Postman) to separate client-library bugs from server bugs.
  11. Behind proxies: test directly against the app (port-forward to the pod) vs through the LB. If direct works and LB doesn't, it's proxy config.
  12. Packet level: Wireshark/tcpdump on the server (tcpdump -i any port 8080 -A) for unencrypted internal hops.

10.4 The Bug Encyclopedia (symptom → cause → fix)

A. Connection & handshake problems

# Symptom Likely cause Fix
A1 WebSocket connection to 'wss://.../ws' failed with 404 Wrong path; app has server.servlet.context-path (e.g., /api → /api/ws); SockJS endpoint used with raw WS; gateway route missing Use the correct full path; for SockJS endpoints use SockJS client or /ws/websocket; add route
A2 Handshake returns 200 Request hit an HTML page (SPA fallback / static route) or proxy didn't treat it as upgrade Route /ws to the backend before SPA fallback; fix proxy Upgrade headers
A3 403 on handshake Origin not allowed (setAllowedOrigins); Spring Security HTTP rules deny /ws; CSRF in HTTP layer (rare for GET) Add origin to allow-list (exact scheme+host+port); permitAll or authenticate /ws deliberately
A4 400 "Can "Upgrade" only to "WebSocket"" / "Connection header must include Upgrade" Proxy stripped/rewrote Upgrade/Connection Nginx: proxy_http_version 1.1 + Upgrade + Connection headers
A5 Works locally, fails in prod behind Nginx/Ingress Same as A4, or HTTP/2-only hop without extended CONNECT Fix proxy config; ensure HTTP/1.1 upgrade to backend
A6 Browser: "Mixed Content: ... insecure WebSocket endpoint" ws:// from an https:// page Use wss://
A7 SockJS: CORS error on /ws/info SockJS uses XHR → CORS applies; origin not allowed setAllowedOriginPatterns(...) on the endpoint
A8 Android emulator can't connect to ws://localhost:8080 localhost = the emulator Use 10.0.2.2 (emulator) or LAN IP (device)
A9 Android: CLEARTEXT communication not permitted Android 9+ blocks cleartext Use wss://, or dev-only network security config
A10 iOS: connection fails to ws:// ATS blocks non-TLS wss:// or dev-only ATS exception
A11 Immediate close 1006 with no response, only in some environments TLS certificate invalid/self-signed; corporate proxy; firewall Fix certificate chain; test with openssl s_client; try SockJS/443
A12 Spring log: "Handshake failed due to invalid Upgrade header: null" Plain HTTP request reached WS endpoint (health check, crawler, misrouted) Usually harmless; make health checks hit /actuator/health
A13 "Unexpected server response: 502/504" LB can't reach backend / backend slow / backend not listening on that port Check target health, ports, security groups
A14 New connections refused at ~8k per instance Tomcat server.tomcat.max-connections (default 8192) reached Raise max-connections (+ fds), or scale out
A15 Connection fails only on corporate networks Proxy blocks/breaks WebSocket Use wss:// on 443; enable SockJS fallbacks

B. Connection drops and reconnects

# Symptom Likely cause Fix
B1 Drops every ~60 s when idle Nginx/ALB idle timeout (60 s default) with no heart-beats Enable heart-beats (10–25 s) + raise proxy timeouts
B2 Drops after exactly 30 s on GCP Backend service timeout = WS max duration Raise backend service timeout (e.g., 3600 s+)
B3 Drops around 100 s idle behind Cloudflare Cloudflare idle timeout Heart-beats < 100 s
B4 Frequent 1006 on mobile Network changes, background suspension Heart-beats, reconnect with backoff, resync, push notifications for background
B5 Client: "did not receive server activity for the last N ms" then reconnect Heart-beats were negotiated but the server's beats (or any data) don't reach the client in time: outbound thread pool saturated, a proxy buffering the stream, long GC pauses, or the browser throttling timers in a background tab Check the outbound pool and GC logs, set proxy_buffering off, give heart-beats more tolerance, and resync when a tab becomes visible again
B6 Server closes sessions with 4500 / stats show "send limit" Slow client exceeded sendBufferSizeLimit/sendTimeLimit Reduce message rate (conflate/batch), raise limits moderately, investigate client network
B7 1009 close Message larger than container buffer or Spring limit Raise setMessageSizeLimit and container buffers, or send smaller messages
B8 Everyone disconnects at deploy and servers spike Expected disconnect + synchronized reconnect (thundering herd) Jittered backoff, rolling deploy, readiness/preStop, capacity headroom
B9 Infinite reconnect loop, each attempt gets ERROR "Invalid token" Token expired; client reuses old token Refresh in beforeConnect; on auth ERROR force re-login; don't retry identical credentials forever
B10 Connection closes ~60 s after connect without traffic, server log "no messages received" timeToFirstMessage exceeded: client never sent CONNECT (e.g., RN NULL issue, client bug) Fix client CONNECT; check RN flags
B11 RabbitMQ log "missed heartbeats from client, timeout: Xs" and closes Client heart-beats not reaching broker in time (blocked JS thread, mobile sleep) Increase interval; avoid blocking main thread; reconnect
B12 Connections slowly become unbalanced across pods Long-lived connections don't rebalance Least-conn LB; max connection age with jitter

C. STOMP session problems

# Symptom Likely cause Fix
C1 WebSocket opens but onConnect never fires CONNECT not sent/parsed: RN NULL chopping; wrong sub-protocol; server not STOMP endpoint (raw handler) RN: forceBinaryWSFrames + appendMissingNULLonIncoming; check endpoint config
C2 Immediately ERROR + close after CONNECT Exception in an inbound interceptor (auth failed, CSRF missing) Read ERROR message; fix token/CSRF; customise error handler for clearer messages
C3 ERROR "Failed to send message to ExecutorSubscribableChannel[clientInboundChannel]; nested exception is ... AccessDeniedException" Spring Security authorisation denied the frame Fix message security rules or interceptor order (@Order(HIGHEST_PRECEDENCE + 99))
C4 MissingCsrfTokenException / "Could not verify the provided CSRF token because no token was found" on CONNECT @EnableWebSocketSecurity requires CSRF token in CONNECT Send the CSRF header in connectHeaders, or configure without CSRF for header-token auth (Chapter 8)
C5 Principal is null in @MessageMapping User never set: used StompHeaderAccessor.wrap() copy, or interceptor not on inbound channel, or handshake unauthenticated Use MessageHeaderAccessor.getAccessor(...) + setUser on CONNECT
C6 CONNECTED received but heart-beat header is 0,0 Simple broker without TaskScheduler; or client sent 0,0 Configure setTaskScheduler + setHeartbeatValue
C7 BeanCurrentlyInCreationException / circular dependency when injecting TaskScheduler Scheduler depends on message broker config Dedicated scheduler bean or @Lazy injection
C8 Old client gets ERROR on ACK Client sends 1.1-style ACK (message-id/subscription) to a 1.2 session Use 1.2 id = MESSAGE ack header (update library)
C9 ERROR "Broker not available" / MessageDeliveryException: Message broker not active Relay can't reach broker (down, wrong host/port, credentials, network policy, reactor-netty missing) Check BrokerAvailabilityEvent logs, broker connectivity, credentials, add io.projectreactor.netty:reactor-netty + io.netty:netty-all
C10 A rejected CONNECT/SUBSCRIBE (bad token, forbidden destination) gets no ERROR frame; the client hangs until its connect timeout; server logs Failed to send GenericMessage [...] from OrderedMessageChannelDecorator StompEndpointRegistry#setPreserveReceiveOrder(true) is on. The ordering decorator catches and only logs exceptions thrown by inbound interceptors, so StompSubProtocolHandler never sends ERROR or closes the session (verified in Spring Framework 7.0.9 and main) Don't combine setPreserveReceiveOrder(true) with rejecting interceptors, or make the client time out the CONNECT (connectionTimeout) and treat it as an auth failure. Found while testing the Appendix B sample
C11 Client sees close code 1002 right after an ERROR frame Expected: Spring closes the session with CloseStatus.PROTOCOL_ERROR (1002) after any STOMP ERROR (default error path and custom StompSubProtocolErrorHandler alike) Read the ERROR frame's message header for the reason; don't treat 1002 as a network problem

D. Routing & delivery problems

# Symptom Likely cause Fix
D1 @MessageMapping method never called Client sends to /chat.send instead of /app/chat.send; prefix mismatch; class not a Spring @Controller bean; path separator mismatch (. vs /) Align prefix; component scanning; consistent PathMatcher
D2 Return value of @MessageMapping not received by client Client subscribed to a different destination than the default (/topic + input destination) Use explicit @SendTo; subscribe to the right topic
D3 SimpMessagingTemplate.convertAndSend("/app/x") doesn't invoke controller Template sends to broker channel; /app only routes client messages Call the service directly; or send to broker destinations only
D4 Message sent to /queue/x silently disappears Broker only configured for /topic (enableSimpleBroker("/topic")) Add the prefix to the broker config
D5 Subscribed but receives nothing Subscribed before connected (exception swallowed); typo in destination; leading/trailing spaces in header; destination not authorised (dropped) Subscribe in onConnect; log frames; check security rules
D6 convertAndSendToUser messages never arrive Name mismatch with Principal#getName() (email vs id vs sub); client subscribed to /queue/x instead of /user/queue/x; no Principal on session; user connected to another instance Align names; subscribe with /user prefix; set Principal; enable user destination broadcast (relay)
D7 Users on server A don't get messages sent on server B Simple broker per instance Broker relay or Redis/Kafka backplane
D8 Duplicate messages in UI Multiple subscriptions to same destination (React re-renders, StrictMode, subscribing in onConnect and in components, multiple Client instances) Single client + ref-counted subscriptions; unsubscribe on cleanup
D9 Messages lost after reconnect No re-subscribe; events during downtime not replayed Subscribe in onConnect; resync via REST with last seq
D10 Messages arrive out of order Thread pools; multiple producers setPreservePublishOrder(true), setPreserveReceiveOrder(true); sequence numbers
D11 Client logs "Unhandled received MESSAGE" MESSAGE for a subscription that was already unsubscribed (in-flight), or subscription id mismatch Usually benign; ensure ids are unique; handle onUnhandledMessage
D12 @SendToUser reply goes to all tabs broadcast = true default @SendToUser(broadcast = false)
D13 @SendToUser for anonymous users No Principal → Spring falls back to the session (single session delivery) Works for the current session; for later pushes keep session id or assign anonymous Principal in handshake
D14 RabbitMQ wildcard subscription doesn't match Using / separators in routing keys (/topic/prices/AAPL) with */# Use dots: /topic/prices.AAPL, AntPathMatcher(".")
D15 RabbitMQ ERROR "not_found" /amq/queue/x where queue x doesn't exist Declare queue first, or use /queue/x
D16 RabbitMQ queue count grows forever Per-session user queues (/queue/x-user...) not auto-deleted Use /exchange/amq.direct/... user destinations, policies with x-expires
D17 Subscription works for user A but A can still see list after being removed Authorisation only at SUBSCRIBE time Revoke: close sessions / re-check on change / send through user destinations

E. Payload / conversion problems

# Symptom Likely cause Fix
E1 Handler receives null fields / conversion error Missing content-type: application/json; record/class not deserialisable; unknown properties failing Send content-type; check Jackson config; @JsonIgnoreProperties(ignoreUnknown = true)
E2 MessageConversionException: Cannot convert from [[B] to [com.x.Dto] No suitable converter (Jackson missing, wrong content-type like text/plain) Add Jackson; set content-type; register converter
E3 Dates arrive as arrays [2024,1,1,...] or fail Jackson without JavaTimeModule / custom ObjectMapper not used for messaging Use Boot's ObjectMapper (auto-configured), or register modules in your converter
E4 Client gets "\"hello\"" (double-quoted) Returning a String converted as JSON Return an object, or configure StringMessageConverter precedence
E5 Non-ASCII text garbled / frames corrupted content-length computed in characters; wrong charset Let the library compute; UTF-8 everywhere
E6 JSON.parse error in client Body isn't JSON (string/error text); binary body read as text Check content-type; use binaryBody for binary
E7 Large payload fails only for some users Over message size limits (B7) Limits or redesign
E8 @Valid not triggering Missing validation starter / @Valid Add starter and annotation
E9 MethodArgumentNotValidException handler not invoked Imported MVC class instead of org.springframework.messaging.handler.annotation.support.MethodArgumentNotValidException Fix import

F. Server performance & stability

# Symptom Likely cause Fix
F1 Latency grows under load, CPU low Blocking handlers saturating inbound pool; maxPoolSize ineffective with unbounded queue Increase core size; offload blocking work; async returns
F2 OutOfMemoryError Slow clients buffering; unbounded executor queues; leaked maps (presence, rate-limit buckets); huge subscriptions Send limits; bounded queues; cleanup on disconnect; heap dump analysis
F3 IllegalStateException: The remote endpoint was in state [TEXT_PARTIAL_WRITING] Concurrent sends to a raw WebSocketSession Wrap with ConcurrentWebSocketSessionDecorator or single-threaded sending
F4 GC pauses → heart-beat timeouts → mass reconnects Large heap, allocation-heavy fan-out GC tuning (G1/ZGC), reduce allocations, conflation, more instances
F5 Broker CPU/memory high, connections at limit Relay creates 1 TCP connection per client Size broker, or move to backplane pattern
F6 SessionDisconnectEvent handlers run twice Documented behaviour Make handlers idempotent
F7 Wrong "online" counts in cluster Local in-memory presence Redis-based presence with TTL, or userRegistryBroadcast
F8 Autoscaler doesn't add pods while sockets max out HPA on CPU Scale on connection count/memory custom metrics

G. Client-specific problems

# Symptom Likely cause Fix
G1 TypeError: There is no underlying STOMP connection publish/subscribe before connected or after disconnect Check client.connected; outbox; subscribe in onConnect
G2 Two connections in dev (React) StrictMode double-invokes effects Singleton client; tolerate in dev
G3 Memory leak in SPA Subscriptions/listeners not cleaned on unmount Unsubscribe in effect cleanup
G4 Stale state in callbacks Closure captured old state Functional setState(prev => ...), refs
G5 React Native: ReferenceError: TextEncoder Missing polyfill on old RN/JSC Import text-encoding polyfill
G6 React Native: frames garbled / never CONNECTED NULL byte truncation forceBinaryWSFrames: true, appendMissingNULLonIncoming: true
G7 Old tutorial code Stomp.over(...) doesn't reconnect Legacy API with a socket instance Migrate to new Client({...}) + activate()
G8 Token visible in logs debug enabled in production, or token in URL Disable debug; CONNECT headers; redact
G9 Background tab stops receiving / timers throttled Browser throttles timers in background tabs; heart-beats may be delayed stompjs: heartbeatStrategy: TickerStrategy.Worker (heart-beats from a Web Worker), raise heartbeatToleranceMultiplier; resync on visibilitychange
G11 Reconnecting stops forever after the auth server or token endpoint was briefly down; Node logs an unhandled rejection (fetch failed) stompjs 7.x awaits beforeConnect without try/catch and calls _connect() with void. A throwing beforeConnect rejects, nothing schedules the next attempt Wrap the token fetch in beforeConnect with try/catch (keep the old headers and let the attempt fail normally); add onChangeState monitoring
G12 After a server restart the client says "reconnecting" forever; debug shows Connection not established in Nms, closing socket and nothing after The connection attempt got stuck in CONNECTING, and close() on it never produced a close event (observed with Node's built-in WebSocket), so no reconnect was scheduled discardWebsocketOnCommFailure: true (stompjs drops the socket and synthesises the close → reconnect proceeds)
G13 Client keeps showing stale data after the server lost its state (restart with in-memory store, DB restore) Resync asked for "events after seq N" but the server's sequence restarted below N and replied "nothing new" Server must answer "reload" (e.g., HTTP 410) when the client's seq is ahead of its own; client then loads a full snapshot
G10 App reconnect hammering after server outage Fixed short reconnectDelay across all clients stompjs: reconnectTimeMode: ReconnectionTimeMode.EXPONENTIAL + maxReconnectDelay. It adds no jitter, so also randomise reconnectDelay per client (e.g., 1000 + Math.random()*4000)

10.5 "What do I do when…" playbook

…users report "real-time stopped working" (incident)?

  1. Check dashboards: session count, connect rate, close codes, broker availability.
  2. Are connections being established (101s at LB)? If not → LB/proxy/certificate/DNS.
  3. Are CONNECTs failing (ERROR)? → auth provider/JWKS endpoint down? clock skew? CSRF change?
  4. Are messages produced? Check the producing service logs and the broker (queue depth, publish rates).
  5. Are messages routed? Check broker bindings/subscriptions; relay availability events.
  6. Mitigate first (roll back recent deploy, scale out, restart a stuck broker node), then root-cause.

…a deploy causes a spike of errors? Jittered reconnect present? Readiness gating? Maybe the new version changed message formats (backward compatibility!) or security rules (403/ERROR).

…memory grows steadily over days? Heap dump: look for ConcurrentWebSocketSessionDecorator buffers (slow clients), your own maps keyed by session id (missing cleanup on disconnect), DefaultSubscriptionRegistry sizes (subscriptions never unsubscribed), executor queues.

…one customer says they never get updates but others do? Their network/proxy (corporate) → check their close codes/transport; their user id mapping (D6); authorisation for their tenant; they're subscribed to the wrong destination after a client version mismatch.

…messages are slow (seconds of delay)? Inbound/outbound queue sizes, GC pauses, broker queue backlogs, proxy buffering (proxy_buffering off), client main-thread blocking (huge re-renders per message → batch UI updates).

…you must add a new real-time feature fast? Reuse the existing connection, define destination naming + authorisation rule + schema first, add to AsyncAPI doc, implement with resync story, load test fan-out.

10.6 Useful log/config snippets

properties
# Temporarily, when debugging locally
logging.level.org.springframework.web.socket=DEBUG
logging.level.org.springframework.messaging=DEBUG
logging.level.org.springframework.security.messaging=DEBUG
