logging.level.org.springframework.web.socket.config.WebSocketMessageBrokerStats=INFO
java
// Log every inbound STOMP command (redacting secrets) for troubleshooting
public class StompAuditInterceptor implements ChannelInterceptor {
    private static final Logger log = LoggerFactory.getLogger(StompAuditInterceptor.class);
    @Override
    public Message<?> preSend(Message<?> m, MessageChannel c) {
        StompHeaderAccessor a = MessageHeaderAccessor.getAccessor(m, StompHeaderAccessor.class);
        if (a != null && a.getCommand() != null) {
            log.debug("in cmd={} session={} user={} dest={} subId={}",
                a.getCommand(), a.getSessionId(),
                a.getUser() != null ? a.getUser().getName() : null,
                a.getDestination(), a.getSubscriptionId());
        }
        return m;
    }
}

Next: Chapter 11: Architecture, Design Patterns & Organisational Best Practices

Chapter 11Production

Architecture, Design Patterns & Organisational Best Practices

Goal: design real-time systems the way strong senior engineers and architects do, and run them in a big organisation with many teams, environments, audits and on-call rotations.

11.1 ELI5

Building one walkie-talkie is easy. Running a city-wide radio network means rules: who gets which channel, what words to use, what to do when a tower breaks, how to add new channels without confusing old radios, and who fixes things at 3 a.m. That's what this chapter is about.

11.2 Decision framework: should this feature be real-time at all?

Ask:

  1. Does the user need it within seconds? If minutes are fine → polling/refresh/email.
  2. Direction? Server→client only → consider SSE. Both ways, high frequency → WebSocket.
  3. Online only? Offline users need push notifications / inbox anyway.
  4. Audience size per message? 1 (private) vs 10 (room) vs 1M (broadcast) changes the design.
  5. Message rate? 1/min vs 50/s per client: conflation, binary formats.
  6. Consistency needs? Is missing an event acceptable? (UI hint → yes; balance update → no → resync/ack design.)
  7. Ops maturity? Stateful infra, brokers, on-call. Or buy a managed service.

Why STOMP (vs raw WebSocket/custom JSON)? Standard framing, pub/sub semantics, Spring integration (@MessageMapping, user destinations, security), broker interoperability, mature clients. Why not? Very high-frequency binary data (games, audio), non-Spring stacks where Socket.IO/SignalR are idiomatic, or when you need features STOMP lacks (built-in replay → consider Kafka-backed gateways, Ably, etc.).

Architecture Decision Record (ADR) template

# ADR-012: Real-time updates for shared to-do lists
Status: Accepted (2026-10-01)
Context: Users collaborate on lists; changes must appear on other devices within 2s. Mobile + web.
Decision: STOMP over WebSocket via Spring Boot "realtime-gateway"; RabbitMQ relay in prod; JWT in CONNECT;
          events carry per-list sequence numbers; clients resync via REST after reconnect.
Alternatives: SSE (no client→server channel needed? we need typing/presence → rejected), polling (latency/cost),
          Firebase (data residency), Socket.IO (Java stack).
Consequences: Stateful service to operate; RabbitMQ capacity planning; need LB timeouts; on-call runbook.

11.3 Reference architecture for a company

                      ┌───────────────── Edge ──────────────────┐
 Web / Mobile ─wss──► │ CDN/WAF → L7 Load Balancer (timeouts,   │
                      │ origin allow-list, connection limits)    │
                      └───────────────┬─────────────────────────┘
                                      ▼
                     ┌──────────────────────────────────┐
                     │   Realtime Gateway (Spring Boot) │  N stateless-ish instances
                     │   - STOMP endpoint /ws           │  (state = live connections)
                     │   - AuthN (JWT on CONNECT)       │
                     │   - AuthZ (subscribe/send rules) │
                     │   - Thin @MessageMapping → calls │
                     │     domain services via REST/gRPC│
                     └──────┬──────────────▲────────────┘
                            │ relay / backplane
                            ▼              │
                     ┌───────────────────────────────┐
                     │ RabbitMQ (STOMP) / Redis / Kafka│
                     └──────▲────────────────────────┘
                            │ domain events (outbox → Kafka → gateway consumer)
          ┌─────────────────┴──────────────────┐
          │ Domain services (orders, todo, chat)│  own their DB; never hold sockets
          └─────────────────────────────────────┘

Principles:

  • Separate the real-time gateway from domain services. Domain teams publish events; the gateway owns connections, auth, fan-out. Domain services stay stateless and independently deployable.
  • Events in, pushes out: the gateway consumes domain events (Kafka/RabbitMQ) and maps them to STOMP destinations, so domain teams don't need to know about WebSockets.
  • Commands via REST (or /app messages forwarded to services). Many companies send commands over HTTP (easy auth, idempotency, retries, observability) and use WebSocket only for server→client pushes. That's a valid, simpler design.
  • Gateway is horizontally scalable; connection state is disposable (clients reconnect + resync).

11.4 Destination naming conventions (write them down!)

A consistent, documented scheme prevents bugs and security holes.

Client → server (commands)       /app/{domain}.{action}              /app/todo.upsert, /app/chat.send
Public broadcast                  /topic/{domain}.{scope}             /topic/system.announcements
Entity-scoped                     /topic/{domain}.{entityType}.{id}   /topic/todo.list.L1, /topic/chat.room.42
Tenant-scoped                     /topic/t.{tenantId}.{domain}...     /topic/t.acme.orders.123
Private per user                  /user/queue/{purpose}               /user/queue/notifications, /user/queue/errors
Request/reply initial data        /app/{domain}.{resource}            (@SubscribeMapping) /app/todo.lists

Rules:

  • Dots as separators (RabbitMQ-friendly), lowercase, no spaces.
  • IDs must be validated (regex) and authorised.
  • Never encode secrets or PII (emails) in destinations (they appear in logs and broker UIs).
  • Keep a registry (AsyncAPI) of every destination: owner team, payload schema, auth rule, rate.

11.5 Message envelope and versioning

Use a consistent envelope for every event:

jsonc
// (comments added for explanation; real JSON has none)
{
  "id": "01J9Z6Q7T4M8K2H5V3B1C0D9EF",          // unique event id (ULID/UUID) for dedupe
  "type": "todo.item.updated",                  // event type
  "version": 2,                                  // schema version of `data`
  "seq": 1842,                                   // per-entity sequence for ordering/gap detection
  "occurredAt": "2026-10-15T10:21:33.120Z",
  "correlationId": "c-7f2a…",                    // trace across systems / ties to clientRequestId
  "actor": { "id": "u-123", "type": "user" },
  "data": { "listId": "L1", "itemId": "I9", "title": "Buy milk", "done": true }
}

Versioning rules (consumers in the field may be old app versions for months, especially mobile):

  • Additive changes only within a version (add optional fields; never remove/rename/retype).
  • Clients ignore unknown fields and unknown event types.
  • Breaking change → new version or new type, and the server sends both until old clients are gone (or negotiate by client version sent in CONNECT headers, e.g., client-version: 5.2.0).
  • Track client versions in metrics to know when it's safe to drop old formats.

AsyncAPI (document your real-time API like OpenAPI documents REST)

yaml
asyncapi: 3.0.0
info: { title: Realtime Gateway, version: 1.4.0 }
servers:
  production:
    host: api.example.com
    pathname: /ws
    protocol: wss          # STOMP over WebSocket; document the STOMP layer in description/bindings
channels:
  todoList:
    address: /topic/todo.list.{listId}
    parameters: { listId: { description: "List id, must be a member" } }
    messages:
      itemUpdated: { $ref: '#/components/messages/TodoItemUpdated' }
operations:
  receiveTodoUpdates:
    action: receive
    channel: { $ref: '#/channels/todoList' }
components:
  messages:
    TodoItemUpdated:
      payload:
        type: object
        required: [id, type, seq, data]
        properties:
          id: { type: string }
          type: { const: todo.item.updated }
          seq: { type: integer }
          data: { $ref: '#/components/schemas/TodoItem' }

Generate docs and TypeScript/Java types from it; validate in CI.

11.6 Design patterns catalogue

Pattern Problem Solution
Notification + fetch Big payloads, consistency Push small "changed (id, version)" events; client fetches via REST
Snapshot + stream Initial state + live updates without gaps Subscribe first, fetch snapshot (or @SubscribeMapping), apply buffered events with seq > snapshot version
Resync on reconnect Missed events while offline Client stores last seq; REST ?after=seq or full reload
Idempotent commands Retries cause duplicates clientRequestId + server dedupe
Optimistic UI Latency Apply locally, reconcile on server event, roll back on error
Conflation High-frequency updates Latest-value-per-key every X ms
Batching Many tiny messages Send arrays every X ms
Fan-out on write Broadcast to many Broker/backplane fan-out, topic per scope
Request/reply over STOMP RPC-like call @SendToUser(broadcast=false) reply or reply-to + correlation-id headers
Presence with TTL Who's online across a cluster Redis keys with TTL refreshed by heart-beats/events; publish presence changes
Outbox Reliable event publishing Event row in same transaction; relay publishes
Gateway pattern Many teams, one connection Central real-time gateway consuming domain events
Backplane Scale simple broker Redis/Kafka fan-out between nodes
Circuit breaker on push Downstream overload Shed non-critical events (typing indicators) first

11.7 Worked designs (interview-ready)

11.7.1 Chat application (Slack/WhatsApp-like, web + mobile)

  • Destinations: /topic/chat.room.{roomId} (channels), /user/queue/dm (direct messages), /user/queue/receipts (delivered/read), /topic/chat.room.{roomId}.typing (ephemeral).
  • Send: /app/chat.send with clientMsgId; server validates membership, persists (DB with per-room seq), then publishes after commit.
  • History: REST paginated GET /rooms/{id}/messages?before=seq.
  • Reliability: clients dedupe by clientMsgId/id, detect gaps with seq, resync on reconnect; offline → push notification.
  • Typing indicators: ephemeral, rate-limited (max 1 per 3 s per user), never persisted, dropped first under load.
  • Read receipts: batched ("read up to seq N"), not per message.
  • Presence: Redis TTL keys, publish only changes; for huge orgs, presence only for contacts/visible users.
  • Scale: gateway + RabbitMQ relay or Kafka partitioned by roomId → per-room ordering.
  • Security: room membership checked on SUBSCRIBE and SEND; sender from Principal; XSS-safe rendering; attachments via pre-signed URLs, not over the socket.

11.7.2 Notification system

  • /user/queue/notifications for online users; persist all in an inbox table (unread counts via REST).
  • Domain services emit events → notification service decides channels (in-app, push, email) → gateway pushes in-app.
  • On connect: client fetches unread count + latest notifications via REST, then listens.

11.7.3 Live dashboard / stock ticker

  • /topic/prices.{symbol}; client subscribes only to visible symbols.
  • Conflation (e.g., 4–10 updates/s per symbol), deltas, binary encoding for very high rates.
  • Slow clients get dropped (send limits) and resync with snapshot.
  • Separate critical (order fills: /user/queue/fills, at-least-once with resync) from best-effort market data.

11.7.4 Collaborative to-do list (this repo's domain)

  • Events per list with seq; snapshot via @SubscribeMapping("/todo.list.{id}") or REST.
  • Conflict handling: optimistic concurrency (expectedVersion) → conflict error to /user/queue/errors → client refetches item.
  • Offline-first mobile: local DB (SQLite/WatermelonDB/Realm), outbox of commands with clientRequestId, replay on reconnect.
  • For rich-text collaborative editing use OT/CRDT (e.g., Yjs/Automerge). Those usually have their own sync protocol over WebSocket; STOMP can carry their binary updates but is rarely the best fit.

11.7.5 Live location tracking (delivery app)

  • Driver app publishes location every 3–5 s to /app/driver.location (HTTP is also fine).
  • Server stores latest location (Redis GEO), publishes to /topic/order.{orderId}.location for the customer (authorised to that order only).
  • Conflate; send only when moved > N meters.

11.7.6 Long-running job progress

  • Client starts job via REST → gets jobId.
  • Subscribes to /user/queue/jobs or /topic/job.{jobId} (authorised).
  • Worker publishes progress via the event pipeline; final state persisted; client polls REST once on reconnect.

11.8 Organisational best practices

11.8.1 Ownership and governance

  • One team owns the real-time platform (gateway, broker, client SDK, docs, runbooks). Domain teams own their events.
  • Destination registry (AsyncAPI) reviewed like an API change; security review for new destinations (auth rule mandatory).
  • Shared client SDK (TypeScript + Kotlin/Swift wrappers) encapsulating connection, reconnect, resync, auth, metrics. Teams shouldn't each write their own STOMP setup.

11.8.2 Environments and configuration

  • Same topology in staging as prod (proxy, LB timeouts, broker relay). "Works in dev with simple broker, fails in prod with RabbitMQ" is a classic.
  • Externalise: allowed origins, broker host/creds (secret manager), heart-beat values, limits, feature flags.
  • Never commit broker passwords; rotate credentials.

11.8.3 Code organisation (Spring)

realtime-gateway/
  config/        WebSocketConfig, WebSocketSecurityConfig, BrokerRelayConfig, TransportLimits
  security/      JwtConnectInterceptor, SubscriptionGuard, RateLimitInterceptor, CustomStompErrorHandler
  controller/    *WsController (thin: validate, authorise, delegate)
  events/        Domain event consumers → SimpMessagingTemplate publishers
  presence/      PresenceService (Redis)
  metrics/       WsMetrics, executor binders
  docs/          asyncapi.yaml
  • Controllers thin; business logic in services (testable without sockets).
  • All destination strings in one constants class (no string typos scattered around).

11.8.4 Code review checklist for real-time changes

  • New destination documented (AsyncAPI) with schema, owner, auth rule.
  • SUBSCRIBE/SEND authorisation implemented and tested (including negative tests).
  • Identity from Principal; tenant isolation.
  • Payload backward compatible; unknown fields ignored by clients.
  • Event has id + seq; client handles duplicates/gaps; resync path exists.
  • Published after commit/outbox.
  • Rate and size reasonable; conflation if high-frequency.
  • No blocking calls in handlers without pool sizing.
  • Metrics/logs added; no PII/tokens logged.
  • Client unsubscribes on cleanup.

11.8.5 SLOs and on-call

Example SLOs:

  • Connection success rate ≥ 99.5% (successful CONNECTED / attempts, excluding auth failures).
  • Push latency p95 < 1 s from event commit to client receipt (sampled).
  • Unexpected disconnect rate < X per connection-hour.

Runbook entries (each with symptoms, dashboards, mitigation, escalation):

  1. Mass disconnect / reconnect storm.
  2. Broker unavailable (relay).
  3. Auth failures spike (JWKS/IdP problem, clock skew, key rotation).
  4. Memory pressure on gateway pods.
  5. Message lag / backlog in broker or Kafka consumer.
  6. Single tenant/customer can't connect (proxy, origin, firewall).

11.8.6 Release practices

  • Rolling deploys with connection draining and jittered client reconnect.
  • Canary gateway instances: route a small % of new connections; compare error/close-code rates.
  • Feature flags for new event types (server sends only when flag on; client ignores unknown types anyway).
  • Mobile: keep old payload versions alive long enough; force-upgrade path for truly breaking changes.
  • Chaos drills: kill a gateway pod, restart a broker node, inject latency. Verify recovery and resync.

11.8.7 Compliance and audit

  • Data residency: brokers and gateways in the right region; per-region topics.
  • Retention: real-time messages that matter must also be persisted (audit/compliance), not only pushed.
  • Access logs: who connected when, from where (IP), which user, close reason.
  • Pen tests include WebSocket (CSWSH, IDOR on subscriptions, injection, DoS).

11.9 Anti-patterns (and what to do instead)

Anti-pattern Why it's bad Do instead
One WebSocket per component/screen Many sockets, duplicates, leaks One connection, shared client, ref-counted subs
Using WebSocket as the source of truth Messages lost on disconnect DB is truth; push is notification; resync
Sending huge payloads (files, big lists) over the socket Size limits, head-of-line blocking, memory Send ids/URLs, fetch over HTTP/CDN
Trusting from/userId in payloads Impersonation Principal from session
setAllowedOriginPatterns("*") with cookies CSWSH Allow-list
No authorisation on SUBSCRIBE Data leaks (IDOR) Per-destination ownership checks
Simple broker in a multi-instance deployment Messages reach only some users Relay or backplane
No heart-beats Silent dead connections, proxy timeouts 10–25 s heart-beats
Fixed-interval reconnect for everyone Thundering herd Backoff + jitter
Business logic inside interceptors Untestable, slow Interceptors for cross-cutting only
Blocking I/O in @MessageMapping with default pool Latency collapse Size pools / async
Publishing before DB commit Clients see rolled-back data After-commit listeners / outbox
No message schema/versioning Breaking old mobile apps Envelope + additive changes + AsyncAPI
Logging full frames in prod Token/PII leaks, cost Redacted, sampled logs
Per-message DB lookup for authorisation on every MESSAGE Expensive Authorise on SUBSCRIBE, revoke on change

11.10 Migration guides

11.10.1 From polling to WebSocket

  1. Keep the polling endpoint (fallback + resync).
  2. Add WebSocket push of "changed" events; reduce polling interval to a slow safety net (e.g., 5 min) or only on reconnect.
  3. Measure load reduction and latency; remove polling for supported clients later.

11.10.2 From simple broker to broker relay

  1. Introduce RabbitMQ in staging with the STOMP plugin; dedicated users/vhost.
  2. Switch enableSimpleBroker → enableStompBrokerRelay with the same prefixes; switch to . separators if using wildcards (affects client destinations, so plan client rollout).
  3. Fix user destinations to auto-delete (/exchange/amq.direct/...) or add queue expiry policies.
  4. Enable userDestinationBroadcast and userRegistryBroadcast.
  5. Load test (broker connections = clients!); add broker monitoring; then scale gateway instances.

11.10.3 Removing SockJS

  1. Measure the share of HttpStream/HttpPoll sessions (WebSocketMessageBrokerStats).
  2. Add a raw WebSocket endpoint alongside SockJS (/ws-native), migrate clients to brokerURL.
  3. Remove SockJS when fallback usage is negligible.

11.10.4 From legacy stomp.js (Stomp.over) to @stomp/stompjs

Stomp.over(socket) → new Client({ webSocketFactory | brokerURL }); connect(headers, cb) → connectHeaders + onConnect; send(dest, headers, body) → publish({ destination, headers, body }); disconnect → deactivate; move subscriptions into onConnect.

11.10.5 From AbstractSecurityWebSocketMessageBrokerConfigurer to @EnableWebSocketSecurity (mandatory for Spring Security 7, where the old class is removed)

Replace configureInbound(MessageSecurityMetadataSourceRegistry) rules with an AuthorizationManager<Message<?>> bean built from MessageMatcherDelegatingAuthorizationManager.Builder; handle CSRF (on by default); keep your CONNECT auth interceptor ordered before security.

11.11 Chapter summary

  • Decide deliberately whether a feature needs real-time and which technology.
  • Central real-time gateway + domain events is the scalable organisational pattern.
  • Document destinations, schemas and auth rules (AsyncAPI); version payloads additively.
  • Patterns: notification+fetch, snapshot+stream, resync, idempotent commands, conflation, outbox, presence with TTL.
  • Run it like a product: SDK, runbooks, SLOs, canaries, chaos drills, security reviews.

Next: Chapter 12: Interview Questions & Answers

Chapter 12Interviews

Interview Questions & Answers, Part 1

How to use this chapter: questions are grouped by topic and tagged by level: [E] entry/junior, [M] mid-level, [S] senior/lead/architect. Each answer is written the way you should say it in an interview: direct answer first, then the "why", then a detail that shows depth. These cover the questions commonly asked at product companies, service companies, fintech, and e-commerce for Java/Spring, full-stack, front-end, and system-design rounds.

Part 1: Fundamentals, WebSocket protocol, browser API, STOMP, Spring. Part 2: Security, scaling & brokers, reliability & performance, debugging scenarios, system design, coding tasks, behavioural, rapid-fire.

A. Fundamentals & comparisons

A1. [E] What is a WebSocket? A protocol (RFC 6455) that provides a persistent, full-duplex communication channel over a single TCP connection. It starts as an HTTP/1.1 request with Upgrade: websocket; the server answers 101 Switching Protocols, and from then on both sides exchange lightweight frames at any time. It's used for real-time features like chat, live dashboards, notifications, collaboration and games.

A2. [E] Why not just use HTTP for real-time? HTTP is request/response and client-initiated: the server can't push data unprompted. Emulating push with polling wastes requests (most return nothing), adds latency equal to the interval, and repeats headers each time. WebSocket keeps one connection open with 2–14 bytes of framing overhead per message and lets the server push instantly.

A3. [E] Explain short polling, long polling, SSE, and WebSocket.

  • Short polling: client requests every N seconds. Simple, wasteful, latency up to N.
  • Long polling: server holds the request until data arrives or a timeout, then the client re-requests. Near real-time but one full HTTP exchange per message.
  • SSE: one long HTTP response streaming text/event-stream events, server→client only, text only, auto-reconnect with Last-Event-ID built in.
  • WebSocket: bidirectional, binary or text, lowest overhead, but you implement reconnect/heart-beats and run stateful infrastructure.

A4. [M] When would you choose SSE over WebSocket? When data flows only server→client (notifications, feeds, progress, LLM token streaming), when you want built-in reconnect/resume, simpler infrastructure (plain HTTP, works great with HTTP/2 multiplexing), and no need for binary. Client→server actions can go over normal REST calls.

A5. [M] When should you NOT use WebSocket? Infrequent updates (polling is simpler), one-way feeds (SSE), cacheable content (HTTP/CDN), background/offline delivery on mobile (push notifications), request/response APIs (REST/gRPC), very large file transfers (HTTP with resumable uploads), or teams without capacity to operate stateful connection infrastructure.

A6. [M] Is WebSocket built on HTTP? Only the opening handshake is HTTP (an HTTP/1.1 GET with Upgrade). After 101, the connection speaks the WebSocket framing protocol directly over TCP (or TLS). It's "HTTP-compatible" so it can use ports 80/443 and pass through HTTP infrastructure. Over HTTP/2 (RFC 8441) and HTTP/3 (RFC 9220), it's bootstrapped with an extended CONNECT on a stream.

A7. [E] What ports and URL schemes does WebSocket use? ws:// (default port 80) and wss:// (TLS, default 443). Fragments (#) aren't allowed in WebSocket URIs.

A8. [M] What's the difference between WebSocket and a raw TCP socket? TCP is a byte stream with no message boundaries, no browser access, no HTTP-compatible handshake. WebSocket adds a handshake through HTTP infrastructure, message framing (text/binary), masking, control frames (ping/pong/close), origin-based security, and a browser API. Browsers can't open raw TCP sockets.

A9. [M] Is WebSocket stateful or stateless? Implications? Stateful: each server holds live connections. Implications: load balancers must support upgrades and long connections; scaling out needs a broker/backplane so messages reach users on other instances; deployments disconnect clients (need reconnect + resync); memory per connection; heart-beats needed to survive idle timeouts; metrics shift to concurrent connections and message rates.

A10. [E] What is full-duplex? Both parties can send at the same time, independently. HTTP/1.1 is effectively half-duplex per exchange (request, then response).

A11. [M] Compare WebSocket with WebRTC and WebTransport. WebRTC: peer-to-peer media and data channels over UDP (SCTP/DTLS), can be unreliable/unordered, needs signalling (often done via WebSocket) and STUN/TURN. WebTransport: client-server over HTTP/3/QUIC with multiple streams and unreliable datagrams, no head-of-line blocking; newer with growing support. WebSocket: single ordered reliable TCP stream, universally supported.

A12. [M] What is Socket.IO and can a WebSocket client talk to a Socket.IO server? Socket.IO is a library with its own protocol (Engine.IO underneath) that uses WebSocket or HTTP long-polling, adding rooms, acknowledgements, reconnection. Its packets have their own framing, so a plain WebSocket client can't talk to a Socket.IO server (and vice versa) without the Socket.IO client.

A13. [M] Was HTTP/2 Server Push a replacement for WebSockets? No. It pushed resources into the browser cache in anticipation of requests (e.g., CSS), not application messages to JavaScript, and Chrome removed it in 2022. Real-time alternatives on HTTP/2 are SSE or WebSocket over HTTP/2 (RFC 8441).

A14. [E] What is pub/sub? Topic vs queue? Publish/subscribe decouples senders from receivers through named destinations. A topic delivers each message to all current subscribers (fan-out); a queue delivers each message to one of the consumers (competing consumers, load balancing). In STOMP, destination semantics are broker-defined: /topic and /queue are conventions.

A15. [S] How does TCP's reliability relate to message reliability in WebSocket apps? TCP guarantees ordered delivery of bytes only while the connection lives. If the connection drops, bytes in flight and messages published while disconnected are lost; neither side automatically knows what the other received. Application-level reliability needs ids/sequence numbers, acks, persistence and resync after reconnect.

B. WebSocket protocol (RFC 6455)

B1. [E] Describe the WebSocket handshake. Client sends GET /path HTTP/1.1 with Host, Upgrade: websocket, Connection: Upgrade, Sec-WebSocket-Key (base64 of 16 random bytes), Sec-WebSocket-Version: 13, and optionally Origin, Sec-WebSocket-Protocol, Sec-WebSocket-Extensions. Server answers HTTP/1.1 101 Switching Protocols with Upgrade, Connection and Sec-WebSocket-Accept, plus the selected sub-protocol/extensions. Then frames flow.

B2. [M] How is Sec-WebSocket-Accept computed and why? base64(SHA-1(Sec-WebSocket-Key + "258EAFA5-E914-47DA-95CA-C5AB0DC85B11")). It proves the server understood the WebSocket handshake (not some HTTP server or cache blindly replying), preventing cross-protocol confusion and stale cached responses. It is not authentication or encryption.

B3. [M] What status code indicates a successful handshake? What if the server returns 200 or 302? 101 Switching Protocols. Anything else is a failed handshake. Browsers don't follow redirects for WebSocket; a 200 typically means the request hit a normal page (misrouting or a proxy not forwarding Upgrade).

B4. [M] Describe the structure of a WebSocket frame. Byte 0: FIN (1 bit), RSV1–3 (3 bits), opcode (4 bits). Byte 1: MASK bit + 7-bit payload length. If length is 126, the next 2 bytes hold the length; if 127, the next 8 bytes. Then a 4-byte masking key if MASK=1, then the payload. Overhead: 2–14 bytes.

B5. [E] List the opcodes. 0x0 continuation, 0x1 text, 0x2 binary, 0x8 close, 0x9 ping, 0xA pong; 0x3–0x7 and 0xB–0xF reserved.

B6. [M] What is masking and why do only clients mask? Clients XOR the payload with a random 4-byte key per frame. It prevents attacker-controlled JavaScript from producing predictable bytes on the wire that confused intermediaries could misinterpret as HTTP requests (cache poisoning, per the 2011 Huang et al. research). The server isn't attacker-scriptable in that model, so server frames aren't masked. A server must close the connection (1002) on an unmasked client frame. Masking is not encryption: the key is in the frame.

B7. [M] What is fragmentation? Can control frames be fragmented? A message can be split into frames: first frame has the opcode and FIN=0, following frames use opcode 0 (continuation), the last has FIN=1. It allows streaming messages of unknown size and keeps the connection responsive. Control frames can't be fragmented and must be ≤125 bytes, but they can be interleaved between fragments of a data message.

B8. [M] What's the difference between a frame and a message? A message is the application-level unit (one text or binary payload). A frame is the wire unit. One message = one or more frames. APIs like the browser's deliver whole messages.

B9. [M] What are ping and pong frames? Control frames for keep-alive/liveness. Receiving a ping requires replying with a pong carrying the same payload. An unsolicited pong can be a one-way heart-beat. Browsers auto-reply to pings, but JS can't send pings, which is why app-level heart-beats (like STOMP's) exist.

B10. [M] Explain the closing handshake. One side sends a Close frame (optional 2-byte status code + UTF-8 reason ≤123 bytes), stops sending data, the other replies with a Close frame, and the TCP connection closes (server closes TCP first, ideally). If TCP dies without close frames, the API reports 1006.

B11. [E/M] Name common close codes. 1000 normal, 1001 going away, 1002 protocol error, 1003 unsupported data, 1005 no status (reserved), 1006 abnormal (reserved, never sent), 1007 invalid payload (e.g., bad UTF-8), 1008 policy violation, 1009 message too big, 1010 mandatory extension, 1011 internal error, 1012 service restart, 1013 try again later, 1015 TLS failure (reserved). 3000–3999 for libraries/frameworks, 4000–4999 private use.

B12. [M] What does close code 1006 mean and how do you debug it? "Abnormal closure": the connection was lost without a close frame. The code is generated locally, never sent. Causes: network loss, server crash, proxy/LB idle timeout, TLS errors, the server killing the TCP connection. Debug with timing (exactly 60 s → proxy timeout), server logs, LB logs, heart-beat configuration, mobile network transitions.

B13. [M] What's a sub-protocol? Give STOMP's. An application-level protocol negotiated in the handshake via Sec-WebSocket-Protocol. The client offers a list; the server selects one. STOMP: v10.stomp, v11.stomp, v12.stomp.

B14. [M] What is permessage-deflate? Trade-offs? RFC 7692 extension compressing each message with DEFLATE (RSV1 marks compressed messages). It saves bandwidth for text/JSON but costs CPU and memory per connection (sliding windows with context takeover), and mixing secrets with attacker-controlled data in the same compression context can enable CRIME/BREACH-style leaks.

B15. [M] Text vs binary frames? Text frames must contain valid UTF-8 (otherwise close 1007); binary frames can be any bytes. Binary is used for protobuf/MessagePack, images, or when bodies may contain NULL bytes.

B16. [S] How does WebSocket work over HTTP/2? HTTP/2 has no Upgrade mechanism. RFC 8441 adds SETTINGS_ENABLE_CONNECT_PROTOCOL; the client sends an extended CONNECT (:method CONNECT, :protocol websocket) on a new stream; the server returns :status 200; there's no Sec-WebSocket-Key/Accept exchange. Many WebSockets can share one TCP connection. RFC 9220 does the same for HTTP/3.

B17. [M] What is the maximum message size? The protocol allows payloads up to 2^63−1 bytes per frame. In practice servers impose limits (Tomcat 8 KB message buffer by default, Spring STOMP 64 KB, API Gateway 128 KB), and exceeding them causes close 1009. Design messages to be small.

B18. [S] Why must proxies be configured specially for WebSocket? Upgrade and Connection are hop-by-hop headers that proxies normally don't forward, and proxies often default to HTTP/1.0 upstream or short read timeouts. You must forward the headers with HTTP/1.1 (Nginx: proxy_http_version 1.1, proxy_set_header Upgrade $http_upgrade, Connection "upgrade") and raise idle timeouts.

B19. [M] Does the Same-Origin Policy / CORS apply to WebSocket? No. Any page can open a WebSocket to any server, and the browser includes the page's Origin header and cookies per SameSite rules. Servers must validate Origin to prevent Cross-Site WebSocket Hijacking.

B20. [S] Why does the RFC require the server to close the TCP connection first? So the server ends up in TCP TIME_WAIT instead of the client, letting the client reopen a connection immediately.

B21. [M] What does "half-open connection" mean and how do you detect it? One side believes the connection is open while the other side is gone (e.g., phone lost signal, NAT entry expired). TCP doesn't notice until it sends data and retransmissions fail (can take many minutes). Detect with ping/pong or application heart-beats and a timeout.

B22. [S] Could you implement a WebSocket server by hand? What's hard? Yes: parse the HTTP upgrade, compute Accept, parse frames. Hard parts: partial TCP reads/buffering, fragmentation, masking, UTF-8 validation across fragments, control frames during fragmented messages, close handshake, backpressure, extensions, limits. In production, use a mature library.

C. Browser API & clients

C1. [E] How do you open a WebSocket in the browser? const ws = new WebSocket('wss://host/ws', ['v12.stomp']); then handle open, message, error, close, and send with ws.send(data) after open.

C2. [E] What are the readyState values? 0 CONNECTING, 1 OPEN, 2 CLOSING, 3 CLOSED.

C3. [M] Can you set custom headers (Authorization) on a browser WebSocket? No. The constructor only takes URL and protocols. Workarounds: cookies (with Origin checks), token in a query parameter (logged, so use short-lived tickets), the sub-protocol hack, or authenticating in the first message (STOMP CONNECT headers, the usual choice).

C4. [M] What does bufferedAmount tell you? Bytes queued by send() not yet transmitted. Use it for client-side backpressure (pause sending when high). send() never blocks.

C5. [M] Does the browser WebSocket reconnect automatically? No (unlike EventSource). Implement reconnect with exponential backoff + jitter, re-subscribe, and resync state. Libraries like stompjs (reconnectDelay) do the reconnect part.

C6. [M] Why is onerror useless for diagnostics? Browsers intentionally hide error details to prevent network probing from web pages. Use DevTools and the close event's code/reason.

C7. [M] What can you pass to close()? Code 1000 or 3000–4999 (others throw InvalidAccessError) and a reason ≤123 UTF-8 bytes (else SyntaxError).

C8. [S] How would you share one WebSocket across many tabs? SharedWorker owning the socket with MessagePort communication, or leader election (Web Locks/BroadcastChannel) where one tab holds the connection and rebroadcasts. Trade-off: complexity vs connection count.

C9. [M] What's "thundering herd" in reconnects and how do you prevent it? When a server restarts, all clients reconnect at the same moment and overload the remaining capacity. Prevent with exponential backoff, random jitter, rolling deploys, and server-side load shedding (close code 1013).

C10. [M] How do you handle WebSocket in React correctly? One shared client (module singleton/context), subscriptions in useEffect with cleanup (unsubscribe), ref-counted per destination to avoid duplicates, a ref for the latest callback to avoid re-subscribing, and functional state updates to avoid stale closures. Be aware that StrictMode double-invokes effects in development.

C11. [M] What special issues exist with STOMP on React Native? RN's WebSocket historically truncated text frames at the NULL byte that terminates STOMP frames. Fix with stompjs options forceBinaryWSFrames: true and appendMissingNULLonIncoming: true. Older engines need a TextEncoder polyfill. Android emulator must use 10.0.2.2 for host localhost; Android/iOS block cleartext ws:// by default; background apps get suspended, so you need push notifications and resync on foreground.

C12. [M] What's SockJS and is it still needed? A WebSocket emulation library/protocol that falls back to HTTP streaming/polling when WebSocket is blocked (old proxies, IE). Spring supports it via .withSockJS(). Today it's rarely needed: WebSocket over wss://443 works almost everywhere. Keep it only if metrics show users on fallback transports. Fallback transports require sticky sessions.

C13. [S] What happens to a WebSocket when a laptop sleeps or a phone changes networks? The TCP connection usually dies silently (half-open). Without heart-beats the client may think it's connected for minutes. With heart-beats, the library detects missing traffic, closes, and reconnects; the app must then re-subscribe and resync missed data.

D. STOMP protocol

D1. [E] What is STOMP? Simple (or Streaming) Text Oriented Messaging Protocol: a simple, text-based, frame-oriented messaging protocol (HTTP-like frames) that defines commands such as CONNECT, SEND, SUBSCRIBE, ACK and MESSAGE, so clients and brokers in any language can interoperate. It runs over TCP or WebSocket.

D2. [E] Why use STOMP over WebSocket instead of raw WebSocket? WebSocket only moves messages; it has no notion of destinations, subscriptions, headers, acknowledgements, receipts or errors. STOMP standardises those, gives you broker interoperability (RabbitMQ, ActiveMQ), many client libraries, and lets Spring route messages to @MessageMapping methods. Without it you'd invent an ad-hoc protocol.

D3. [E] Describe the STOMP frame format.

COMMAND\n
header1:value1\n
header2:value2\n
\n
body\0

Command line, header lines, a blank line, an optional body, and a NULL byte terminator. In 1.2 lines can end with CRLF.

D4. [E] List client and server frames. Client: CONNECT (or STOMP), SEND, SUBSCRIBE, UNSUBSCRIBE, ACK, NACK, BEGIN, COMMIT, ABORT, DISCONNECT. Server: CONNECTED, MESSAGE, RECEIPT, ERROR.

D5. [M] What headers does CONNECT require in STOMP 1.2? accept-version and host. Optional: login, passcode, heart-beat. Custom headers (like Authorization) are allowed and are how token auth is usually done.

D6. [M] How is the version negotiated? Client lists versions in accept-version; server picks the highest common one and returns it in CONNECTED's version header. If there's none, the server sends ERROR with a version header listing supported versions and closes. If accept-version is absent, 1.0 is assumed.

D7. [M] Explain STOMP heart-beating and its negotiation. CONNECT has heart-beat:cx,cy (client can send every cx ms; wants to receive every cy ms); CONNECTED has sx,sy. Client→server interval = 0 if cx=0 or sy=0, else max(cx, sy). Server→client = 0 if sx=0 or cy=0, else max(sx, cy). A heart-beat is an EOL sent when idle; any traffic counts. If nothing arrives within the interval (plus tolerance), the peer is considered dead and the connection is closed.

D8. [M] Example: client heart-beat:5000,10000, server heart-beat:20000,0. Result? Client→server: sy=0 → no client heart-beats. Server→client: sx=20000, cy=10000 → max = 20000 ms.

D9. [M] What are the ack modes? auto (default; message considered delivered when sent), client (client must ACK; cumulative, so acking a message acks all earlier ones on that subscription), client-individual (must ACK each message individually; not cumulative). Unacked messages may be redelivered if the client disconnects.

D10. [M] How do ACK/NACK work in 1.2 vs 1.1? In 1.2, the MESSAGE carries an ack header, and ACK/NACK send id:<that value>. In 1.1, ACK/NACK used subscription and message-id headers. NACK (since 1.1) tells the broker the message wasn't consumed; the broker may redeliver, dead-letter or discard it.

D11. [M] What is a receipt? Any client frame except CONNECT can include receipt:<id>; the server replies with RECEIPT receipt-id:<id> after processing it. Since STOMP is stream-based, it also implies receipt of all earlier frames. Used for confirming sends, subscriptions, and graceful DISCONNECT. It doesn't confirm delivery to subscribers.

D12. [M] What happens when the server sends ERROR? It contains a message header and details in the body (and receipt-id if related to a frame with a receipt). The server must close the connection right after. So ERROR is fatal and shouldn't be used for ordinary business validation errors (send those as regular MESSAGEs to a user error queue).

D13. [M] What's the purpose of the id header in SUBSCRIBE? Required since 1.1. It uniquely identifies the subscription within the connection, enables multiple subscriptions to the same destination, appears in MESSAGE's subscription header so the client can dispatch, and is used by UNSUBSCRIBE.

D14. [M] What does content-length do? Why is it important? It gives the body size in bytes (octets). With it, the body may contain NULL bytes (binary). Without it, the body ends at the first NULL. A classic bug: computing it from string length (characters) breaks non-ASCII text.

D15. [M] How are special characters in headers handled? STOMP 1.1+ escapes them inside header names/values: a newline is written as the two characters \n, a colon as \c, a backslash as \\, and (1.2) a carriage return as \r. Any other escape (like \t) is a fatal protocol error. CONNECT and CONNECTED frames are not escaped (backward compatibility with 1.0). Header values aren't trimmed, so destination: /x has a value with a leading space.

D16. [M] What if a header is repeated? Only the first occurrence is used.

D17. [M] Which frames can have a body? Only SEND, MESSAGE and ERROR.

D18. [M] What are STOMP transactions? BEGIN transaction:tx1, then SEND/ACK/NACK frames with transaction:tx1 are buffered until COMMIT (applied atomically) or ABORT (discarded). Uncommitted transactions are aborted on DISCONNECT or connection failure. Broker support varies (Spring's simple broker doesn't support them).

D19. [M] How do you disconnect gracefully? Send DISCONNECT with a receipt, wait for the RECEIPT (guarantees earlier frames were received), then close the socket.

D20. [M] What's the STOMP command used for? An alias of CONNECT (1.1+), letting servers distinguish STOMP from HTTP on the same port. Most clients still send CONNECT for compatibility with 1.0 servers.

D21. [M] What's new in STOMP 1.1 and 1.2? 1.1: heart-beating, NACK, version negotiation, virtual hosting (host), header escaping/UTF-8, required SUBSCRIBE id, client-individual mode, STOMP frame. 1.2: CRLF line endings, simplified ACK/NACK via ack/id, \r escape, clarified repeated headers, connection lingering, clarified id scopes.

D22. [M] Does STOMP define what /topic and /queue mean? No. Destinations are opaque strings interpreted by the server/broker. /topic (fan-out) and /queue (point-to-point) are common conventions. RabbitMQ also has /exchange/..., /amq/queue/..., /temp-queue/.... Spring's simple broker treats all destinations as pub/sub.

D23. [S] Compare STOMP with MQTT and AMQP. STOMP: text, very simple, opaque destinations, ack modes, great for browsers/Spring. MQTT: compact binary, topic hierarchy with +/# wildcards, QoS 0/1/2, retained messages, last will; ideal for IoT/mobile. AMQP 0-9-1: binary, rich routing (exchanges, bindings), confirms; back-end messaging (RabbitMQ). AMQP 1.0: standardised enterprise messaging (Artemis, Azure Service Bus).

D24. [S] What are STOMP's limitations? Text overhead, ERROR is always fatal, no built-in request/reply correlation (conventions only), destination semantics vary by broker, no replay/history, spec unchanged since 2012, and no flow control beyond broker features like prefetch.

D25. [M] How does request/reply work over STOMP? By convention: the client sends with reply-to (e.g., a temp queue or user queue) and a correlation-id; the responder sends the reply there with the same correlation id. In Spring, @SendToUser(broadcast = false) or @SubscribeMapping covers most request/reply needs.

D26. [M] Can STOMP carry binary data? Yes: include content-length and an appropriate content-type, and preferably send as binary WebSocket frames. stompjs offers binaryBody.

E. Spring WebSocket / STOMP

E1. [E] How do you enable STOMP over WebSocket in Spring Boot? Add spring-boot-starter-websocket, create a @Configuration with @EnableWebSocketMessageBroker implementing WebSocketMessageBrokerConfigurer, register an endpoint (registry.addEndpoint("/ws")), set application prefixes (/app) and enable a broker (enableSimpleBroker("/topic", "/queue") or a relay).

E2. [E] What do setApplicationDestinationPrefixes and enableSimpleBroker do? Messages whose destination starts with the application prefix (e.g., /app) are routed to @MessageMapping methods. Messages and subscriptions with broker prefixes (e.g., /topic, /queue) go to the broker, which fans them out to subscribers.

E3. [M] Explain the three channels in Spring's STOMP support. clientInboundChannel carries messages from WebSocket clients; clientOutboundChannel carries messages to clients; brokerChannel carries messages from server-side application code to the broker. Inbound/outbound are backed by thread pools (2 × CPUs core size by default). Interceptors can be attached to them.

E4. [M] Walk through what happens when a client SENDs to /app/chat. SubProtocolWebSocketHandler receives the WebSocket message → StompSubProtocolHandler decodes it into a Message → clientInboundChannel (interceptors run) → SimpAnnotationMethodMessageHandler matches @MessageMapping("/chat") → payload converted (Jackson) → method invoked → return value sent to brokerChannel (to @SendTo or default /topic/chat) → broker finds subscribers → clientOutboundChannel → encoded STOMP MESSAGE → WebSocket.

E5. [M] What's the default destination of a @MessageMapping return value? The input destination with the application prefix replaced by /topic, e.g., /app/greeting → /topic/greeting. Override with @SendTo or @SendToUser.

E6. [M] @SendTo vs @SendToUser? @SendTo sends to a broker destination (all subscribers). @SendToUser sends to the current user's user destination (/user/queue/...): all their sessions by default, or only the originating session with broadcast = false. Without an authenticated user, @SendToUser falls back to the current session.

E7. [M] What's @SubscribeMapping used for? Handling SUBSCRIBE frames to application destinations (e.g., /app/rooms). The return value is sent directly back to that client via the outbound channel, not through the broker, which makes it ideal for loading initial data when a UI component subscribes.

E8. [M] How do you send a message from a REST controller or scheduled job? Inject SimpMessagingTemplate (or SimpMessageSendingOperations) and call convertAndSend("/topic/x", payload) or convertAndSendToUser(username, "/queue/x", payload).

E9. [M] Can SimpMessagingTemplate.convertAndSend("/app/x") trigger a @MessageMapping? No. The template sends to the brokerChannel; only client messages arriving on the inbound channel are routed to annotated handlers. Call the service directly instead.

E10. [M] Explain user destinations. Clients subscribe to /user/queue/x; Spring's UserDestinationMessageHandler translates it to a session-unique destination like /queue/x-user{sessionId}. Server code calls convertAndSendToUser("anna", "/queue/x", payload) → /user/anna/queue/x → resolved via SimpUserRegistry to all of anna's sessions. Requires a Principal whose getName() matches.

E11. [S] Why might convertAndSendToUser not deliver? Principal name mismatch (e.g., email vs id vs JWT sub), no Principal set on the session, client subscribed to /queue/x instead of /user/queue/x, user connected to another instance (need relay with user destination broadcast), wrong user prefix configuration, or the broker prefix (/queue) not enabled.

E12. [M] How do you get the current user in a @MessageMapping method? Add a java.security.Principal parameter (or @AuthenticationPrincipal with Spring Security's argument resolver), or read SimpMessageHeaderAccessor#getUser().

E13. [M] How do you access the session id and session attributes? SimpMessageHeaderAccessor#getSessionId() / @Header("simpSessionId"), and getSessionAttributes() for the per-session map (populated by handshake interceptors like HttpSessionHandshakeInterceptor or your code).

E14. [M] HandshakeInterceptor vs ChannelInterceptor? HandshakeInterceptor runs once during the HTTP upgrade (can reject with an HTTP status, copy attributes, inspect HTTP headers/cookies). ChannelInterceptor runs for every STOMP message on a channel (preSend, postSend, afterSendCompletion) and is used for CONNECT authentication, authorisation, logging, rate limiting.

E15. [M] Why must you use MessageHeaderAccessor.getAccessor(message, StompHeaderAccessor.class) in an interceptor rather than StompHeaderAccessor.wrap(message)? getAccessor returns the message's own mutable accessor, so setUser(...) changes the actual message/session. wrap creates a copy, so changes are lost unless you build and return a new message.

E16. [M] How do you handle exceptions in message handlers? @MessageExceptionHandler methods in the controller or a @ControllerAdvice (supported since 5.3), typically combined with @SendToUser("/queue/errors") to report errors to the client. Unhandled exceptions are only logged, so the client receives nothing. Exceptions in inbound interceptors become STOMP ERROR frames (customisable via StompSubProtocolErrorHandler).

E17. [M] How do you validate STOMP payloads? Annotate the payload @Valid/@Validated with Bean Validation on the classpath (or provide a validator via getValidator()), and handle org.springframework.messaging.handler.annotation.support.MethodArgumentNotValidException.

E18. [M] How do you configure heart-beats with the simple broker? registry.enableSimpleBroker("/topic","/queue").setTaskScheduler(scheduler).setHeartbeatValue(new long[]{10000,10000}). Without a TaskScheduler, the simple broker doesn't do heart-beats (it answers 0,0).

E19. [M] What transport limits does Spring have and what are the defaults? WebSocketTransportRegistration: messageSizeLimit 64 KB, sendBufferSizeLimit 512 KB, sendTimeLimit 10 s, timeToFirstMessage 60 s. Plus container limits (e.g., Tomcat 8 KB message buffers via ServletServerContainerFactoryBean).

E20. [S] What happens when a client is too slow to receive messages? Spring's ConcurrentWebSocketSessionDecorator buffers outbound messages per session; if the buffer exceeds sendBufferSizeLimit or a send takes longer than sendTimeLimit, the session is closed (status 4500 "session not reliable"). This protects server memory. The client reconnects and resyncs.

E21. [S] Is message order guaranteed in Spring STOMP? Not by default, because the inbound and outbound channels use thread pools. Enable MessageBrokerRegistry#setPreservePublishOrder(true) (5.1+) for outbound per-session order and StompEndpointRegistry#setPreserveReceiveOrder(true) (6.1+) for inbound per-session order; or use sequence numbers.

E22. [M] What session events does Spring publish? SessionConnectEvent, SessionConnectedEvent, SessionSubscribeEvent, SessionUnsubscribeEvent, SessionDisconnectEvent (may fire more than once per session, so handlers must be idempotent), and BrokerAvailabilityEvent.

E23. [M] How would you track online users? Listen to connected/disconnect events (count sessions per user, since a user can have many tabs), or query SimpUserRegistry. In a cluster use the multi-server user registry (relay userRegistryBroadcast) or Redis with TTLs. Publish presence changes to a topic.

E24. [M] Simple broker vs STOMP broker relay? Simple broker: in-memory, no setup, single instance only, subset of STOMP (no acks/transactions/limited receipts). Relay: forwards to RabbitMQ/ActiveMQ over TCP, supports clustering and full broker features, needs Reactor Netty, opens one TCP connection per client session plus a system connection.

E25. [M] What are clientLogin/systemLogin in the relay? clientLogin/Passcode: credentials the relay uses for each client session's TCP connection to the broker. systemLogin/Passcode: credentials for the shared system connection used to send messages from application code. End-user identity is not passed to the broker; Spring is the security boundary.

E26. [M] How do you use dot-separated destinations? registry.setPathMatcher(new AntPathMatcher(".")) so @MessageMapping("chat.room.{id}") matches /app/chat.room.42. Useful with RabbitMQ, whose topic routing keys use dots.

E27. [M] How do you add SockJS fallback? registry.addEndpoint("/ws").withSockJS(). Clients use new SockJS('https://host/ws') (via stompjs webSocketFactory). Native WebSocket clients then use /ws/websocket. Fallback transports need sticky sessions in clusters.

E28. [M] How do you configure allowed origins? addEndpoint("/ws").setAllowedOrigins("https://app.example.com") or setAllowedOriginPatterns("https://*.example.com"). Default is same origin only. Avoid * in production, especially with cookie auth.

E29. [S] How do you size Spring's channel thread pools? Inbound threads run your handlers: for blocking I/O handlers increase core size or make handlers async; the queue is unbounded by default, so with ThreadPoolTaskExecutor maxPoolSize only matters if you bound the queue. Outbound rarely needs tuning except with many slow clients. Monitor active threads and queue sizes via WebSocketMessageBrokerStats or Micrometer.

E30. [M] What does WebSocketMessageBrokerStats show? Periodic (30 min default) INFO logs with current WebSocket sessions by transport, total sessions, abnormal closes (connect failures, send limit, transport errors), STOMP frames processed (CONNECT/CONNECTED/DISCONNECT), relay status, and inbound/outbound/SockJS thread pool stats.

E31. [M] How do you return a value asynchronously from a @MessageMapping? Return a CompletableFuture/ListenableFuture (or reactive types); Spring sends the result when it completes, freeing the inbound thread.

E32. [M] How do you customise JSON conversion? Spring Boot uses its auto-configured ObjectMapper for messaging. For custom converters, override configureMessageConverters (return true to keep defaults). Clients should send content-type:application/json.

E33. [S] How do you send to a specific session (not a user)? Create headers with SimpMessageHeaderAccessor.create(SimpMessageType.MESSAGE), setSessionId(id), setLeaveMutable(true), and call convertAndSendToUser(sessionId, "/queue/x", payload, accessor.getMessageHeaders()). The client subscribes to /user/queue/x.

E34. [M] How do you write an integration test for a STOMP endpoint? @SpringBootTest(webEnvironment = RANDOM_PORT), a WebSocketStompClient with a JSON converter, connectAsync to ws://localhost:{port}/ws with auth headers, subscribe with a StompFrameHandler that pushes to a BlockingQueue, send, and poll with a timeout. Avoid subscribe/send races with receipts or a round-trip.

E35. [M] Is Spring's STOMP support available in WebFlux? No built-in STOMP broker/annotation support in WebFlux. WebFlux has a reactive WebSocketHandler for raw messages; for higher-level reactive messaging consider RSocket.

E36. [S] Explain SubProtocolWebSocketHandler. The WebSocketHandler registered for STOMP endpoints. It delegates to sub-protocol handlers by negotiated Sec-WebSocket-Protocol (STOMP by default), manages session lifecycle, wraps sessions in ConcurrentWebSocketSessionDecorator with buffer/time limits, checks timeToFirstMessage, and closes sessions on shutdown.

E37. [M] What's SimpMessageType? The Spring-level message type in headers: CONNECT, CONNECT_ACK, MESSAGE, SUBSCRIBE, UNSUBSCRIBE, HEARTBEAT, DISCONNECT, DISCONNECT_ACK, OTHER. Security matchers (e.g., simpTypeMatchers) use it.

E38. [M] How do you access a custom STOMP header the client sent with SEND? @Header("myHeader") String v (Spring checks top-level then native headers) or StompHeaderAccessor#getFirstNativeHeader("myHeader").

E39. [M] Can you use @PreAuthorize on @MessageMapping methods? Yes, with method security enabled; Spring Security's SecurityContextChannelInterceptor populates the SecurityContext from the message's user during handling.

E40. [S] What happens on application shutdown? The WebSocket infrastructure stops and SubProtocolWebSocketHandler closes open sessions (status 1001 Going Away). Clients reconnect to other instances. Combine with readiness probes, preStop delays and jittered reconnects for smooth rollouts.

E41. [M] How do you raise the max message size for big payloads? registration.setMessageSizeLimit(...) in configureWebSocketTransport, and container buffers via ServletServerContainerFactoryBean#setMaxTextMessageBufferSize. But prefer sending references and fetching large data over HTTP.

E42. [S] How does the selector header work in the simple broker and why is it sensitive? A SUBSCRIBE selector header holds a SpEL expression evaluated against message headers to filter deliveries. It caused CVE-2018-1270/1275 (SpEL injection → RCE in old versions, fixed by using SimpleEvaluationContext). Configure the header name with setSelectorHeaderName, or disable it (null) if unused.

E43. [M] What's @DestinationVariable? Extracts template variables from the message destination, e.g., @MessageMapping("/rooms/{roomId}") → @DestinationVariable String roomId. Validate it, since it's client input.

E44. [M] Where does the Principal of a WebSocket session come from? From the HTTP handshake request (request.getPrincipal() via Spring Security / DefaultHandshakeHandler#determineUser), or set on the STOMP CONNECT message by an interceptor (accessor.setUser). Spring then associates it with the session for subsequent messages.

E45. [M] What is SimpUserRegistry? A registry of connected users, their sessions and subscriptions (getUser, getUsers, getUserCount, findSubscriptions). Default local implementation; a multi-server view is available with the relay's user registry broadcast.

Continue to Part 2.

Chapter 12bInterviews

Interview Questions & Answers, Part 2

Levels: [E] entry, [M] mid, [S] senior/lead. Part 1 covered fundamentals, the WebSocket protocol, the browser API, STOMP and Spring.

F. Security

F1. [E] How do you secure a WebSocket connection? Use wss:// (TLS), validate the Origin header, authenticate (handshake session or token in STOMP CONNECT), authorise every SUBSCRIBE and SEND, validate input, limit sizes/rates/connections, and avoid leaking tokens in URLs/logs.

F2. [M] What is Cross-Site WebSocket Hijacking (CSWSH)? A malicious site opens a WebSocket to your server from the victim's browser; since CORS doesn't apply to WebSockets and the browser may attach cookies, a cookie-authenticated socket gets established on the attacker's behalf, letting them read/send messages as the victim. Prevent it with Origin allow-lists, token-based auth in the CONNECT frame (not cookies alone), SameSite cookies, and CSRF tokens on CONNECT (Spring Security's default).

F3. [M] How do you authenticate STOMP clients with JWT in Spring? Client sends Authorization: Bearer <jwt> as a CONNECT header. A ChannelInterceptor on clientInboundChannel checks StompCommand.CONNECT, decodes/validates the JWT (JwtDecoder), converts it to an Authentication, and calls accessor.setUser(auth) using the accessor from MessageHeaderAccessor.getAccessor. The config class gets @Order(Ordered.HIGHEST_PRECEDENCE + 99) so it runs before Spring Security's message authorisation. Spring keeps the user for the session.

F4. [M] Why not put the token in the WebSocket URL? URLs are logged by proxies, load balancers, servers and may stay in browser history; leaked tokens are replayable. If you must (e.g., gateway auth), use short-lived, single-use tickets.

F5. [M] How do you authorise subscriptions? With Spring Security message rules (simpSubscribeDestMatchers(...)) for coarse checks, plus a custom ChannelInterceptor on SUBSCRIBE that parses the destination (e.g., /topic/orders.{id}) and verifies ownership/membership. Deny by default, block wildcard subscriptions, and handle revocation (close sessions or stop delivery when membership changes).

F6. [M] How would you stop clients from publishing directly to /topic/...? Allow client SEND only to /app/** (Spring Security simpDestMatchers("/app/**")... + simpTypeMatchers(MESSAGE, SUBSCRIBE).denyAll() + anyMessage().denyAll()), so all broadcasts come from server code after validation.

F7. [M] How do you deal with token expiry for long-lived connections? Store expiry in session attributes at CONNECT; reject inbound messages after expiry; close sessions server-side when the token expires or is revoked (track WebSocketSessions via a decorator factory); enforce a maximum connection age; client refreshes tokens in beforeConnect and reconnects.

F8. [M] What is CSRF protection in Spring WebSocket security? With @EnableWebSocketSecurity, Spring Security requires a valid CSRF token in the CONNECT frame headers by default, protecting cookie-authenticated sessions against CSWSH. Clients obtain the token from the server and add it to connectHeaders. For pure header-token auth you can configure the security interceptors manually without the CSRF interceptor.

F9. [M] Should you trust the sender field in a chat message payload? Never. Set identity on the server from the authenticated Principal; ignore or overwrite client-supplied identity fields.

F10. [S] What DoS vectors exist for WebSocket servers and how do you mitigate them? Connection floods (LB/IP limits, per-user session caps), idle unauthenticated sockets (timeToFirstMessage, auth deadline), huge frames (messageSizeLimit, container buffers), message floods (per-session rate limits), subscription floods (caps, no wildcards), slow readers (send buffer/time limits), expensive handlers (bounded pools, timeouts), reconnect storms (client jitter, 1013 close code).

F11. [M] How do you prevent XSS through chat messages? Treat message content as untrusted: render as text (React escapes by default), sanitise any HTML (DOMPurify), validate length/charset on the server, and use a Content Security Policy.

F12. [S] What known vulnerabilities affected Spring's STOMP support? CVE-2018-1270 (and CVE-2018-1275 for an incomplete 4.3.x fix): the simple broker evaluated the SUBSCRIBE selector header as SpEL with a full evaluation context, allowing remote code execution. Fixed by using SimpleEvaluationContext (5.0.5 / 4.3.16+). Lesson: patch dependencies and disable unused features.

F13. [S] Is checking the Origin header enough? No. It stops browser-based CSWSH but not non-browser clients (who can forge Origin). You still need real authentication and authorisation. Defence in depth: Origin + token auth + per-destination authorisation + rate limits.

F14. [M] Does the broker relay pass the end user's identity to RabbitMQ? No. The relay uses configured clientLogin/systemLogin credentials. The Spring application must enforce user-level security before forwarding frames. Never expose the broker's STOMP port publicly.

F15. [S] How do you isolate tenants in a multi-tenant real-time system? Derive tenant from the authenticated token (not payload), include tenant id in destination naming (/topic/t.{tenant}.…), authorise tenant on every SUBSCRIBE/SEND, separate vhosts/brokers for strict isolation, per-tenant rate limits and metrics.

F16. [M] What should never appear in logs for a STOMP system? CONNECT Authorization/passcode headers, cookies, full message bodies with PII. Disable verbose client debug logging in production.

G. Brokers, clustering & scaling

G1. [E] Why doesn't the simple broker work with multiple server instances? Each instance has its own in-memory subscription registry; a message published on instance A only reaches clients connected to A. Use an external broker relay or a pub/sub backplane.

G2. [M] How does the STOMP broker relay work? Spring opens a TCP connection to the broker for each client session (forwarding the client's frames for broker destinations and returning MESSAGE frames) and one shared "system" connection for messages sent by server code. It requires Reactor Netty and publishes BrokerAvailabilityEvent on connectivity changes.

G3. [M] Which brokers support STOMP? RabbitMQ (STOMP plugin, port 61613; Web STOMP plugin, port 15674 /ws), ActiveMQ Classic, ActiveMQ Artemis, Apache Apollo (retired), plus others. Amazon MQ offers managed ActiveMQ/RabbitMQ.

G4. [M] Explain RabbitMQ STOMP destinations. /exchange/<name>[/<pattern>] (publish to/bind from an exchange), /queue/<name> (queue created if needed; competing consumers), /amq/queue/<name> (existing queue), /topic/<routing-key> (via amq.topic, wildcards * and # with dot-separated words), /temp-queue/<name> (temporary reply queues).

G5. [M] How do user destinations work across a cluster? Configure the relay with userDestinationBroadcast (unresolved user messages are broadcast so the instance holding the user's session delivers them) and userRegistryBroadcast (instances share their user registries → multi-server SimpUserRegistry).

G6. [S] The relay opens one TCP connection per client. What if you have 500k users? That's 500k broker connections: heavy for any broker. Alternatives: backplane pattern (simple broker per node + Redis/Kafka fan-out between nodes), shard users across broker clusters, or a dedicated real-time gateway that consumes from Kafka with a small number of connections and fans out locally.

G7. [M] Describe a Redis-based backplane. Each instance runs a simple broker for its own clients and subscribes to a Redis pub/sub channel. To broadcast, an instance publishes to Redis; every instance receives it and calls SimpMessagingTemplate locally. Redis pub/sub is at-most-once (missed during disconnects); use Redis Streams/Kafka for replay; shard channels for scale.

G8. [M] Do WebSockets need sticky sessions? Not for pure WebSocket, because a connection stays on the backend it was established with. Sticky sessions are needed for SockJS HTTP fallback transports (many HTTP requests per logical session) and helpful if you keep server-side state that survives reconnects.

G9. [M] How do you configure Nginx for WebSocket? proxy_http_version 1.1; proxy_set_header Upgrade $http_upgrade; proxy_set_header Connection $connection_upgrade; (with a map on $http_upgrade), long proxy_read_timeout/proxy_send_timeout, proxy_buffering off, forward Host/X-Forwarded headers.

G10. [M] Why do connections drop every 60 seconds behind a load balancer? The LB/proxy idle timeout (60 s default for Nginx and AWS ALB) closes connections without traffic. Fix with heart-beats shorter than the timeout and/or longer timeouts.

G11. [S] How do you autoscale WebSocket servers? CPU is a poor signal (idle connections cost memory). Scale on connections per pod, memory, or message throughput via custom metrics. Since existing connections don't rebalance to new pods, use least-connection balancing and optionally a max connection age with jitter to gradually rebalance.

G12. [S] How do you deploy WebSocket servers without disrupting users? Rolling deploys with readiness gating and preStop delays (stop new connections, then close existing ones with 1001), clients reconnect with jitter and resync, keep payloads backward compatible across versions, add capacity headroom, canary new versions and watch close codes/connection success rates.

G13. [M] What OS limits matter for many connections? File descriptors (ulimit -n), ephemeral port ranges for proxy→backend connections, somaxconn/SYN backlog for reconnect bursts, memory for socket buffers and TLS, container/pod limits, Tomcat max-connections (8192 default in Spring Boot).

G14. [S] Estimate capacity for a broadcast-heavy system. Deliveries/s = publishes/s × subscribers per message. E.g., 50 msgs/s to a 20k-subscriber topic = 1M deliveries/s → network bandwidth (1M × message size) and outbound processing dominate. Mitigate with conflation, batching, smaller payloads, sharding audiences, and more nodes.

G15. [M] How does RabbitMQ prefetch relate to STOMP? The SUBSCRIBE prefetch-count header limits unacknowledged messages delivered to a consumer when using client/client-individual ack, giving backpressure and fair dispatch among competing consumers.

G16. [S] How would you avoid per-session queue leaks with RabbitMQ user destinations? Use destinations RabbitMQ creates as auto-delete (e.g., /exchange/amq.direct/<name> for user destinations, per Spring's docs) or apply policies with x-expires; monitor queue counts.

G17. [M] Can browsers connect directly to RabbitMQ Web STOMP? Yes, but you lose Spring's application layer (@MessageMapping, user destinations, custom authorisation), expose the broker publicly, and must manage broker-level auth (e.g., OAuth2 plugin) and permissions per user. Most enterprises keep a gateway in front.

G18. [S] What changes when moving from a single node to multi-region? Users connect to the nearest region; cross-region messages need replication (federation/shovel in RabbitMQ, Kafka MirrorMaker, or a global pub/sub); latency and ordering across regions; data residency; presence aggregation; failover strategies (DNS/GSLB) with client reconnect.

H. Reliability & performance

H1. [M] What delivery guarantee does STOMP over WebSocket give? By default effectively at-most-once: messages are lost if the client is disconnected or the simple broker restarts. At-least-once requires a durable broker queue, client acks and redelivery, and idempotent consumers; "exactly-once" is achieved as at-least-once + deduplication.

H2. [S] How do you make sure clients don't miss updates during disconnects? Persist state/events in a database with per-entity sequence numbers; clients track the last seq; on reconnect or gap detection they fetch missed events (REST ?after=seq) or a snapshot; WebSocket messages act as notifications. Subscribe before fetching the snapshot and merge by seq.

H3. [M] How do you handle duplicate messages? Give each event a unique id and/or per-entity sequence; consumers ignore ids already processed or seqs ≤ last applied. For commands, the client sends an idempotency key (clientRequestId) and the server dedupes.

H4. [S] How do you avoid publishing events for transactions that roll back? Publish after commit (@TransactionalEventListener(phase = AFTER_COMMIT)) or use the transactional outbox pattern with a relay/CDC for at-least-once publication.

H5. [M] What's backpressure in WebSocket systems? Mechanisms to cope when a receiver can't keep up. Browser send() doesn't block (watch bufferedAmount); servers buffer per session. Spring bounds buffers (sendBufferSizeLimit, sendTimeLimit) and closes slow sessions. Application strategies: conflation, batching, deltas, dropping non-critical updates, broker prefetch.

H6. [M] What is conflation? Sending only the latest value per key at a fixed interval instead of every intermediate update (e.g., price ticks), reducing load and helping slow clients.

H7. [M] How do you choose heart-beat intervals? Shorter than the smallest idle timeout in the path (proxies, LBs, NAT), typically 10–25 s; balance faster failure detection against traffic/battery cost; mobile may need shorter intervals due to NAT timeouts.

H8. [S] Why might maxPoolSize have no effect on Spring's inbound channel executor? ThreadPoolTaskExecutor creates threads beyond corePoolSize only when the queue is full; the default queue is unbounded, so the pool never grows. Increase corePoolSize or bound the queue (with a rejection strategy).

H9. [M] What metrics would you monitor? Active sessions, connects/disconnects per second by close code, CONNECT failures, inbound/outbound message rates and sizes, executor active threads/queue sizes, send-limit closes, handler latency, end-to-end latency, broker health (connections, queue depth, unacked, memory), relay availability.

H10. [M] How would you load test a STOMP server? k6/Gatling/Artillery/JMeter sending raw STOMP frames over WebSocket, or Java clients with WebSocketStompClient. Simulate realistic idle/active mixes, subscriptions, fan-out, reconnect storms, slow clients; run soak tests for leaks; distribute load generators to avoid client-side port/fd limits; measure p95/p99 latency.

H11. [S] JSON vs binary payloads: when to switch? When message rate × size makes bandwidth/CPU significant (market data, telemetry, games). Protobuf/MessagePack reduce size and parse time; send as binary frames with an appropriate content-type and a custom MessageConverter. Otherwise JSON's debuggability wins.

H12. [M] Does enabling compression always help? No. It helps large text payloads and bandwidth-limited clients but costs CPU and per-connection memory (significant at 100k+ connections) and can introduce side-channel risk. Measure.

H13. [S] How do you ensure ordering for a chat room across a cluster? Assign a per-room sequence number at write time (DB sequence/row version, or a single partition per room in Kafka); deliver in that order (partition by room); clients reorder/dedupe by seq and resync on gaps. Enable Spring's publish ordering per session.

H14. [M] How do you build presence ("online" status) reliably? Track sessions per user (multiple tabs/devices); store in Redis with TTL refreshed periodically or on activity; mark offline when the last session disconnects or TTL expires (handles crashes where disconnect events never fire); publish only changes; limit presence fan-out to relevant users.

I. Debugging & scenario questions

I1. [M] Users report messages stop arriving after about a minute of inactivity. What's going on? Almost certainly an idle timeout (proxy/LB, e.g., 60 s) killing silent connections. Check close codes (1006 at ~60 s), heart-beat negotiation (maybe 0,0 because the simple broker has no TaskScheduler), and proxy timeouts. Fix: enable heart-beats (10–25 s), raise timeouts, and make sure clients reconnect and resync.

I2. [M] It works locally but in production the handshake fails with 400/200. Why? The reverse proxy/ingress isn't forwarding Upgrade/Connection or uses HTTP/1.0 upstream, or the SPA's fallback route returns index.html (200) for /ws. Fix proxy config and routing order.

I3. [M] After deploying a second instance, some users miss messages. Why? The simple broker is per-instance. Users connected to instance B don't get messages published on instance A. Use a broker relay or backplane; for user destinations, enable user destination broadcast.

I4. [M] convertAndSendToUser works for some users but not others. Check that Principal#getName() matches the name passed (e.g., some users' principal is email, others id), that those users have a Principal at all, that they subscribed to /user/queue/..., and whether they're connected to another instance.

I5. [M] The React app shows every message twice. Duplicate subscriptions: multiple client instances, subscribing on every render, subscribing both in onConnect and in components, StrictMode double effects without cleanup, or reconnect without unsubscribing old handlers. Use a singleton client with ref-counted subscriptions and cleanup.

I6. [M] Mobile (React Native) app connects but never gets CONNECTED. NULL-terminator truncation in RN's WebSocket. Set forceBinaryWSFrames and appendMissingNULLonIncoming. Also check TextEncoder availability, cleartext restrictions and host addresses.

I7. [S] Server memory keeps growing during peak hours and the app eventually crashes. Suspect slow clients buffering outbound messages (check send limits), unbounded inbound queue due to blocking handlers, maps keyed by session not cleaned on disconnect, or subscriptions never unsubscribed. Take heap dumps, check WebSocketMessageBrokerStats queue sizes, configure send limits, bound queues, conflate high-frequency topics.

I8. [M] Clients get an ERROR frame immediately after CONNECT. An inbound interceptor threw: invalid/expired token, missing CSRF token with @EnableWebSocketSecurity, interceptor ordering (Spring Security authorisation running before your auth interceptor), or unauthorised destination. Read the ERROR message header; check server logs.

I9. [S] After a deploy, the system collapses under reconnects. Thundering herd: all clients reconnect simultaneously with a fixed delay, overwhelming remaining instances, auth services (JWKS/IdP) and snapshot REST endpoints. Mitigate with exponential backoff + jitter, rolling deploys, capacity headroom, caching of snapshots, rate limiting reconnects, 1013 "try again later".

I10. [M] Messages arrive out of order in the UI. Thread-pool-based channels can reorder. Enable setPreservePublishOrder(true) / setPreserveReceiveOrder(true) or include sequence numbers and reorder/drop stale events client-side.

I11. [M] A big JSON message closes the connection with 1009. It exceeds the container buffer (Tomcat 8 KB default per message) or Spring's messageSizeLimit. Raise limits deliberately or redesign to send references.

I12. [S] Only one corporate customer can't connect. Their proxy/firewall/TLS inspection blocks or breaks WebSocket. Confirm via their close codes and network logs; ensure wss:// on 443; offer SockJS fallback or polling; check their Origin (embedded portals) and IP allow-lists.

I13. [M] @MessageMapping method isn't invoked even though the client sends. Destination prefix mismatch (/chat vs /app/chat), path separator mismatch (dots vs slashes without AntPathMatcher(".")), controller not scanned, or security rules silently dropping the message.

I14. [M] User removed from a project still receives its updates. Authorisation happened only at SUBSCRIBE. Implement revocation: on membership change, close the user's sessions (forcing re-subscribe with checks) or route updates via per-user delivery that re-checks membership.

J. System design questions (with answer outlines)

J1. [S] Design a chat system like WhatsApp/Slack. Requirements: 1:1 and group chat, delivery/read receipts, presence, typing, history, offline delivery, multi-device.

  • Connection layer: real-time gateway (STOMP over WebSocket), JWT on CONNECT, heart-beats, LB with long timeouts.
  • Write path: client sends /app/chat.send with clientMsgId → message service validates membership → persists with per-conversation seq (DB/Cassandra) → event to Kafka (partition by conversationId).
  • Fan-out: gateway consumers deliver to /topic/conv.{id} or per-user queues; offline recipients get push notifications.
  • Reliability: dedupe by clientMsgId, ordered by seq, resync history via REST on reconnect, delivery receipts as batched "delivered/read up to seq".
  • Presence/typing: Redis TTL, ephemeral, rate-limited.
  • Scale: shard gateways, Kafka partitions, conflation of typing/presence, hot group handling (large channels → pull model).
  • Security: membership checks on subscribe/send, E2E encryption discussion, XSS, rate limits.

J2. [S] Design a real-time notification service for 10M users.
