Producers emit domain events → notification service applies preferences → persists to an inbox (for unread counts/history) → pushes in-app via gateway user destinations if online (presence lookup) else mobile push/email. Gateways scale horizontally with a backplane (Kafka/Redis keyed by userId → gateway node mapping in Redis to avoid broadcasting to all nodes). Clients on connect fetch unread count via REST.

J3. [S] Design a live sports score / stock ticker for millions of viewers. Huge fan-out, read-only, latest-value semantics. Use conflation at the source (e.g., 1–4 updates/s), a hierarchical fan-out (origin → regional relays/edges → gateways), small binary/delta payloads, CDN-backed snapshots for initial state, SSE or WebSocket depending on the need for client→server; drop intermediate updates for slow clients; no per-user state.

J4. [S] Design collaborative document editing. Use OT or CRDTs (Yjs/Automerge) for merging; WebSocket for low-latency sync of operations; server persists operation log/snapshots; awareness (cursors) as ephemeral, conflated messages; per-document routing (all collaborators of a document to the same node or a document actor); offline edits merged via CRDT on reconnect. STOMP can transport updates but many CRDT libraries ship their own protocols.

J5. [S] Design live order tracking for a food delivery app. Driver location updates via app (HTTP or WS) every few seconds → location service (Redis GEO, latest-only) → publish to /topic/order.{id}.location (authorised to customer/restaurant). Order status changes from order service events. Conflation, battery-friendly intervals, push notifications for status changes when app is backgrounded, resync on foreground.

J6. [S] Design an online auction/bidding system. Bids via REST/command with idempotency key; strong consistency for the highest bid (single writer per auction: DB row lock or partitioned actor); broadcast new highest bid with seq to /topic/auction.{id}; clients show server-confirmed state; end-of-auction timing on the server; anti-sniping extension rules; rate limits; audit log.

J7. [S] Your simple-broker Spring app must scale from 1 to 20 instances. Plan the migration. Choose relay (RabbitMQ) vs backplane (Redis/Kafka) based on connection counts and features; switch destinations/naming (dots); configure user destination & registry broadcast or redesign presence in Redis; LB/proxy timeouts; remove local in-memory state; load test; staged rollout with metrics.

K. Coding / whiteboard tasks

K1. [M] Write the Spring config for a STOMP endpoint with a simple broker and heart-beats. See Chapter 5 §5.8. Key lines: @EnableWebSocketMessageBroker, addEndpoint("/ws").setAllowedOriginPatterns(...), setApplicationDestinationPrefixes("/app"), enableSimpleBroker("/topic","/queue").setTaskScheduler(ts).setHeartbeatValue(new long[]{10000,10000}).

K2. [M] Write a CONNECT-frame JWT interceptor. See Chapter 8 §8.5.2. Mention getAccessor, StompCommand.CONNECT, getFirstNativeHeader("Authorization"), setUser, exception → ERROR, @Order(HIGHEST_PRECEDENCE + 99).

K3. [M] Write a React hook that subscribes to a destination and cleans up. See Chapter 6 §6.6. Mention singleton client, useEffect cleanup, ref for callback, re-subscription on reconnect.

K4. [M] Write the STOMP frames to connect, subscribe and send.

CONNECT
accept-version:1.2
host:example.com
heart-beat:10000,10000

^@
SUBSCRIBE
id:sub-0
destination:/topic/news

^@
SEND
destination:/app/news.post
content-type:application/json
content-length:15

{"title":"Hi!"}^@

(content-length = 15 bytes for {"title":"Hi!"}.)

K5. [S] Implement exponential backoff with jitter.

ts
const delay = Math.random() * Math.min(maxDelay, base * 2 ** attempt);   // "full jitter"

Reset attempt on successful connect; stop on permanent auth errors.

K6. [S] Compute the Sec-WebSocket-Accept for a given key. base64(sha1(key + "258EAFA5-E914-47DA-95CA-C5AB0DC85B11")), e.g., key dGhlIHNhbXBsZSBub25jZQ== → s3pPLMBiTxaQ9kYGzzhZRbK+xOo=.

K7. [S] Parse a WebSocket frame header.

java
int b0 = buf[0] & 0xFF, b1 = buf[1] & 0xFF;
boolean fin = (b0 & 0x80) != 0; int opcode = b0 & 0x0F;
boolean masked = (b1 & 0x80) != 0; long len = b1 & 0x7F; int off = 2;
if (len == 126) { len = ((buf[2] & 0xFF) << 8) | (buf[3] & 0xFF); off = 4; }
else if (len == 127) { len = 0; for (int i = 2; i < 10; i++) len = (len << 8) | (buf[i] & 0xFF); off = 10; }
byte[] mask = masked ? Arrays.copyOfRange(buf, off, off + 4) : null; if (masked) off += 4;
// payload[i] ^= mask[i % 4]

K8. [S] Write a subscription authorisation interceptor for /topic/orders.{id}. See Chapter 8 §8.6.3: regex the destination, look up ownership, throw AccessDeniedException (or drop + error queue).

L. Behavioural / experience questions (prepare your stories)

L1. Tell me about a real-time feature you built. Structure: problem → why WebSocket/STOMP → architecture (gateway, broker, auth) → hardest problem (e.g., missed updates on reconnect → seq + resync) → results (latency, load reduction) → what you'd do differently.

L2. Describe a production incident with WebSockets. E.g., mass disconnects every 60 s after an infra change (new LB with default idle timeout) → detected via close-code dashboards → mitigated by raising timeouts → fixed permanently with heart-beats and alerting → postmortem: add LB config to IaC review checklist.

L3. How did you scale a real-time system? Simple broker → RabbitMQ relay or Redis backplane, capacity tests, metrics-driven autoscaling.

L4. How did you ensure security? CSWSH protection, JWT on CONNECT, destination authorisation with ownership, pen-test findings fixed.

L5. How do you onboard other teams to your real-time platform? SDKs, AsyncAPI docs, destination naming rules, review process, examples, runbooks.

M. Rapid-fire (one-liners)

N. Tricky "gotcha" questions

  1. "Is Sec-WebSocket-Key used for security?" No; it only proves protocol understanding.
  2. "Does masking encrypt data?" No; it prevents proxy cache poisoning. TLS encrypts.
  3. "Can the server send a 1006 close code?" No; 1005, 1006 and 1015 are reserved for local reporting.
  4. "Does a STOMP RECEIPT mean subscribers got the message?" No; only that the server processed the frame.
  5. "Will the simple broker redeliver unacked messages?" No; it doesn't support acks.
  6. "Does @SubscribeMapping's return value go through the broker?" No; directly to the subscribing client (unless @SendTo).
  7. "Do STOMP heart-beats use WebSocket ping frames?" No; they're EOL bytes inside the WebSocket data stream.
  8. "Can two subscriptions on one connection share an id?" No; ids must be unique per connection.
  9. "Can ws.close(1001) be called from browser JS?" No; only 1000 or 3000–4999.
  10. "If the client sends destination: /topic/a (with space), what's the destination?" " /topic/a": values aren't trimmed.
  11. "Does SessionDisconnectEvent fire exactly once?" Not guaranteed; can fire multiple times.
  12. "Does HTTP/2 Server Push replace WebSockets?" No (and it's deprecated in browsers).
  13. "Will TCP tell me immediately when the client disappears?" No; half-open connections need heart-beats.
  14. "Does maxPoolSize grow Spring's inbound pool?" Not with the default unbounded queue.
  15. "Is SockJS a WebSocket implementation?" It's an emulation layer with its own protocol, using WebSocket when possible.
  16. "Does turning on setPreserveReceiveOrder(true) change error behaviour?" Yes (Spring 7.0.x): exceptions from inbound interceptors are only logged, so no ERROR frame is sent and the connection stays open.
  17. "What close code does a Spring STOMP client see after an ERROR frame?" 1002 (PROTOCOL_ERROR); Spring closes the session after sending ERROR.
  18. "What happens if stompjs's beforeConnect throws?" In 7.x the rejection is unhandled and no further reconnect is scheduled, so always catch inside it.

O. More questions (added after a research pass on common interview topics)

O1. [E] People call WebSocket "TCP for the web" and STOMP "HTTP for the web". What does that mean? WebSocket gives browsers a raw, bidirectional message pipe (like TCP gives programs a byte pipe). STOMP adds HTTP-like structure on top (a command, headers and a body), so both sides agree on what a message means and where it goes.

O2. [E] Walk me through how /app/** messages reach a controller. The client SENDs to /app/chat.send. Spring sees the application prefix /app, strips it, and matches the remainder (/chat.send) against @MessageMapping patterns. The payload is converted by a MessageConverter and the method is invoked on a clientInboundChannel thread.

O3. [M] How does Spring find the "right connection" when you call convertAndSendToUser? It doesn't look up a socket directly. It sends to /user/{name}/queue/x; UserDestinationMessageHandler asks SimpUserRegistry for the sessions of {name} and rewrites the destination to each session's unique queue (/queue/x-user{sessionId}). The broker delivers to the subscription the client created by subscribing to /user/queue/x.

O4. [M] Which Spring component sends STOMP heart-beats on the server? The broker: SimpleBrokerMessageHandler (when a TaskScheduler is configured) or the external broker via the relay. There is no "HeartbeatInterceptor" class in Spring's STOMP support (see section P).

O5. [M] What changed for WebSocket/STOMP in Spring Framework 7 / Spring Security 7? MappingJackson2MessageConverter is deprecated for removal and replaced by the Jackson 3 based JacksonJsonMessageConverter. Spring Security 7 removed AbstractSecurityWebSocketMessageBrokerConfigurer and MessageSecurityMetadataSourceRegistry, so you must use @EnableWebSocketSecurity with an AuthorizationManager<Message<?>>. (Spring 6.2 had already added ChannelRegistration.executor(Executor) for custom executors such as virtual threads.)

O6. [M] How do you run Spring's message channels on virtual threads? Since 6.2: registration.executor(new VirtualThreadTaskExecutor("ws-in-")) in configureClientInboundChannel (and similarly for outbound). Load test it, and remember ordering still needs the preserve-order flags.

O7. [M] What happens when the simple broker's subscriber is too slow? Messages queue in ConcurrentWebSocketSessionDecorator. Spring uses its TERMINATE overflow strategy and closes the session with CloseStatus.SESSION_NOT_RELIABLE (4500) when the send time or buffer limit is exceeded. The decorator also has a DROP strategy if you use it directly.

O8. [M] Does Spring Security protect outbound messages? No. It secures only the clientInboundChannel; you protect data by authorising SUBSCRIBE frames. That's a performance decision, since one inbound message can fan out to thousands of outbound ones.

O9. [M] Why should clients be denied SUBSCRIBE to /queue/** directly? Because user destinations resolve to /queue/...-user{sessionId} names. A client allowed to subscribe to /queue/** (or guess those names) could read other users' private messages. Allow /user/queue/** and deny the raw broker prefix.

O10. [M] How do typing indicators usually work in a chat design? They are ephemeral events sent over the existing socket, rate-limited, never persisted. Server-side they're often a short-TTL key (e.g., 5 s in Redis) so no explicit "stopped typing" event is needed. They're the first thing to shed under load.

O11. [M] How is presence implemented at scale? A per-user key with a short TTL refreshed by heart-beats/activity (offline when it expires, which handles crashes that never fire disconnect events), published as change events only to interested users. A user→gateway-node map (e.g., in Redis) lets you route private messages to the right node instead of broadcasting to all nodes.

O12. [S] How many WebSocket connections can one server handle? There's no single number: it depends on memory per connection (kernel buffers, container buffers such as Tomcat's 8 KB per-session message buffers, framework objects, your state), message rate and fan-out. Public write-ups quote tens to hundreds of thousands per node. In an interview, state assumptions (e.g., ~20–50 KB per connection → ~20k–50k per GB of heap) and say you'd measure with a load test. Also mention OS limits (file descriptors), Tomcat's max-connections (Spring Boot default 8192) and ephemeral ports.

O13. [S] With 10 million users, how do you route a message to the one gateway node holding the recipient's socket? Keep a connection registry (userId → nodeId, with TTL) in Redis or a similar store. Producers publish to that node's channel/partition, not to all nodes. Alternatives: consistent hashing of users to gateway clusters, or Kafka partitions keyed by userId with each gateway consuming its assigned partitions.

O14. [M] What does wasClean in the browser close event mean? true if the closing handshake completed (close frames exchanged); false if the connection dropped (typically code 1006).

O15. [M] Your WebSocket works through Nginx but drops at exactly 60 s, even with Nginx timeouts raised. What else? Another hop: an AWS ALB in front (default idle timeout 60 s, configurable 1–4000 s), a corporate proxy, or the backend container's idle timeout. Heart-beats shorter than the smallest idle timeout fix all of them at once.

O16. [M] What are AWS API Gateway WebSocket limits worth knowing? 2-hour max connection duration, 10-minute idle timeout, 128 KB message payload, 32 KB frame size (non-adjustable). An oversized message closes the connection with 1009. It doesn't speak STOMP natively.

O17. [M] Why might a GCP HTTPS load balancer drop WebSockets after 30 seconds? Its backend service timeout (default 30 s) acts as the maximum WebSocket connection lifetime. Raise it (e.g., 3600 s+).

O18. [M] Your company still runs the community ingress-nginx controller for WebSockets. Any concern? Yes: it was retired in March 2026 (no more releases or security patches). Plan migration to a Gateway API implementation or another maintained controller, and re-verify WebSocket timeouts there.

O19. [M] What's the difference between @SendToUser with and without an authenticated user? With a user: sent to that user's sessions (all, or only the current one with broadcast = false). Without a user: Spring falls back to the current session id and forces broadcast = false. If neither exists, it throws MissingSessionUserException.

O20. [M] Can a STOMP client receive messages for destinations it never subscribed to? No. MESSAGE frames are only sent for active subscriptions (each carries the subscription id). A frame that arrives after a client-side unsubscribe reaches stompjs's onUnhandledMessage.

O21. [M] What's the role of ExecutorChannelInterceptor? Its beforeHandle/afterMessageHandled run on the thread that actually handles the message (the executor thread). That's the right place to set and clear ThreadLocals such as the security context, MDC logging keys or tenant context. preSend runs on the sending thread.

O22. [S] How do you version a real-time API used by mobile apps that update slowly? Use an envelope with type and version; make additive changes only; clients ignore unknown fields/types; the client sends its version in CONNECT headers so the server can adapt or reject; track client versions in metrics; keep old formats until usage is negligible. Document everything in AsyncAPI.

O23. [M] How would you test that a subscription is active before sending in an integration test? Use STOMP receipts (setAutoReceipt(true) + addReceiptTask) where supported, a round-trip ping through the same session, or Awaitility polling SimpUserRegistry#findSubscriptions. Avoid fixed sleeps.

O24. [M] Why is RabbitMQ's /queue/<name> risky for Spring user destinations? RabbitMQ creates /queue/ queues as durable, non-exclusive, non-auto-deleted by default, so per-session user queues (/queue/x-user{sessionId}) pile up after sessions end. Use /exchange/amq.direct/... (auto-delete) or expiry policies.

O25. [S] How do you do a zero-downtime broker (RabbitMQ) upgrade under the relay? Cluster RabbitMQ with quorum queues for durable data; upgrade node by node; front the cluster with a TCP LB or rotate addresses in a custom TcpClient; Spring's relay reconnects its system session every 5 s and publishes BrokerAvailabilityEvent; clients get disconnected sessions and reconnect with jitter; verify user-destination queues and bindings come back.

O26. [M] What does heartbeatToleranceMultiplier do in stompjs? The client treats the server as dead if no data arrives within multiplier × incoming heart-beat interval (default 2). Raise it for flaky mobile networks or throttled background tabs.

O27. [M] How do you avoid a reconnect storm with stompjs? Use reconnectTimeMode: ReconnectionTimeMode.EXPONENTIAL with maxReconnectDelay, and add your own randomness (stompjs doesn't add jitter), e.g., a random initial reconnectDelay per client or adjusting it in onWebSocketClose.

O28. [S] Where do you terminate TLS for WebSockets and why? Usually at the load balancer (offloads CPU/memory, central cert management); re-encrypt to the backend in zero-trust networks. Consider per-connection TLS memory cost if terminating on the app nodes.

O29. [M] What's the difference between SessionConnectEvent and SessionConnectedEvent? SessionConnectEvent fires when the client's CONNECT arrives (has the native CONNECT headers). SessionConnectedEvent fires after the broker has answered with CONNECTED; the original CONNECT is in its simpConnectMessage header.

O30. [M] Is @ServerEndpoint how Spring Boot does STOMP? No. @ServerEndpoint and @ClientEndpoint are Jakarta WebSocket (JSR 356) annotations for raw WebSocket endpoints. Spring STOMP uses @EnableWebSocketMessageBroker, @MessageMapping and friends (see Appendix A).

P. Common wrong answers you will find online (don't repeat them)

Wrong claim Correct answer
"Spring Boot sends STOMP heart-beats through a HeartbeatInterceptor." No such Spring STOMP class. Heart-beats are negotiated in CONNECT/CONNECTED and sent by the broker (simple broker with a TaskScheduler, or the external broker via the relay) and by clients.
"Spring Boot creates STOMP endpoints with @ServerEndpoint/@ClientEndpoint." Those are Jakarta WebSocket (JSR 356) annotations for raw WebSocket. STOMP uses @EnableWebSocketMessageBroker + @MessageMapping.
"Sec-WebSocket-Key secures the connection." It only proves the server speaks WebSocket. Security = TLS + Origin + auth.
"Masking encrypts client data." It prevents proxy cache poisoning; the key is sent in clear.
"CORS protects WebSocket endpoints." CORS doesn't apply; check Origin (CSWSH).
"The simple broker supports acks/redelivery." It doesn't support acks, receipts (except DISCONNECT) or transactions.
"WebSocket guarantees delivery because it's on TCP." Only while the connection lives; disconnects lose messages. You need ids, resync and persistence.
"Use HTTP/2 Server Push for real-time." It was for cache preloading and has been removed from Chrome.
"1006 is sent by the server when something fails." 1006 is never sent; it's reported locally when the connection dropped without a close frame.
"Sticky sessions are always required for WebSockets." Only for SockJS HTTP fallbacks (or app-specific reconnect state).
"Set maxPoolSize to scale Spring's inbound channel." With the default unbounded queue the pool never grows past corePoolSize.
"AbstractSecurityWebSocketMessageBrokerConfigurer is the way to secure messages." Deprecated in 5.8/6 and removed in Spring Security 7.

Chapter 13Interviews

Cheat Sheet & Glossary

13.1 One-page cheat sheet

WebSocket handshake

GET /ws HTTP/1.1                         HTTP/1.1 101 Switching Protocols
Host: h                                  Upgrade: websocket
Upgrade: websocket                       Connection: Upgrade
Connection: Upgrade                      Sec-WebSocket-Accept: base64(sha1(key+GUID))
Sec-WebSocket-Key: <16 random bytes b64> Sec-WebSocket-Protocol: v12.stomp
Sec-WebSocket-Version: 13
Origin: https://app
Sec-WebSocket-Protocol: v12.stomp, v11.stomp
GUID = 258EAFA5-E914-47DA-95CA-C5AB0DC85B11

Frame

FIN|RSV1-3|opcode(4) | MASK|len(7) | [len16|len64] | [mask key 4B] | payload
opcodes: 0 cont, 1 text, 2 binary, 8 close, 9 ping, A pong
client→server masked; control frames ≤125 B, unfragmented

Close codes

1000 normal · 1001 going away · 1002 protocol · 1003 unsupported · 1005* no status · 1006* abnormal · 1007 bad data · 1008 policy · 1009 too big · 1010 extension · 1011 internal · 1012 restart · 1013 try later · 1015* TLS · 3000–3999 libs · 4000–4999 app. (* never sent on the wire)

STOMP frames

COMMAND\nheader:value\n\nbody\0
Client: CONNECT/STOMP SEND SUBSCRIBE UNSUBSCRIBE ACK NACK BEGIN COMMIT ABORT DISCONNECT
Server: CONNECTED MESSAGE RECEIPT ERROR
Frame Required headers
CONNECT accept-version, host (opt: login, passcode, heart-beat)
CONNECTED version (opt: session, server, heart-beat)
SEND destination
SUBSCRIBE destination, id (opt: ack=auto/client/client-individual)
UNSUBSCRIBE id
ACK / NACK id (= MESSAGE's ack header)
BEGIN/COMMIT/ABORT transaction
MESSAGE destination, message-id, subscription
RECEIPT receipt-id
ERROR (message) → connection closes

Heart-beat: client→server = (cx==0‖sy==0) ? 0 : max(cx,sy); server→client = (sx==0‖cy==0) ? 0 : max(sx,cy). Escapes (1.2): \r \n \c \\; none in CONNECT/CONNECTED. First repeated header wins. Bodies only on SEND/MESSAGE/ERROR.

Spring essentials

java
@Configuration @EnableWebSocketMessageBroker
class Cfg implements WebSocketMessageBrokerConfigurer {
  public void registerStompEndpoints(StompEndpointRegistry r) {
    r.addEndpoint("/ws").setAllowedOriginPatterns("https://*.example.com"); // .withSockJS()
  }
  public void configureMessageBroker(MessageBrokerRegistry r) {
    r.setApplicationDestinationPrefixes("/app");
    r.enableSimpleBroker("/topic","/queue").setTaskScheduler(ts).setHeartbeatValue(new long[]{10000,10000});
    // r.enableStompBrokerRelay("/topic","/queue").setRelayHost(..).setRelayPort(61613)...
    r.setUserDestinationPrefix("/user");
    r.setPreservePublishOrder(true);
  }
  public void configureClientInboundChannel(ChannelRegistration reg) { reg.interceptors(jwtConnectInterceptor); }
  public void configureWebSocketTransport(WebSocketTransportRegistration t) {
    t.setMessageSizeLimit(64*1024).setSendBufferSizeLimit(512*1024).setSendTimeLimit(10_000).setTimeToFirstMessage(30_000);
  }
}
Annotation / API Purpose
@MessageMapping("/x") Handle client SEND to /app/x
@SubscribeMapping("/x") Reply once to SUBSCRIBE /app/x
@SendTo("/topic/y") Broadcast return value
@SendToUser("/queue/z") Return value to current user (broadcast=false → one session)
@MessageExceptionHandler Handle exceptions (also in @ControllerAdvice)
@DestinationVariable, @Header, @Headers, @Payload, Principal Method args
SimpMessagingTemplate.convertAndSend / convertAndSendToUser Push from anywhere
SimpUserRegistry Who's connected
Session*Event, BrokerAvailabilityEvent Lifecycle
StompSubProtocolErrorHandler Customise ERROR frames
WebSocketMessageBrokerStats Built-in stats log

stompjs essentials

ts
const c = new Client({ brokerURL: 'wss://h/ws', reconnectDelay: 5000, heartbeatIncoming: 10000, heartbeatOutgoing: 10000,
  beforeConnect: async () => { c.connectHeaders = { Authorization: `Bearer ${await token()}` }; },
  onConnect: () => { c.subscribe('/topic/x', m => use(JSON.parse(m.body))); },
  onStompError: f => console.error(f.headers.message) });
c.activate();
c.publish({ destination: '/app/y', body: JSON.stringify(d), headers: { 'content-type': 'application/json' } });
await c.deactivate();
// React Native: forceBinaryWSFrames: true, appendMissingNULLonIncoming: true

Nginx

nginx
map $http_upgrade $connection_upgrade { default upgrade; '' close; }
location /ws { proxy_pass http://backend; proxy_http_version 1.1;
  proxy_set_header Upgrade $http_upgrade; proxy_set_header Connection $connection_upgrade;
  proxy_read_timeout 3600s; proxy_send_timeout 3600s; proxy_buffering off; }

Top 10 rules

  1. wss:// + Origin allow-list + token in CONNECT.
  2. Authorise every SUBSCRIBE and SEND; deny by default; identity from Principal.
  3. Heart-beats 10–25 s, shorter than every idle timeout.
  4. Reconnect with backoff + jitter; re-subscribe in onConnect.
  5. DB is truth; events carry ids/seq; resync after reconnect.
  6. Publish after commit (or outbox); idempotent commands.
  7. Simple broker = single instance; cluster → relay or backplane.
  8. Bound buffers (send limits), size pools, conflate high-frequency data.
  9. Small messages; big data over HTTP.
  10. Measure: sessions, close codes, queues, latency; load + chaos test.

13.2 Glossary

Term Definition
ABORT STOMP frame cancelling a transaction
ACK / NACK STOMP frames acknowledging (or rejecting) a message for client/client-individual subscriptions
accept-version CONNECT header listing STOMP versions the client supports
AMQP Advanced Message Queuing Protocol; binary messaging protocol (0-9-1 RabbitMQ, 1.0 OASIS standard)
AsyncAPI Specification for documenting message-driven APIs (channels, messages, payloads)
At-least-once / at-most-once / exactly-once Delivery guarantees: ≥1, ≤1, exactly 1 (effectively via dedupe)
Backplane Pub/sub layer (Redis, Kafka) connecting multiple server instances so messages reach all their clients
Backpressure Mechanism to slow producers when consumers can't keep up
bfcache Browser back/forward cache; pages with open WebSockets may be evicted or have connections closed
Binary frame WebSocket opcode 0x2, arbitrary bytes
Broker Server that routes/stores messages between producers and consumers
Broker relay Spring's StompBrokerRelayMessageHandler forwarding STOMP to an external broker
brokerChannel Spring channel for messages from application code to the broker
bufferedAmount Browser WebSocket property: bytes queued but not yet sent
ChannelInterceptor Spring hook on message channels (preSend, postSend, afterSendCompletion)
clientInboundChannel / clientOutboundChannel Spring channels for messages from / to WebSocket clients
Close frame / handshake Opcode 0x8 frame with status code + reason; both sides exchange before TCP close
Comet Umbrella term for long polling/HTTP streaming push techniques
Conflation Sending only the latest value per key at intervals
CONNECT / CONNECTED STOMP frames to open a session / server confirmation
content-length STOMP header: body size in bytes; allows NULLs in body
Context takeover permessage-deflate option to reuse the compression dictionary across messages
Continuation frame Opcode 0x0, subsequent fragment of a message
Control frame Close, ping, pong (≤125 bytes, not fragmented)
CSWSH Cross-Site WebSocket Hijacking: attacker page opening an authenticated socket via victim's cookies
Destination STOMP address string (e.g., /topic/x), broker-defined semantics
DISCONNECT STOMP frame to close a session gracefully (with receipt)
Durable subscription/queue Survives disconnects/restarts and keeps messages
EOL End of line: LF, or CRLF in STOMP 1.2
ERROR STOMP server frame reporting an error; connection then closes
Exchange RabbitMQ routing entity (direct, topic, fanout, headers)
Extended CONNECT HTTP/2 (RFC 8441) / HTTP/3 (RFC 9220) mechanism to bootstrap WebSockets
Fan-out Delivering one message to many subscribers
FIN bit Marks the final frame of a message
Fragmentation Splitting a message into multiple frames
Full-duplex Both sides can send simultaneously
Half-open connection One side thinks the connection is alive while the other is gone
HandshakeInterceptor Spring hook around the HTTP upgrade
Heart-beat Periodic EOL (STOMP) or ping/pong (WebSocket) to prove liveness
host header STOMP CONNECT header naming a virtual host
Idempotency key Client-generated id letting the server ignore duplicate commands
Jitter Randomness added to retry delays to spread load
Long polling Server holds a request open until data is available
Masking XOR of client payloads with a random 4-byte key (anti cache-poisoning)
MESSAGE STOMP server frame delivering a message to a subscription
message-id Unique id of a MESSAGE frame
MQTT Lightweight pub/sub protocol for IoT, QoS 0/1/2
Native headers Spring's term for the raw STOMP headers inside a Message
Opcode 4-bit WebSocket frame type
Origin Header carrying the page's origin; checked to prevent CSWSH
Outbox pattern Writing events to a DB table in the same transaction for reliable publishing
permessage-deflate WebSocket compression extension (RFC 7692)
Ping / Pong WebSocket control frames for liveness
Prefetch Max unacked messages a broker sends to a consumer
Presence Tracking which users are online
Principal Authenticated user identity attached to a WebSocket session
Pub/Sub Publish/subscribe messaging pattern
Queue Point-to-point destination; each message to one consumer
readyState Browser WebSocket state: 0 CONNECTING, 1 OPEN, 2 CLOSING, 3 CLOSED
RECEIPT / receipt-id STOMP server confirmation of a frame that carried a receipt header
Resync Fetching missed data after reconnect or gap detection
RFC 6455 The WebSocket protocol specification
RSV bits Reserved frame bits used by extensions (RSV1 = compressed in permessage-deflate)
Sec-WebSocket-Accept Server handshake header proving WebSocket support
Sec-WebSocket-Key Client handshake nonce
Sec-WebSocket-Protocol Sub-protocol negotiation header
SEND STOMP frame to send a message to a destination
Sequence number (seq) Monotonic per-entity counter for ordering/gap detection
SimpMessagingTemplate Spring API to send messages to the broker
SimpUserRegistry Spring registry of connected users/sessions/subscriptions
Simple broker Spring's in-memory STOMP broker
SockJS WebSocket emulation protocol/library with HTTP fallbacks
SSE Server-Sent Events: one-way server push over HTTP
Sticky session LB routing a client's requests to the same backend
STOMP Simple/Streaming Text Oriented Messaging Protocol
Sub-protocol Application protocol negotiated over WebSocket (e.g., v12.stomp)
SUBSCRIBE / UNSUBSCRIBE STOMP frames to start/stop receiving messages from a destination
subscription header MESSAGE header carrying the matching SUBSCRIBE id
Text frame Opcode 0x1, UTF-8 payload
Thundering herd Many clients reconnecting/retrying simultaneously
TLS Transport Layer Security; makes wss://
Topic Pub/sub destination; each message to all subscribers
Transaction (STOMP) BEGIN/COMMIT/ABORT grouping SEND/ACK frames atomically
User destination Spring /user/... destinations resolved to per-session destinations
Virtual host Broker namespace (RabbitMQ vhost), selected by STOMP host header
WebSocketStompClient Spring's Java STOMP client
WebTransport HTTP/3-based API with streams and datagrams
wss:// WebSocket over TLS

Appendix AAppendices

Beyond Spring STOMP (Other Stacks, Protocols and Integrations)

Goal: fill the gaps around the main book: raw WebSocket APIs in Java (Jakarta, Spring, WebFlux), RSocket, Spring Session, a Kafka-backed real-time gateway, WebSocket over HTTP/2 in practice, Node.js, Socket.IO, SignalR, Go, GraphQL subscriptions, CRDT sync (Yjs) and MQTT over WebSocket. You don't need to master all of these, but interviewers love "how does X compare to STOMP?" and real companies mix stacks.

A.1 ELI5

STOMP is one language you can speak on the WebSocket telephone line. Other teams use other languages on the same line (Socket.IO, SignalR, GraphQL subscriptions, MQTT, Yjs), or talk on the line with no shared language at all (raw WebSocket). Knowing the neighbours helps you choose well and explain your choice.

A.2 Jakarta WebSocket (JSR 356) @ServerEndpoint

The standard Java API for raw WebSocket, implemented by Tomcat, Jetty, Undertow and others. No STOMP, no broker: you get sessions and messages.

java
@ServerEndpoint(value = "/raw/echo",
                decoders = ChatDecoder.class, encoders = ChatEncoder.class)
public class EchoEndpoint {

    private static final Set<Session> SESSIONS = ConcurrentHashMap.newKeySet();

    @OnOpen
    public void onOpen(Session session, EndpointConfig config) {
        session.setMaxIdleTimeout(0);                         // rely on app heart-beats instead
        session.setMaxTextMessageBufferSize(64 * 1024);
        SESSIONS.add(session);
    }

    @OnMessage
    public void onMessage(Session session, ChatMessage msg) {   // decoded by ChatDecoder
        for (Session s : SESSIONS) {
            if (s.isOpen()) s.getAsyncRemote().sendObject(msg); // async send; see thread-safety note
        }
    }

    @OnClose
    public void onClose(Session session, CloseReason reason) { SESSIONS.remove(session); }

    @OnError
    public void onError(Session session, Throwable t) { SESSIONS.remove(session); }
}

In Spring Boot, register annotated endpoints with a ServerEndpointExporter bean (embedded container):

java
@Bean
public ServerEndpointExporter serverEndpointExporter() { return new ServerEndpointExporter(); }

Key facts:

  • @ServerEndpoint classes are instantiated per connection by the container by default, so they aren't Spring singletons. Injecting Spring beans needs SpringConfigurator or a custom ServerEndpointConfig.Configurator.
  • Session.getBasicRemote() is blocking; getAsyncRemote() is non-blocking. Sending concurrently to the same session from multiple threads can throw IllegalStateException (Tomcat: TEXT_PARTIAL_WRITING). Serialise sends per session.
  • Encoder/Decoder classes convert between objects and text/binary.
  • @ClientEndpoint + ContainerProvider.getWebSocketContainer().connectToServer(...) for Java clients.
  • Interview point: @ServerEndpoint is not how Spring STOMP works. Spring STOMP uses @EnableWebSocketMessageBroker + @MessageMapping.

A.3 Spring's raw WebSocketHandler API (servlet stack)

When you want Spring integration (beans, security, interceptors) without STOMP:

java
@Configuration
@EnableWebSocket
public class RawConfig implements WebSocketConfigurer {
    private final GameSocketHandler handler;
    public RawConfig(GameSocketHandler handler) { this.handler = handler; }

    @Override
    public void registerWebSocketHandlers(WebSocketHandlerRegistry registry) {
        registry.addHandler(handler, "/game")
                .addInterceptors(new HttpSessionHandshakeInterceptor())
                .setAllowedOriginPatterns("https://*.example.com");
    }
}

@Component
public class GameSocketHandler extends TextWebSocketHandler {
    private final Map<String, WebSocketSession> sessions = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();

    @Override
    public void afterConnectionEstablished(WebSocketSession raw) {
        // Make sends thread-safe + bounded: 5 s send limit, 256 KB buffer, drop or terminate on overflow
        WebSocketSession s = new ConcurrentWebSocketSessionDecorator(raw, 5_000, 256 * 1024,
                ConcurrentWebSocketSessionDecorator.OverflowStrategy.DROP);
        sessions.put(raw.getId(), s);
    }

    @Override
    protected void handleTextMessage(WebSocketSession session, TextMessage message) throws Exception {
        TextMessage out = new TextMessage("echo:" + message.getPayload());
        for (WebSocketSession s : sessions.values()) if (s.isOpen()) s.sendMessage(out);
    }

    @Override
    public void afterConnectionClosed(WebSocketSession session, CloseStatus status) {
        sessions.remove(session.getId());
    }

    @Override
    public boolean supportsPartialMessages() { return false; }   // true → receive fragments (handle buffering yourself)
}

Notes:

  • BinaryWebSocketHandler for binary protocols (protobuf, game state).
  • PerConnectionWebSocketHandler creates a handler instance per connection (when you want per-connection state in fields).
  • OverflowStrategy.DROP drops the oldest buffered messages instead of closing the session when the buffer limit is exceeded; good for "latest state" streams. Exceeding the send time limit still terminates the session.
  • Heart-beats: send PingMessages from a scheduler and track PongMessages in handlePongMessage.
  • You get no destinations, subscriptions or user routing, so you build them. This is the main reason teams pick STOMP.

A.4 Spring WebFlux reactive WebSocket

WebFlux (Netty or servlet containers in reactive mode) has its own org.springframework.web.reactive.socket.WebSocketHandler. No STOMP support here.

java
@Configuration
public class ReactiveWsConfig {
    @Bean
    public HandlerMapping wsMapping(PriceHandler handler) {
        return new SimpleUrlHandlerMapping(Map.of("/prices", handler), -1);   // order before annotated controllers
    }
    // Needed only if you are NOT using the WebFlux config (@EnableWebFlux / Boot auto-config):
    // @Bean WebSocketHandlerAdapter handlerAdapter() { return new WebSocketHandlerAdapter(); }
}

@Component
public class PriceHandler implements WebSocketHandler {
    private final Sinks.Many<String> prices = Sinks.many().multicast().directBestEffort(); // drops for slow subscribers

    public void publish(String json) { prices.tryEmitNext(json); }

    @Override
    public Mono<Void> handle(WebSocketSession session) {
        Mono<Void> input = session.receive()                           // Flux<WebSocketMessage>
                .map(WebSocketMessage::getPayloadAsText)
                .doOnNext(cmd -> { /* handle client commands */ })
                .then();
        Mono<Void> output = session.send(prices.asFlux()
                .onBackpressureLatest()                                 // conflation: keep only latest when slow
                .map(session::textMessage));
        return Mono.zip(input, output).then();                         // completes when either side ends
    }
}
  • session.receive() returns a Flux<WebSocketMessage>; session.send(Publisher) returns Mono<Void>. The handler's returned Mono<Void> signals when the session is done.
  • Reactive streams give real backpressure semantics (onBackpressureLatest, onBackpressureBuffer(n), limitRate).
  • Payload buffers (DataBuffer) are released by the framework when you map them in the pipeline; if you hold raw messages beyond the pipeline, call retain()/release correctly.
  • Client: ReactorNettyWebSocketClient().execute(uri, session -> ...).
  • When to choose: fully reactive stack, high connection counts on Netty, streaming pipelines. If you want STOMP semantics, stay on Spring MVC or use RSocket.

A.5 RSocket (application protocol with reactive semantics)

RSocket is a binary application protocol with four interaction models: request-response, fire-and-forget, request-stream, request-channel (bidirectional streams), plus flow control (request(n)), resumption and metadata routing. It can run over TCP or WebSocket.

Spring Boot:

properties
spring.rsocket.server.mapping-path=/rsocket
spring.rsocket.server.transport=websocket
java
@Controller
public class PriceRSocketController {
    @MessageMapping("prices.{symbol}")                 // routing metadata, same annotation name as STOMP!
    public Flux<Price> stream(@DestinationVariable String symbol) { return priceService.stream(symbol); }

    @MessageMapping("orders.place")
    public Mono<OrderAck> place(OrderRequest req) { return orderService.place(req); }
}

Browser client: rsocket-js. Comparison with STOMP: RSocket has built-in request/response, backpressure and resumption, but it's binary, less widely adopted, has fewer brokers/tools, and its browser libraries are less mature. STOMP is simpler and ubiquitous with brokers. Use RSocket for reactive service-to-service or advanced streaming needs.

A.6 Spring Session integration (keep HTTP sessions alive)

Problem: with cookie/HTTP-session authentication, a user may be active only over WebSocket for an hour. The HTTP session isn't touched, so it expires, and the next REST call fails, or worse, the WebSocket keeps working for a logged-out user.

Spring Session provides AbstractSessionWebSocketMessageBrokerConfigurer, which:

  • keeps the HTTP Session alive on incoming WebSocket messages, and
  • closes WebSocket sessions when the HTTP session is terminated (logout/expiry).
java
@Configuration
@EnableScheduling
@EnableWebSocketMessageBroker
public class WebSocketConfig<S extends Session> extends AbstractSessionWebSocketMessageBrokerConfigurer<S> {

    @Override
    protected void configureStompEndpoints(StompEndpointRegistry registry) {   // note: not registerStompEndpoints
        registry.addEndpoint("/ws").setAllowedOriginPatterns("https://app.example.com");
    }

    @Override
    public void configureMessageBroker(MessageBrokerRegistry registry) {
        registry.enableSimpleBroker("/queue/", "/topic/");
        registry.setApplicationDestinationPrefixes("/app");
    }
}

Combine with a Spring Session store (spring-session-data-redis / JDBC) for clustering. Not needed if you use stateless JWTs in CONNECT.

A.7 A Kafka-backed real-time gateway (complete pattern)

Large companies often keep domain services ignorant of WebSockets: they publish events to Kafka, and a real-time gateway pushes them to connected clients.

 order-service ──► Kafka topic "user-notifications" (key = userId) ──► realtime-gateway pods ──► STOMP clients
 todo-service  ──► Kafka topic "list-events" (key = listId)        ──►        "

A.7.1 Broadcast-style consumption (simple, every pod gets everything)

Each gateway pod uses a unique consumer group so it receives all events, then delivers to its local sessions.

java
@Component
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class KafkaToStompBridge {
    private final SimpMessagingTemplate messaging;
    private final ObjectMapper json;

    @KafkaListener(topics = "list-events",
                   groupId = "realtime-gateway-#{T(java.util.UUID).randomUUID().toString()}") // unique group per pod
    public void onListEvent(ConsumerRecord<String, String> rec) throws IOException {
        JsonNode event = json.readTree(rec.value());
        String listId = rec.key();
        messaging.convertAndSend("/topic/todo.list." + listId, event);     // simple broker: only local subscribers get it
    }

    @KafkaListener(topics = "user-notifications",
                   groupId = "realtime-gateway-#{T(java.util.UUID).randomUUID().toString()}")
    public void onNotification(ConsumerRecord<String, String> rec) throws IOException {
        messaging.convertAndSendToUser(rec.key(), "/queue/notifications", json.readTree(rec.value()));
        // no-op on pods where this user has no session
    }
}
  • Set auto.offset.reset=latest for broadcast groups (a new pod doesn't need history; clients resync via REST).
  • Random group ids leave stale groups behind in Kafka. Use a stable per-pod id (e.g., the StatefulSet pod name) instead of a random UUID in production.
  • Cost: every pod processes every event. Fine up to a few thousand events/s.

A.7.2 Targeted routing (scales further)

  1. On connect, the gateway records userId → podId in Redis (TTL refreshed by heart-beats/events, removed on disconnect).
  2. A router service consumes user-notifications, looks up the pod, and forwards to that pod's own channel (a Redis channel pod:{podId} or a per-pod Kafka partition).
  3. If the user is offline → push notification / inbox only. This avoids every pod processing every message, but adds a lookup and a moving part.

A.7.3 Ordering and reliability

  • Key Kafka records by entity id (listId, conversationId) → per-entity ordering within a partition.
  • Include seq in the event so clients can detect gaps; resync via REST on reconnect.
  • The gateway is not a durable store: if a pod restarts, its clients reconnect and resync.

A.8 WebSocket over HTTP/2 and HTTP/3 in practice

  • Browsers can bootstrap WebSockets over HTTP/2 (RFC 8441) when the server advertises SETTINGS_ENABLE_CONNECT_PROTOCOL. If it doesn't, they open a separate HTTP/1.1 connection with the classic Upgrade. Your code doesn't change; new WebSocket(url) is the same.
  • Servers/proxies vary: some implement RFC 8441 (e.g., Jetty and Envoy support it), many deployments don't, and often the edge speaks HTTP/2 to browsers while forwarding WebSockets to the app over HTTP/1.1 Upgrade. Check each hop's documentation before relying on it.
  • Benefits: many WebSockets share one TCP+TLS connection (fewer handshakes, fewer sockets on mobile). Drawbacks: TCP head-of-line blocking affects all streams on that connection; per-stream flow control interacts with WebSocket backpressure; less mature tooling.
  • HTTP/3 (RFC 9220) removes TCP head-of-line blocking via QUIC; support is still emerging. WebTransport is the HTTP/3-native alternative with unreliable datagrams.
  • Practical advice: don't architect around HTTP/2 WebSockets; treat them as a transparent optimisation where available.

A.9 Node.js

A.9.1 Raw WebSocket server

See Chapter 3 §3.9 (ws library with ping/pong liveness). For STOMP semantics in Node, teams usually either:

  • put RabbitMQ Web STOMP (or ActiveMQ) behind their auth layer, or
  • use Socket.IO / their own JSON protocol on ws.

A.9.2 STOMP client in Node

js
// Node 22+ has a global WebSocket. On older Node, polyfill it with the 'ws' package:
// import { WebSocket } from 'ws'; Object.assign(globalThis, { WebSocket });
import { Client } from '@stomp/stompjs';

const client = new Client({
  brokerURL: 'ws://localhost:8080/ws',
  connectHeaders: { Authorization: `Bearer ${process.env.TOKEN}` },
  onConnect: () => {
    client.subscribe('/topic/todo.list.L1', (m) => console.log('event', m.body));
    client.publish({ destination: '/app/todo.upsert', body: JSON.stringify({ listId: 'L1', title: 'from node' }),
                     headers: { 'content-type': 'application/json' } });
  },
});
client.activate();

For STOMP over TCP (e.g., to RabbitMQ 61613) Node has libraries such as stompit; check maintenance status before adopting.

A.10 Socket.IO in depth (and how it differs from STOMP)

Architecture: Socket.IO (server: Node; clients: JS, Swift, Java, Python, etc.) sits on Engine.IO, which handles transports (HTTP long-polling first, then upgrade to WebSocket by default; or WebSocket-only if configured), heart-beats and reconnection.

Concept Socket.IO STOMP equivalent
Event socket.emit('chat', data) / socket.on('chat', fn) SEND to destination / MESSAGE on subscription
Rooms socket.join('room-1'), io.to('room-1').emit(...) (server-side membership) Client SUBSCRIBE to /topic/room-1
Namespaces io.of('/admin') (multiplex logical channels) Destination prefixes
Acknowledgements socket.emit('save', data, (ack) => ...) callback (request/response built in) Receipts / reply queues
Broadcast socket.broadcast.emit Topic publish
Middleware io.use((socket, next) => ...) (auth) Channel interceptors
Scaling Adapters (Redis, Redis Streams, Postgres, cluster adapter) Broker relay / backplane
Sticky sessions Required when HTTP long-polling is enabled (multiple requests per session) Only for SockJS fallbacks
Recovery Connection state recovery (v4.6+) can restore rooms and missed packets for brief disconnects Your resync design

Key differences to say in interviews:

  • Socket.IO's room membership is server-controlled (join), so authorisation lives in your join logic. STOMP subscriptions are client-requested, so you must authorise SUBSCRIBE.
  • Socket.IO has its own wire protocol, so you can't connect with a plain WebSocket or STOMP client.
  • Socket.IO is idiomatic in Node ecosystems; STOMP is idiomatic with Spring/RabbitMQ.

A.11 ASP.NET Core SignalR

  • Hubs: server classes whose methods clients invoke (connection.invoke("SendMessage", user, text)), and the server calls client methods (Clients.Group("room").SendAsync("ReceiveMessage", ...)).
  • Transports negotiated: WebSockets → Server-Sent Events → Long Polling. A negotiate HTTP request precedes the connection unless you configure WebSockets-only with skipNegotiation.
  • Protocols: JSON or MessagePack hub protocols.
  • Groups (server-managed membership), users (by IUserIdProvider), connections.
  • Scale-out: Redis backplane or Azure SignalR Service (managed connections). Sticky sessions are required for multi-server setups unless all clients use WebSockets with negotiation skipped, or you use Azure SignalR Service.
  • Comparison: SignalR ≈ Socket.IO for .NET: RPC-style hub calls, server-managed groups. STOMP ≈ messaging with destinations. Java/Spring shops pick STOMP; .NET shops pick SignalR.

A.12 Go

  • Libraries: github.com/gorilla/websocket (classic, widely used; the project was archived in 2022 and later revived under new maintainers) and github.com/coder/websocket (formerly nhooyr.io/websocket, context-aware, minimal).
  • Concurrency rule (gorilla): at most one concurrent writer and one concurrent reader per connection. Typical pattern: one goroutine readPump, one goroutine writePump fed by a buffered channel; the write pump also sends pings on a ticker; the read pump sets a read deadline extended on each pong.
  • STOMP in Go: github.com/go-stomp/stomp (client and a simple server, over TCP; wrap a WebSocket as net.Conn if needed).
go
// writePump sketch (gorilla)
ticker := time.NewTicker(pingPeriod)
for {
    select {
    case msg, ok := <-c.send:
        c.conn.SetWriteDeadline(time.Now().Add(writeWait))
        if !ok { c.conn.WriteMessage(websocket.CloseMessage, []byte{}); return }
        if err := c.conn.WriteMessage(websocket.TextMessage, msg); err != nil { return }
    case <-ticker.C:
        c.conn.SetWriteDeadline(time.Now().Add(writeWait))
        if err := c.conn.WriteMessage(websocket.PingMessage, nil); err != nil { return }
    }
}

A.13 GraphQL subscriptions over WebSocket

  • Modern protocol: graphql-transport-ws (implemented by the graphql-ws library). Message types: connection_init (with auth payload), connection_ack, subscribe, next, error, complete, ping, pong.
  • Legacy protocol: subscriptions-transport-ws (Apollo, deprecated), which confusingly uses the sub-protocol name graphql-ws. Client and server must use the same protocol; this mismatch is a classic bug.
  • Auth goes in connection_init's payload (the same idea as STOMP CONNECT headers).
  • Spring for GraphQL supports subscriptions over WebSocket (spring.graphql.websocket.path=/graphql) with @SubscriptionMapping returning a Flux.
  • Comparison: GraphQL subscriptions give typed, client-selected fields per event; STOMP gives free-form messages. Scaling needs the same backplane thinking.

A.14 CRDT-based sync (collaborative editing): Yjs

  • Yjs is a CRDT library; documents converge regardless of update order. y-websocket syncs them over WebSocket with a small binary protocol: sync step 1 (state vector), sync step 2 (missing updates), then incremental updates; plus awareness messages (cursors, presence).
  • Servers: y-websocket server, Hocuspocus (auth, persistence hooks, Redis scaling), y-redis.
  • Persistence: store merged document state (e.g., in a DB/S3) and/or update logs.
  • Why not STOMP? You can carry Yjs updates as binary STOMP bodies on a /topic/doc.{id}, but you lose the ready-made sync handshake, awareness and persistence integrations. Most teams use the Yjs ecosystem directly and keep STOMP for other real-time features.
  • OT vs CRDT: OT (Google Docs style) transforms operations via a central server; CRDTs merge without central coordination (better offline, more metadata).

A.15 MQTT over WebSocket

  • MQTT brokers (Mosquitto, EMQX, HiveMQ, RabbitMQ's Web MQTT plugin) expose WebSocket listeners; browser clients like mqtt.js connect with sub-protocol mqtt.
  • Topic wildcards + (one level) and # (multi-level), separator /; QoS 0/1/2; retained messages; last will; session persistence.
  • Choose MQTT when IoT devices and browsers share the same pub/sub fabric. Choose STOMP when you're in the Spring/RabbitMQ web-app world.

A.16 The big comparison table

STOMP/WS Raw WS Socket.IO SignalR GraphQL subs RSocket MQTT/WS Yjs
Style Messaging (destinations) Bytes/messages Events + rooms RPC hubs + groups Typed queries Reactive streams IoT pub/sub CRDT sync
Ecosystem Spring, RabbitMQ, ActiveMQ Any Node .NET Any GraphQL Spring, Java IoT brokers Collab editors
Who controls membership Client subscribes (authorise!) You Server (join) Server (groups) Client query (authorise!) Client request Client subscribe (ACLs) Doc-level auth
Request/response Convention You Built-in acks Built-in invoke Queries/mutations Built-in Convention n/a
Backpressure Limits/prefetch You Limited Limited Limited Built-in QoS/inflight n/a
Fallback transports SockJS No Long-polling SSE, long-polling No No No No
Browser client maturity High Native High High High Medium High High

A.17 Summary

  • Raw WebSocket APIs (Jakarta, Spring WebSocketHandler, WebFlux) give full control with no messaging semantics.
  • RSocket adds reactive streams, backpressure and resumption; Spring Session keeps cookie sessions aligned with sockets.
  • A Kafka-backed gateway decouples domain teams from WebSockets.
  • Socket.IO and SignalR are framework-specific alternatives with server-managed rooms/groups; GraphQL subscriptions, MQTT and Yjs serve specialised needs.
  • The same production rules apply everywhere: auth on connect, authorise membership, heart-beats, backoff with jitter, resync, backplane for scale.

Runnable Sample: Real-Time Shared To-Do Lists (Spring Boot + STOMP + TypeScript/React Native)

A small but production-shaped project that puts the book's patterns into working, tested code. Companion to The WebSocket + STOMP Book.

examples/stomp-todo/
├── server/   Spring Boot 4.1 (Spring Framework 7, Spring Security 7, Java 21): STOMP over WebSocket
├── client/   TypeScript client for web / Node / React Native + React hook + runnable demos
├── docker-compose.yml + rabbitmq/   RabbitMQ with the STOMP plugin (for relay mode)
└── README.md (this file)

What it demonstrates (and where the book explains it)

Feature Where in the code Book chapter
STOMP endpoint, /app + /topic + /queue + /user prefixes server/.../ws/WebSocketConfig.java 5
Simple broker with heart-beats (TaskScheduler), SpEL selector disabled WebSocketConfig#configureMessageBroker 4, 5, 8
Broker relay switch (app.broker.mode=relay) incl. multi-instance user destinations same 7

React / React Native hook client/src/useTodoList.ts 6

Prerequisites

  • Java 21+, Maven 3.9+ (server)
  • Node.js 22+ (client demos use the built-in WebSocket and fetch)
  • Docker (only for relay mode)

Run it

bash
# 1) server
cd examples/stomp-todo/server
mvn spring-boot:run            # http://localhost:8080, STOMP endpoint ws://localhost:8080/ws

# 2) client demo (another terminal)
cd examples/stomp-todo/client
npm install
npm run demo

Demo users: anna and bob share list L1; carol owns L2 only. Tokens come from a dev-only endpoint (POST /api/dev-token {"user":"anna"}), which you disable with app.dev-tokens=false.

Output from a real run of npm run demo:

bob status: live
bob sees L1 @seq 0: (empty)

--- anna adds two items
bob sees L1 @seq 1: [ ] Buy milk (v1)
bob sees L1 @seq 2: [ ] Buy milk (v1) | [ ] Walk the dog (v1)

--- anna completes "Buy milk"
bob sees L1 @seq 3: [x] Buy milk (v2) | [ ] Walk the dog (v1)

--- anna retries the same command twice (same clientRequestId)
bob sees L1 @seq 4: [x] Buy milk (v2) | [ ] Walk the dog (v1) | [ ] Only once please (v1)
first ack duplicate=false, second ack duplicate=true

--- anna edits with a stale version → CONFLICT (connection stays open)
anna got: CONFLICT

--- carol (not a member) tries to read L1 over REST
carol GET /api/lists/L1 → HTTP 403

Resilience demo (reconnect, re-subscribe, resync)

bash
npm run resilience      # then restart the server when it says so

In a real run, after the restart bob went reconnecting → (backoff 2.2 s, then 4.4 s) → live, re-subscribed, noticed the server's sequence went backwards (in-memory state lost), reloaded the snapshot, and then received the new item live.

Tests

bash
cd server && mvn test     # 11 end-to-end tests: auth, forged tokens, snapshot, broadcast order, idempotency,
                          # conflicts, validation, forbidden subscriptions, spoofed topic publishes, REST resync
cd ../client && npm run typecheck

Use it from this React Native app

tsx
// App.tsx (sketch)
import { TodoRealtimeClient } from './examples/stomp-todo/client/src/TodoRealtimeClient';
import { useTodoList } from './examples/stomp-todo/client/src/useTodoList';

const client = new TodoRealtimeClient({
  baseUrl: 'http://10.0.2.2:8080',      // Android emulator → your machine. iOS simulator: http://localhost:8080
  getToken: () => auth.getFreshToken(),  // your auth
  reactNative: true,                     // stompjs NULL-byte workarounds (forceBinaryWSFrames, appendMissingNULLonIncoming)
});
client.start();                          // after login; client.stop() on logout

function ListScreen() {
  const { state, status, add, toggle } = useTodoList(client, 'L1');
  // render state.items, show `status` ("reconnecting", ...) in the UI
}

Add @stomp/stompjs to the app's dependencies. Android 9+ and iOS block cleartext ws:// by default; use wss:// or a dev-only exception. Reconnect on AppState active; use push notifications for background delivery (Chapter 6).

Relay mode (RabbitMQ)

bash
cd examples/stomp-todo
docker compose up -d                       # RabbitMQ + STOMP plugin on 61613, UI on 15672
cd server
mvn spring-boot:run -Dspring-boot.run.arguments="--app.broker.mode=relay --app.broker.relay.client-login=stomp --app.broker.relay.client-passcode=stomp-dev-password --app.broker.relay.system-login=stomp --app.broker.relay.system-passcode=stomp-dev-password"

Honesty note: the relay configuration compiles and follows Spring's documentation, but it was not executed while writing this sample (no Docker daemon was available). Simple-broker mode was fully tested. Also note: with RabbitMQ, Spring's /user/queue/... destinations become per-session queues that RabbitMQ makes durable and not auto-deleted by default. For production, switch to auto-delete destinations (e.g., /user/exchange/amq.direct/acks) or add an expiry policy (Chapter 7 §7.5.3).

Things deliberately left simple (do these in production)

  • Storage is in memory. Restarting loses data, and multiple server instances don't share it. Use a database; publish events after commit or through an outbox (Chapter 9).
  • HS256 dev tokens. Validate tokens from your identity provider (issuer URI / JWKS) instead, and remove the dev-token endpoint.
  • Presence is per instance. Use Redis with TTLs in a cluster.
  • Allowed origins are localhost only (app.allowed-origin-patterns).
  • Add metrics (session gauges, executor queues, close codes), alerts and load tests (Chapter 9).

Two production gotchas this sample uncovered (now in the book)

  1. setPreserveReceiveOrder(true) swallows inbound interceptor exceptions (Spring Framework 7.0.x, checked in OrderedMessageChannelDecorator): a rejected CONNECT or SUBSCRIBE is only logged. The client gets no ERROR frame and the connection stays open, so the client just hangs. This sample therefore doesn't enable it. See Chapter 10, bug C10.
  2. stompjs beforeConnect must never throw: stompjs 7.x awaits it without a try/catch, so a failing token fetch (e.g., auth server down) becomes an unhandled rejection and silently stops the reconnect loop. Also, with Node's WebSocket a connection attempt can get stuck; discardWebsocketOnCommFailure: true lets the reconnect continue. See Chapter 10, bugs G11 and G12.
Appendix CAppendices

Verification Notes and Sources

This appendix records how the facts in this book were checked, so you know what to trust and what to re-check for your own versions. Verification pass done in October 2026.

How it was checked

The rendered documentation sites (docs.spring.io, stomp.github.io, rabbitmq.com) weren't reachable from the environment used to write this book, so the facts were checked against primary sources one level deeper:

  • the source code of Spring Framework (main branch, version 7.1.0-SNAPSHOT at the time), Spring Security, Spring Boot and Apache Tomcat,
  • the AsciiDoc sources of the Spring Framework and Spring Security reference documentation (the same text that's published on docs.spring.io),
  • the RabbitMQ documentation sources (rabbitmq-website repository),
  • the published @stomp/stompjs 7.5.0 npm package (its compiled source),
  • web searches for cloud provider limits and industry announcements.

Facts confirmed against source code / docs sources

Fact (chapter) Confirmed in
Simple broker heart-beats need a TaskScheduler; default becomes 10000,10000 once set; @Lazy hint to avoid cycles (Ch 5) SimpleBrokerMessageHandler javadoc + reference doc "handle-simple-broker"
Simple broker read check = max(client cx, server sy) × 3, write = max(client cy, server sx) (Ch 4) SimpleBrokerMessageHandler.SessionInfo, HEARTBEAT_MULTIPLIER = 3
Simple broker supports "only a subset of STOMP commands (no acks, receipts, …)" (Ch 4/7) Reference doc "handle-broker-relay"
Spring sends a RECEIPT for a DISCONNECT that carries a receipt header (Ch 4) StompSubProtocolHandler#getDisconnectReceipt
Relay defaults: logins guest, system heart-beats 10000/10000, reconnect every 5 s, "Message broker not active" error, read check ×3 (Ch 7) StompBrokerRelayMessageHandler
Relay needs reactor-netty + netty-all (Ch 5/7) Reference doc "handle-broker-relay"
Transport defaults: 64 KB message, 512 KB send buffer, 10 s send time, 60 s time to first message (Ch 5) WebSocketTransportRegistration javadoc
Slow session closed with CloseStatus.SESSION_NOT_RELIABLE = 4500; overflow strategies TERMINATE (default) / DROP (Ch 2/9) CloseStatus, ConcurrentWebSocketSessionDecorator
Sessions closed with GOING_AWAY (1001) on shutdown (Ch 7) SubProtocolWebSocketHandler#stop
Channel thread pools default to 2 × processors; maxPoolSize ineffective with unbounded queue (Ch 9) Reference doc "configuration-performance"
ChannelRegistration.executor(Executor) since 6.2 (virtual threads) (Ch 9) ChannelRegistration
setPreservePublishOrder / setPreserveReceiveOrder semantics (Ch 5/9) Reference doc "ordered-messages"
@Order(Ordered.HIGHEST_PRECEDENCE + 99) for the CONNECT auth interceptor (Ch 8) Reference doc "authentication-token-based"
SessionDisconnectEvent may be published more than once; be idempotent (Ch 5) Reference doc "application-context-events"
RabbitMQ auto-delete user queues via /exchange/amq.direct/...; userDestinationBroadcast (Ch 7) Reference doc "user-destination"
@SendToUser without a user falls back to the session id with broadcast=false; else MissingSessionUserException (Ch 10/12) SendToMethodReturnValueHandler
MappingJackson2MessageConverter deprecated for removal in 7.0; JacksonJsonMessageConverter since 7.0 (Ch 5/6) Source of both classes
WebSocketStompClient: heart-beat 0,0 until setTaskScheduler, then 10000,10000; connectAsync; receipt timeout 15 s; consider disabling heart-beats when simulating many clients (Ch 6/9) WebSocketStompClient + reference doc "client"
@EnableWebSocketSecurity requires CSRF on CONNECT; CSRF "not configurable" with the annotation; manual configuration alternative (Ch 8) Spring Security reference doc "websocket"
AbstractSecurityWebSocketMessageBrokerConfigurer / MessageSecurityMetadataSourceRegistry removed in Spring Security 7 (Ch 8/11) Spring Security reference doc "websocket"
Spring Security secures only the inbound channel (Ch 8) Spring Security reference doc
Allowed-origins default = same origin since Spring 4.1.5 (Ch 5/8) Spring Security reference doc
Tomcat WebSocket text/binary buffers default 8 KB (Ch 2/5) Tomcat Constants.DEFAULT_BUFFER_SIZE, WsWebSocketContainer
Spring Boot server.tomcat.max-connections default 8192, accept count 100 (Ch 10) Spring Boot TomcatServerProperties
stompjs 7.5.0 defaults: reconnectDelay 5000, LINEAR/EXPONENTIAL modes (no jitter), maxReconnectDelay 15 min, heart-beats 10000/10000, heartbeatToleranceMultiplier 2, heartbeatStrategy Interval/Worker, connectionTimeout 0, maxWebSocketChunkSize 8 KB, RN workarounds, TypeError: There is no underlying STOMP connection (Ch 6) @stomp/stompjs 7.5.0 package source
RabbitMQ: STOMP port 61613; Web STOMP 15674 /ws; /queue/ queues durable, non-exclusive, non-auto-delete by default; /topic subscribe queues auto-delete non-durable; durable subscriptions via durable:true + auto-delete:false (Ch 7) RabbitMQ docs sources (stomp.md, web-stomp.md)
WebSocket handshake Accept example s3pPLMBiTxaQ9kYGzzhZRbK+xOo= and masking example bytes (Ch 2) Recomputed with OpenSSL/Python

Behaviour verified by running the Appendix B sample (Spring Boot 4.1.1 / Spring Framework 7.0.9 / Spring Security 7, stompjs 7.5.0, Node 22)

Behaviour How it was observed
JWT CONNECT auth, deny-by-default message rules, SUBSCRIBE membership checks, spoofed /topic SEND rejected 11 passing integration tests with WebSocketStompClient
ERROR frame is followed by a server-side close (code 1002) StompSubProtocolHandler#sendToClient / sendErrorMessage + tests observing disconnects
setPreserveReceiveOrder(true) → interceptor exceptions only logged, no ERROR, connection stays open Failing tests, then OrderedMessageChannelDecorator source (7.0.9 and main)
stompjs beforeConnect rejection stops reconnecting and is unhandled Server-restart test + stompjs _connect() source
Connection attempt stuck in CONNECTING after timeout is recovered by discardWebsocketOnCommFailure Server-restart test with debug logs
Exponential reconnect backoff doubles (2.2 s → 4.4 s), re-subscription, snapshot reload on sequence regression Server-restart test
Idempotent retries, ordered per-list seq, CONFLICT as MESSAGE with connection kept open, REST 403 for non-members npm run demo against the live server

Facts confirmed by web search (secondary sources; re-check provider docs for your account/region)

Fact Notes
ingress-nginx retired March 2026 (announced Nov 2025; reaffirmed Jan 2026) Kubernetes blog posts
AWS ALB idle timeout default 60 s, range 1–4000 s AWS docs (translated pages), re:Post
AWS API Gateway WebSocket: 2 h max duration, 10 min idle, 128 KB message, 32 KB frame; 1009 on oversize AWS quota table
GCP HTTPS LB backend service timeout (default 30 s) acts as WebSocket max lifetime Community + docs quotes
Cloudflare closes WebSockets idle for ~100 s (Enterprise can adjust) Community/support reports

Not independently re-verified in this pass

  • The exact wording of the STOMP 1.2 specification (the stomp.github.io source wasn't reachable). Key protocol rules (heart-beat formula, ack modes, header escaping and its CONNECT/CONNECTED exemption) are consistent with the Spring and stompjs implementations above and with spec quotations found in search results.
  • RFC 6455 text (well established; examples recomputed).
  • Browser-specific behaviour (bfcache, SharedWorker support, background throttling) changes often. Test your target browsers.
